The global Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for Fourier–Jacobi periods on unitary groups I: Coarse expansions of the relative trace formulae
Paul Boisseau, Weixiao Lu, Hang Xue

Abstract
. This is the first of a series of three papers where we prove the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture for Fourier–Jacobi periods on unitary groups and an Ichino–Ikeda type refinement. Our strategy is based on the comparison of relative trace formulae formulated by Liu. The goal of this first paper is to introduce the relative trace formulae and establish the coarse expansions.



1.
Introduction

In the 1990s, Gross and Prasad
[
18
,
19
]
formulated some conjectures on the
restriction problems for orthogonal groups. These conjectures were later
extended to all classical groups by Gan, Gross and Prasad in their
book
[
15
]
. Ichino and Ikeda
[
21
]
gave a refinement of the
conjecture of Gross and Prasad, and this refinement was further extended to
other cases
[
20
,
31
,
41
,
42
]
. These conjecture are usually
referred to as the Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjectures and have attracted
significant amount of research. These conjectures describe the relation
between certain period integrals of automorphic forms on classical groups and
the central values of some
L
-functions.

There are two kinds of period integrals in the GGP conjecture: Bessel periods
and Fourier–Jacobi periods. Fourier–Jacobi periods usually involve a theta
functions while Bessel periods do not. Specifying to the case of unitary
groups, Bessel periods are on the unitary groups
\U(n)\times\U(m)
where

n-m
is odd, while the Fourier–Jacobi case is when
n-m
is even. The
cases of Bessel periods on unitary groups, together with their refinements,
are now completely settled, by the combination of the work of many people,
cf.
[
24
,
44
,
46
,
45
,
43
,
8
,
9
,
7
,
5
,
6
]
for an
incomplete list. The proof follows the approach of Jacquet and Rallis
via the comparison of relative trace formulae.

Inspired by the work of Jacquet and Rallis, Liu
[
30
]
proposed a
relative trace formula approach towards the Fourier–Jacobi case of the conjecture. Following
this approach, we previously in
[
40
,
41
]
proved some cases of the
GGP conjecture for
\U(n)\times\U(n)
under various local conditions. The
goal of this series of three papers is to work out the comparison of these relative trace
formulae in general, and prove the GGP conjecture for Fourier–Jacobi periods
on unitary groups. The goal of this series of papers is to prove, among many other things, the following two conjectures.

(1)

The global GGP conjecture for
\U(n)\times\U(m)
, where
n-m
is
even, as stated in
[
15
]
*Conjecture 26.1.

(2)

An exact analogue of a conjecture of Ichino and Ikeda stated in
[
21
]
*Conjecture 1.4
in the context of Fourier–Jacobi periods, which is
a refinement of the global GGP conjecture.

This is the first paper in this series. Its goal is to introduce the relative trace formulae and establish the coarse expansions. In the rest of this introduction, we explain what we are going to prove in this series of papers in a little more detail. This serves as an introduction to the whole series of the papers.

1.1.
Fourier–Jacobi periods

Let
E/F
be a quadratic extension of number fields, and
\mathbb{A}_{E}
and
\mathbb{A}

be their rings of adeles respectively. Denote by
\mathsf{c}
the nontrivial
element in the Galois group
\Gal(E/F)
. For any positive integer
k
, set

G_{k}:=\Res_{E/F}\GL_{k}
, where
\Res_{E/F}
is the Weil restriction of scalars. Fix a nontrivial additive character

\psi:F\backslash\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let
\eta:F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}
be the quadratic character attached to the extension
E/F
via global class field theory. Let
\mu:E^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character such that
\mu|_{\mathbb{A}^{\times}}=\eta
.

Let
(V,q_{V})
be a nondegenerate
n
-dimensional skew

\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian space, with
skew-Hermitian form
q_{V}
. Let
\Res V
be the
F
-symplectic space whose
underlying vector space is
V
viewed as a
F
-vector space, and
whose symplectic pairing is given by
\Tr_{E/F}\circ q_{V}
. Let
\Res V=L+L^{\vee}
be a polarization, i.e.
L
and
L^{\vee}
are maximal isotropic subspaces
of
V
such that the pairing
\Tr_{E/F}\circ q_{V}
is
nondegenerate when restricted to
L\times L^{\vee}
. We have a Weil representation
\omega=\omega_{\psi,\mu}
, realized on the space of Schwartz functions
\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
,
cf. Subsection
4.4
. It depends on the characters

\psi
and
\mu
. We denote by
\omega^{\vee}=\omega_{\psi^{-1},\mu^{-1}}
the dual
representation of
\omega
which we also realize on
\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. For
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
we may form the theta
function

\theta(g,\phi)=\sum_{x\in L^{\vee}(F)}(\omega(g)\phi)(x),\quad g\in\U(V)(\mathbb{A}).

We define similarly
\theta^{\vee}(g,\phi)
when
\omega^{\vee}
is used.

Put
\U_{V}:=\U(V)\times\U(V)
and
\U_{V}^{\prime}:=\U(V)
, viewed as a subgroup of
\U_{V}

by the diagonal embedding. Let
\pi
be an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation of
\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
, and take
\varphi\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
be a Schwartz function. We introduce the Fourier–Jacobi period

(1.1)

\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\phi)=\int_{\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(h)\theta^{\vee}(h,\phi)\mathrm{d}h.

Here the theta function
\theta^{\vee}
instead of
\theta
is used here for compatibility with the choice in
[
15
]
.

The linear form
(\varphi,\phi)\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\phi,\lambda)
belongs to

\Hom_{\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})}(\sigma\otimes\omega^{\vee},\mathbb{C}).

Thus in order to have a nonzero Fourier–Jacobi period
\mathcal{P}
, this
\Hom

space has to be nonzero in the first place. By the multiplicity one
theorems
[
36
,
35
]
its dimension is at most one. Whether this space is of dimension zero or one is the subject of study of the local GGP conjecture, which is now estalished in
[
16
,
39
]
.

We denote by
\BC
the weak base change of
\pi
to
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\times\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
, and assume that it is a hermitian Arthur parameter, cf.
[
5
]
*Section 1 for an explanation of this notion. We will not need this until the third paper in this series, and it will be carefully explained there.

The global GGP conjecture for Fourier–Jacobi periods on unitary groups, in its simplest form, can be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let the notation and assumptions be as above. Assume that

\Hom_{\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})}(\pi\otimes\omega^{\vee},\mathbb{C})\not=0.

The following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{P}
is not identically zero.

(2)

L(\frac{1}{2},\BC(\pi)\otimes\mu^{-1})\not=0
.

There is a refinement of this theorem, which states that if
\pi
is tempered, then
\lvert\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\phi)\rvert^{2}
equals

\frac{L(\frac{1}{2},\BC(\pi)\otimes\mu^{-1})}{L(1,\pi,\Ad)}\times\cdots

where
\cdots
are some explicit and elementary constants. Both the theorem and its refinement will be proved in the third paper in this series.

In general, we may consider a pair of skew-hermitian spaces
W\subset V
, such that
\dim V-\dim W
is even. Let
\pi
be an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representations of
\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\times\U(W)(\mathbb{A})
. Then we may define Fourier–Jacobi periods for
\pi
, and there is an analogous GGP conjecture for these periods. We will also prove these conjectures and their refinement in the third paper in this series.

1.2.
The relative trace formulae

Our approach is through the comparison of relative trace formulae (RTF) proposed by Liu
[
30
]
, one on unitary groups which gives Fourier–Jacobi periods, and the other on general linear groups which gives central
L
-values.

We explain the RTF on unitary groups. Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}))
be a test function, and

K_{f}(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in\U_{V}(F)}f(x^{-1}\gamma y)

the usual automorphic kernel function on
\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
. Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
be Schwartz functions. We introduce the distribution

(1.2)

J(f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\iint_{(\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}))^{2}}K_{f}(x,y)\theta^{\vee}(x,\phi_{1})\theta(y,\phi_{2})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

If everything is absolutely convergent, e.g. when
V
is anisotropic, then the distribution
J
has a spectral expansion

\sum_{\pi}\sum_{\varphi}\mathcal{P}(\pi(f)\varphi,\phi_{1})\overline{\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\phi_{2})},

where
\pi
ranges over all cuspidal automorphic representations of
\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
and
\varphi
ranges over an orthonormal basis of
\pi
. This expansion is called the spectral side of the RTF.

Again assuming convergence, the distribution
J
unfolds to orbital integrals. Liu introduced in
[
30
]
a partial Fourier transform

-^{\ddagger}:\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\to\mathcal{S}(V(\mathbb{A}_{F})),

and defined a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})\times V(\mathbb{A}_{F}))
by

\varphi(g,v)=\int_{\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})}f(h^{-1},h^{-1}g)(\omega^{\vee}(h)\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\ddagger}(v).

The distribution
J
then unfolds to

J(f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})=\sum_{(\delta,v)}\int_{\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})}\varphi(h^{-1}\delta h,h^{-1}v)\mathrm{d}h,

where
(\delta,v)
ranges over all the orbits of
\U(V)(F)\times V(F)
under the action of
\U(V)(F)
given by

h\cdot(\delta,v)=(h\delta h^{-1},hv).

This is usually referred to as the geometric side of the RTF.

There is a striking similarity between the geometric side of Liu’s RTF and the Jacquet–Rallis RTF which tackles the GGP conjecture for the Bessel periods on unitary groups. In fact the infinitesimal version of the two geometric sides are essentially the same. The work
[
40
]
is largely based on this observation, and Liu’s RTF under the convergence assumptions has been worked out. To extend the work of
[
40
]
and prove the GGP conjecture in complete generality, extra work is needed. In particular we need to truncate the RTF.

1.3.
The truncation

The way we set up and truncate the RTFs contains some of our major innovations in this paper. We explain the ideas behind it here, in the setting of unitary groups.

There seems to be many ways to truncate the right hand side of (
1.2
). We may replace
K_{f}(x,y)
by Arthur’s modified kernel, or apply mixed truncation to
K_{f}(x,y)
. We may also apply some sort of truncation operators to the theta functions. There does not seem to be a priori a “canonical” way to truncate the RTF.

The key observation, and actually the starting point of this series of papers, is that the expression (
1.2
) can be written as a single kernel function on the Jacobi group.
Let
S(V)=\Res V+F
be the Heisenberg group
J(V)=S(V)\rtimes\U(V)
the Jacobi group, cf. Subsection
4.3
for a description of these groups. Take
f\in\mathcal{S}(\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})\times\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F}))
,
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. Define
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\times J(V)(\mathbb{A}))
by

\widetilde{f}(g_{1},\widetilde{g_{2}})=f(g_{1},g_{2})\langle\omega(\widetilde{g_{2}})\phi_{1},\overline{\phi_{2}}\rangle,

where
g_{1}\in\U(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})
,
\widetilde{g_{2}}\in J(V)(\mathbb{A}_{F})
and its image in
\U(V)(\mathbb{A})
is
g_{2}
. If
\widetilde{g_{2}}=((v,0),g_{2})
,
(v,0)\in S(V)
, then

\widetilde{f}(g_{1},\widetilde{g_{2}})=f(g_{1},g_{2})(\omega(\widetilde{g_{2}})\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\ddagger}(v).

That is, the Fourier transform introduced in
[
30
]
produces from a
f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
a test function on the group
\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\times J(V)(\mathbb{A})
. A little computation then gives that

K_{\widetilde{f}}(h_{1},h_{2})=K_{f}(h_{1},h_{2})\theta^{\vee}(h_{1},\phi_{1})\theta(h_{2},\phi_{2}),

if
h_{1},h_{2}\in\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
, if we define the kernel function
K_{\widetilde{f}}(h_{1},h_{2})
on
\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\times J(V)(\mathbb{A})
the same way as
K_{f}
on the unitary groups. If we were able to work with the Jacobi groups just like those reductive groups, then there would be standard way to define modified kernels on
\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\times J(V)(\mathbb{A})
.

A large part of the current paper is to make sense of these ideas. We introduce a special kind of subgroups of Jacobi group, which behave exactly like parabolic subgroups of reductive group. Though we tend to call them parabolic subgroups, but they are not parabolic subgroups in the usual sense, cf.
[
34
]
*Section 6.2. Instead they are defined using a description in
[
34
]
*Section 13.4 via coroots. To distinguish, we call them D-parabolic subgroups. We develop the theory of constant terms of automorphic forms on Jacobi groups along these D-parabolic subgroups, and prove the crucial result on “approximation by constant terms” for Jacobi groups. These are carried out in Sections 3 and 4 of this paper. Once we have this, truncating the RTF (
1.2
) follows a standard process. We model this process on the previous work
[
5
]
. It turns out that the D-parabolic subgroups of the Jacobi groups that we consider here and the parabolic subgroups that appear in the truncation process of the Jacquet–Rallis RTF are closely related. Thus we are able to truncate Liu’s RTF, in such a way that the similarity between its geometric side and that of Jacquet–Rallis RTF is preserved under the truncation process. This similarity will be further developed in the second paper in this series.

1.4.
Organization of this series of papers

In the first of this series of papers, we develop the coarse expansion of the RTFs, following the ideas we outlined above.

The second of this series of paper is devoted to comparing the geometric side of the RTFs. In particular we define the notation of matching of test functions, and prove what is usually called “smooth transfer” and “fundamental lemma”. By descending the geometric sides to their infinitesimal versions, we achieve a full comparison of the geometric sides. Then we prove a local character identity, which in turn characterizes the matching of test functions.

All these preparations lead to the final proof of the GGP conjectures and their refinement in the last paper in this series. We compute explicitly some terms on the spectral side of the RTFs. Following
[
5
,
6
]
, we not only compute those terms corresponding to the cuspidal representations, but also some “regular” Eisenstein series. In this way, we are able to prove the global GGP conjectures and the refinement for Fourier–Jacobi periods in all codimensions.

2.
Notation and conventions

This section contains some notation which will be used throughout the paper. Specific sets of notation will be fixed in each part.

2.1.
General notation

If
R
is a ring,
R^{n}
will denote the set of
n
dimensional column vectors
with coefficients in
R
, and
R_{n}
the set of row vectors.

If
f
and
g
are functions on some space
X
, we write
f\ll g
if there is a
constant
C
such that
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
for all
x\in X
. We say
f
and

g
are equivalent, denoted by
f\sim g
, if
f\ll g
and
g\ll f
.

Let
X
be a measure space. We denote by
\langle-,-\rangle_{L^{2}}
the
L^{2}
-inner product and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
the
L^{2}
-norm. We sometimes also write
\langle-,-\rangle_{X}
to emphasize the space
X
.

If
G
is a group and
f
is a complex valued function on
G
. We put
f^{\vee}(g)=f(g^{-1})
and
f^{*}(g)=\overline{f(g^{-1})}
.

When
G
is a group and
\mathcal{F}
is a space of functions on
G
which is invariant by right
(resp. left) translation, we denote by
\mathrm{R}
(resp.
\mathrm{L}
) the
corresponding representation of
G
on
\mathcal{F}
. If
G
is a Lie group and the
representation is differentiable, we will also denote by the same letter the
induced action of the Lie algebra or of its associated enveloping algebra. If

G
is a topological group equipped with a bi-invariant Haar measure, we
denote by
*
the convolution product of functions on
G

(whenever it is well-defined).

All of the topological vector spaces in this paper will be Banach, Hilbert,
Fréchet, or LF spaces. By an LF space, we mean a topological vector space which can be
expressed as a countable direct limit of Fréchet spaces in the category of locally convex spaces. Note that it is not
necessarily complete or even Hausdorff. If
V=\varinjlim_{n}V_{n}
is an LF space, we say that it is a strict LF space if the maps
V_{n}\to V_{n+1}
are all closed embeddings.
Strict LF spaces are complete, cf.
[
37
]
*Theorem 13.1 (note the LF
spaces in
[
37
]
are by definition what we call strict LF spaces).
The uniform boundedness principle and
the closed graph theorem hold for LF spaces, cf.
[
5
]
*Appendix A. We
use the notation
\otimeshat
to denote the projective completed tensor
product of two locally convex topological vector spaces.

2.2.
Fields

We fix a quadratic extension of number fields
E/F
unless otherwise specified. We denote by

c
the nontrivial Galois conjugation in
\Gal(E/F)
. Let
E^{-}
be the purely imaginary elements in
E
,
i.e.
E^{-}=\{x\in E\mid x^{\mathsf{c}}=-x\}
, and fix a nonzero
\tau\in E^{-}
. We denote by
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}_{E}
the ring of adeles of
F
and
E

respectively. We write
\mathbb{A}_{f}

the ring of finite adeles of
\mathbb{A}
. If
\mathtt{S}
is a finite set of places of
F
, we set
F_{\mathtt{S}}:=\prod_{v\in S}F_{v}
. When
\mathtt{S}=V_{F,\infty}
is the set of Archimedean places of
F
, we also write
F_{\infty}:=F_{V_{F,\infty}}
.

We denote by
\lvert\cdot\rvert
and
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{E}
the normalized
absolute values on
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
and
\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}
respectively. They satisfy

\lvert x\rvert_{E}=\lvert xx^{\mathsf{c}}\rvert
for all
x\in\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}
, and in particular

\lvert x\rvert_{E}=\lvert x\rvert^{2}
if
x\in\mathbb{A}^{\times}
.

We fix a nontrivial additive character of
\psi
of
F\backslash\mathbb{A}
, and let

\psi^{E}
and
\psi_{E}
be the nontrivial additive characters of
\mathbb{A}_{E}
given
respectively by

\psi^{E}(x)=\psi(\Tr_{E/F}(\tau x)),\quad\psi_{E}(x)=\psi(\Tr_{E/F}x).

Let
\eta
be the quadratic character of
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}

associated to the extension
E/F
by global class field theory. We fix an idele class character
\mu:\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}/E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
whose restriction to
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
is
\eta
.

2.3.
Groups

We denote by
\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
the additive group and the multiplicative group
over
F
respectively. If
F^{\prime}/F
is a field extension,
we denote by
G_{F^{\prime}}
the extension of scalars
G\times_{F}F^{\prime}
. We denote by

\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
the Lie algebra of
G
(over
F
).
We write
G_{\infty}
for
G(F_{\infty})
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}
for
\mathrm{Lie}(G_{\infty})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
,
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
for the universal enveloping algebra of

\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
for its center.
If
T
is a torus over
F
, we let
T_{1}
be the maximal split torus of

\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}T
and
T^{\infty}
be the neutral component of
T_{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.3.1.
Hermitian spaces and unitary groups

Let
V
be a vector space over
E
. We take the convention that a
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian
or skew
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian form
q_{V}
on
V
is linear in the first variable and anti-linear
in the second variable. A vector space with a nondegenerate
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian or
skew
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian form is called a
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian or skew
\mathsf{c}
-Hermitian space (or simply Hermitian or skew-Hermitian). For fixed
n
, we denote by
\mathcal{H}
the set of all isomorphism classes of nondegenerate
skew-Hermitian spaces over
E
of dimension
n
. If
v
is a place of
F
, we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{v}
the set of all isomorphism classes of nondegenerate
skew-Hermitian spaces over
E_{v}=E\otimes_{F}F_{v}
of dimension
n
. More generally, for a finite set
\mathtt{S}
of places, we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{S}}
the set of isomorphism classes of nondegenerate
skew-Hermitian spaces over
E_{\mathtt{S}}=E\otimes_{F}F_{\mathtt{S}}
of dimension
n
. We also denote by
\mathcal{H}^{\mathtt{S}}
the set of
V\in\mathcal{H}
such that
V\otimes_{E}E_{v}
has a self-dual lattice for all non-Archimedean
v\not\in\mathtt{S}
.

If
V
is a Hermitian or skew-Hermitian space of dimension
n
, we choose a basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
and put

\disc V=\det(q_{V}(v_{i},v_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\in E^{\times}.

If
V
is Hermitian then
\disc V\in F^{\times}
, while if
V
is
skew-Hermitian then
\tau^{n}\disc V\in F^{\times}
. The image of
\disc V

(when
V
is Hermitian) or
\tau^{n}\disc V
(when
V
is skew-Hermitian) in

F^{\times}/\mathrm{Nm}_{E/F}(E^{\times})
is independent of the choice of the basis.

2.4.
Measures

We now specify measures on the local and adelic points of algebraic groups. In this article, the precise choice of measures is not crucial, as we will not perform any explicit computations involving them. However, the choice of measures will play an important role in the subsequent articles
[
10
,
11
]
, which concern relative character identities, spectral expansions, and related topics. For the sake of consistency, we therefore fix the measures here.

For every place
v
of
F
, let
\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}x_{v}
be the unique Haar measure on
F_{v}
which is self-dual with respect to
\psi_{v}
, the local component of the additive character
\psi
of
\mathbb{A}
.

Let
G
be a connected linear group over
F
.
The choice of a right-invariant rational volume
form
\omega
on
G
together with the measure
\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}x_{v}
determine a right invariant Haar measure
\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}g_{v}
on each
G(F_{v})
(
[
38
]
).
By
[
17
]
, there is an Artin–Tate
L
-function
L_{G}(s)=\prod_{v}L_{G,v}(s)
, and more generally for
\mathtt{S}
a finite set of places its partial counterpart
L_{G}^{\mathtt{S}}(s)=\prod_{v\notin\mathtt{S}}L_{G,v}(s)
. Define
\Delta_{G}^{*}
and

\Delta_{G}^{\mathtt{S},*}
to be the leading coefficient of the Laurent expansion
at
s=0
of
L_{G}(s)
and
L_{G}^{\mathtt{S}}(s)
respectively. For each place
v
,
set
\Delta_{G,v}:=L_{G,v}(0)
. We equip
G(\mathbb{A})
with the Tamagawa
measure
\mathrm{d}g
defined as
\mathrm{d}g=\mathrm{d}_{\psi}g_{\mathtt{S}}\times\mathrm{d}_{\psi}g^{\mathtt{S}}
where
\mathrm{d}_{\psi}g_{\mathtt{S}}=\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}g_{v}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\psi}g^{\mathtt{S}}=(\Delta_{G}^{\mathtt{S},*})^{-1}\prod_{v\notin\mathtt{S}}\Delta_{G,v}\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}g_{v}
. Note that for any model of
G
over

\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\mathtt{S}}
we have for almost all
v

(2.1)

\vol(G(\mathcal{O}_{v}),\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}g_{v})=\Delta_{G,v}^{-1}.

Although
\mathrm{d}_{\psi_{v}}g_{v}
depends on various choices, the Tamagawa measure
\mathrm{d}g
does not. Note that if
G
is a unipotent group, the measure we just picked satisfies
\vol([G])=1
.

If
G=\GL_{n}
, we will take the form

\omega=(\det g)^{-n}\wedge_{i,j}\mathrm{d}g_{ij},

so that (
2.1
) is satisfied for every non-Archimedean place
v
of
F
where
\psi_{v}
is unramified.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Groups, parabolic subgroups

Let
G
be a connected linear algebraic group over a number field
F
. We introduce a class of
subgroups of
G
which will play the role of parabolic subgroups for
reductive groups. The group
G
we have in mind is either a reductive group,
or a Jacobi group which will be introduced in
Section
4
.

By a theorem of Mostow
[
14
]
*Proposition 5.4.1, the group
G
can
be written in the form
U\rtimes H
, where
H
is reductive and
U
is the
unipotent radical of
G
. The group
H
is then called a Levi subgroup of
G
. We fix such a decomposition, choose a maximal split
torus
A_{0}
of
H
and choose
P_{0}\subset H
a minimal parabolic subgroup
that contains
A_{0}
. We say that a parabolic subgroup of
H
is semistandard if it
contains
A_{0}
, and say that it is standard if it contains
P_{0}
. Note that
A_{0}
is
also a maximal split torus of
G
. Denote by
X^{*}(A_{0})
the group of rational characters of
A_{0}
, and by
X_{*}(A_{0})
the group of rational cocharacters of
A_{0}
. Denote by
\langle-,-\rangle:X^{*}(A_{0})\times X_{*}(A_{0})\to\mathbb{Z}
the canonical pairing between these groups.

The torus
A_{0}
has an adjoint action on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
, thus a
root-space decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi_{G}\subset X^{*}(A_{0})}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
satisfies

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\mathrm{Ad}(a)x=\alpha(a)(x)\text{ for all }a\in A_{0}\}.

The set
\Phi_{G}
is the set of
roots
. It consists of those
\alpha\in X^{*}(A_{0})
such that
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\neq 0
. For
\alpha\in\Phi_{G}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
is called a
root space
of
\mathfrak{g}
. We say that
G
has the symmetric root property, or property SR for short, if
the following condition holds:

(SR)

\alpha\in\Phi_{G}\iff-\alpha\in\Phi_{G}.

As maximal split tori in
H
are conjugate to each other, the condition

(
SR
)
is independent of the choice of
A_{0}
. Moreover, the Levi subgroups of
G
are conjugate so that
(
SR
)
also does not depend on the choice of
H
. Note that
(
SR
)
holds for all connected reductive groups.

A subsemigroup of
X^{*}(A_{0})
is by definition a subset
\Gamma
of
X^{*}(A_{0})
closed under
addition. We recall the following root subgroup construction
from
[
13
]
*Proposition 3.3.6, Theorem 3.3.11.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any subsemigroup
\Gamma\subset X^{*}(A_{0})
, there exists a unique connected

F
-subgroup
H_{\Gamma}(G)
of
G
such that

\mathrm{Lie}(H_{\Gamma}(G))=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Gamma\cap\Phi_{G}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Moreover, we have the following properties.

(1)

H_{\Gamma}(G)
is stable under conjugation by
A_{0}
.

(2)

If
0\not\in\Gamma
, then
H_{\Gamma}(G)
is unipotent.

Moreover, assume that
G
is solvable and suppose that there is a disjoint union decomposition
\Phi_{G}=\sqcup_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}
such that
\Phi_{i}
is disjoint from the semigroup
\Gamma_{j}
generated by
\Phi_{j}
whenever
i\neq j
. Then the map induced by group
multiplication

H_{\Gamma_{1}}(G)\times\cdots\times H_{\Gamma_{n}}(G)\to G

is an isomorphism of
F
-schemes.

Remark 3.2
.

Since
\mathrm{char}(F)=0
, a connected subgroup is determined by its Lie
algebra. Therefore, we do not need the
A_{0}
-stable condition in
[
13
]
*Proposition 3.3.6 but
have it as a property instead.

Let
\lambda
be a cocharacter of
A_{0}
. It determines three subsemigroups of

X^{*}(A_{0})
defined by the equations
\{\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\geq 0\}
,

\{\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle>0\}
and
\{\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle=0\}
respectively. The resulting root subgroups are denoted
by
P(\lambda)
,
U(\lambda)
and
Z(\lambda)
respectively. They are the
dynamical subgroups described in
[
34
]
*Section 13.4. We have
the semidirect product decomposition

(3.1)

P(\lambda)=Z(\lambda)\ltimes U(\lambda).

Lemma 3.3
.

If
G
has the property (
SR
), then the
decomposition (
3.1
) only depends on
P(\lambda)
and not on

\lambda
.

Proof.

Let
S\subset\Phi_{G}
be the set of roots for
S(\lambda)
. If
G
has the property (
SR
), then for
\alpha\in S
, we have

\Phi_{U(\lambda)}=\{\alpha\in S\mid-\alpha\not\in S\},

which only depends on
S
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Lemma
3.3
does not hold in general without the condition (
SR
). The mirabolic subgroup in
\GL_{3}
is a counterexample.

From now on we will always assume that
G
has the property (
SR
).

We say that a subgroup of the form
P(\lambda)
for so
\lambda\in X_{*}(A_{0})
is a (semistandard)
D-parabolic
subgroup
, and we call the decomposition (
3.1
) the
D-Levi
decomposition
of
P(\lambda)
. When
P=P(\lambda)
, we denote
M_{P}:=Z(\lambda)
,
N_{P}:=U(\lambda)
,
\mathfrak{m}_{P}:=\mathrm{Lie}(M_{P})
and

\mathfrak{n}_{P}:=\mathrm{Lie}(N_{P})
. By Proposition
3.1
(2),

N_{P}
is always unipotent. When there is no confusion, we will omit
the subscript
P
in
M_{P}
and
N_{P}
, and whenever we write the equality
P=MN

we will always mean that it is the D-Levi decomposition of
P
in the above sense. The D-parabolic
subgroup
P
is called
standard
if
\lambda
is a dominant cocharacter
(with respect to
P_{0}
), or equivalently if
P(\lambda)\cap H
is a standard
parabolic subgroup of
H
.

Note that for any D-parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
,
M_{P}
also has the property (
SR
). Moreover, when
G
is reductive a D-parabolic subgroups is the same as a semistandard parabolic
subgroup, and the D-Levi decomposition is the same as the semistandard Levi
decomposition (that is,
A_{0}\subset M_{P}
).

We denote by
\Psi_{P}
the set of roots of the adjoint action of
A_{0}
on
\mathfrak{n}_{P}
.
If
P=P(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in X_{*}(A_{0})
, then
\Psi_{P}=\{\alpha\in\Phi_{G}\mid\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle>0\}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
P,Q
be two D-parabolic subgroups of
G
. If
P\subset Q
then
N_{Q}\subset N_{P}
.

Proof.

It suffices to check that
\mathfrak{n}_{Q}
is a subspace of
\mathfrak{n}_{P}
. Write

P=P(\lambda)
and
Q=P(\mu)
. Let
\alpha\in X^{*}(A_{0})
with
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0
. Assume for contradiction that
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\leq 0
. Then
\langle-\alpha,\lambda\rangle\geq 0
and
-\alpha\in\mathrm{Lie}(P)\subset\mathrm{Lie}(Q)
. This
contradicts
\langle-\alpha,\mu\rangle<0
.
∎

Let
P=P(\lambda)
and
Q=P(\mu)
with
P\subset Q
. Since
N_{Q}
is a normal
subgroup of
Q
, it is a normal subgroup of
N_{P}
. Set
\Psi_{P}^{Q}=\Psi_{P}\setminus\Psi_{Q}
. Let
N_{P}^{Q}
be the
subgroup corresponding to the semigroup
\{\alpha\in\Phi_{G}\mid\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle=0,\;\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle>0\}
. By the last assertion of Proposition

3.1
, we have

N_{P}=N_{Q}\rtimes N_{P}^{Q}.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{n}_{P}^{Q}\cong\mathfrak{n}_{P}/\mathfrak{n}_{Q}
of
N_{P}^{Q}
is
\oplus_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. In particular the group
N_{P}^{Q}
only depends on
P

and
Q
and not on the choices of
\lambda
and
\mu
.

The following lemma provides a filtration on
N_{P}^{Q}
which will be useful in the
proof of Theorem
3.22
.

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists an increasing filtration of unipotent subgroups

N_{0}=\{0\}\subset N_{1}\subset\cdots\subset N_{k}=N_{P}^{Q}

of
N_{P}^{Q}
such that the following properties hold.

•

Each
N_{i}
is normal in
N_{P}^{Q}
.

•

Put
\mathfrak{n}_{i}=\Lie(N_{i})
. There is a complementary subspace
\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}^{i}
of

\mathfrak{n}_{i}
in
\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}
which is contained in a root space. In
particular, the action of
A_{0}
on the Lie algebra of each
quotient
N_{i+1}/N_{i}

(0\leq i\leq k-1)
is by a character

\alpha_{i}\in X^{*}(A_{0})
.

•

N_{i+1}/N_{i}
is isomorphic to a product of
\mathbb{G}_{a}
’s.

Proof.

Assume that
P=P(\lambda)
and
Q=P(\mu)
. Take a
cocharacter
\nu
of
A_{0}
such that the numbers
\langle\alpha,\nu\rangle
, as

\alpha
runs through
\Psi_{P}^{Q}
, are positive and distinct. For each
n>0
,
let
\Gamma_{\geq n}
be the subsemigroup of
X^{*}(A_{0})
defined by
\{\alpha\mid\langle\alpha,\nu\rangle\geq n,\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle=0,\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle>0\}
. For
n_{0}
large enough, the filtration
\{0\}=H_{\Gamma_{\geq n_{0}}}\subset\ldots\subset H_{\Gamma_{\geq 1}}=N_{P}^{Q}
satisfies the first two conditions. One then further refines this filtration to make it satisfy the third (e.g. take the derived series of each
H_{\Gamma_{\geq n}}/H_{\Gamma_{\geq(n+1)}}
).
∎

Let
P
be a D-parabolic subgroup. We put

[G]_{P}=N_{P}(\mathbb{A})M_{P}(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}).

When
P=G
we simply write
[G]=[G]_{G}
. There is a natural map
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\to[G]_{P}
whose fibers are
N(F)\backslash N(\mathbb{A})
-torsors and hence are compact.

We write
A_{G}
for the maximal central split torus of
G
. More generally, for a D-parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
, we write
A_{P}=A_{M_{P}}
for the maximal central split torus of
M_{P}
.

3.2.
Heights and weights

3.2.1.
Heights on adelic points of algebraic groups

For any
F
-variety
X
, there is a height function
\|\cdot\|_{X(\mathbb{A})}
on
X(\mathbb{A})
introduced in
[
8
]
*Appendix A. When
G
is a connected linear algebraic group over
F
. We fix an embedding
\iota:G\hookrightarrow\GL_{N}
for some
N>0
the height function on

G(\mathbb{A})
can be described by

(3.2)

\lVert g\rVert=\prod_{v}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\{\lvert\iota(g)_{ij}\rvert_{v},\lvert\iota(g)^{-1}_{ij}\rvert_{v}\},

where the product runs over all places of
F
. Note that for another choice of embedding
\iota^{\prime}
yielding a height
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{\prime}
, there exists
r_{0}>0
such that
\lVert g\rVert^{1/r_{0}}\ll\lVert g\rVert^{\prime}\ll\lVert g\rVert^{r_{0}}
for
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
. The equivalence class of
\lVert\cdot\rVert
(in the preceding sense) is therefore independent of the choice of
\iota
. Note that if
G=F_{n}
or
F^{n}
is a vector space, then we may take the height function given, for
g=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in G(\mathbb{A})
, by

(3.3)

\lVert g\rVert=\prod_{v}\max\{1,\lvert x_{1}\rvert_{v},\ldots,\lvert x_{n}\rvert_{v}\}.

The height function
\|\cdot\|
on
G(\mathbb{A})
induces a height function
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
on
G(F_{\infty})
by the embedding
G(F_{\infty})\hookrightarrow G(\mathbb{A})
. It is explicitly given by

\lVert g\rVert_{\infty}=\prod_{v\mid\infty}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\{\lvert\iota(g)_{ij}\rvert_{v},\lvert\iota(g)^{-1}_{ij}\rvert_{v}\},

where the product runs over all Archimedean places of
F
. This is called an
algebraic scale on
G(F_{\infty})
in
[
4
]
.

3.2.2.
Heights modulo a central unipotent subgroup

In some circumstances we will need to work with the group
G
modulo a central unipotent subgroup
Z
(see Subsection
3.3
). In this setting, we will equip
G(\mathbb{A})
with the pull-back of the height function
\|\cdot\|
on the group
(G/Z)(\mathbb{A})
, defined in (
3.2
), by the projection
G\to G/Z
. The resulting function will still be denoted
\|\cdot\|
and be called a height function on
G(\mathbb{A})
. We take the convention that, whenever
G
is equipped with a fixed central unipotent subgroup
Z
, the notation
\|\cdot\|
always designates the function constructed this way. If necessary, the height function on
G(\mathbb{A})
defined in (
3.2
) will be denoted by
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
.

Note that by the equivalence of unipotent groups and
nilpotent Lie algebra,
Z
is necessarily isomorphic to product of copies of

\mathbb{G}_{a}
. Since
H^{1}_{\mathrm{fppf}}(R,Z)=0
for any
F
-algebra
R
,
we have
(G/Z)(R)=G(R)/Z(R)
.

The following two lemmas and Remark
3.9
, where we assume the above setting so that
G
is equipped with a fixed unipotent central subgroup
Z
, show that the distinction between
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
will be mostly inconsequential on adelic quotients.

Lemma 3.7
.

There exist
r_{1},r_{2}>0
, such that

\|g\|^{r_{1}}\ll\inf_{z\in Z(\mathbb{A})}\|zg\|^{\prime}\ll\|g\|^{r_{2}},\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

In other words, the quotient maps
G\to G/Z
satisifes the “norm-descent” property in
[
25
]
*Section 18

Proof.

By
[
8
]
*Proposition A.1.1(ii), the first inequality holds, so that we now
prove the second. The projection map
G\to G/Z
is a
Z
torsor in the fppf
topology. Since
G/Z
is affine we have
H^{1}_{\mathrm{fppf}}(G/Z,Z)=0
, and thus the
torsor is a trivial torsor. It follows that there exists a section
s:G/Z\to G
. By

[
8
]
*Proposition A.1.1(ii) again, there exists
r>0
such that
\|s(g)\|^{\prime}\ll\|g\|^{r}
for all
g\in(G/Z)(\mathbb{A})
, which implies the second inequality.
∎

Let
P
be a D-parabolic subgroup of
G
. Note that it contains
Z
. We define a height function on
[G]_{P}
by

(3.4)

\lVert g\rVert_{P}=\inf_{\gamma\in P(F)}\lVert\gamma g\rVert,\quad g\in[G]_{P}.

Lemma 3.8
.

There exist
r_{1},r_{2}>0
such that

\|g\|_{P}^{r_{1}}\ll\inf_{\gamma\in P(F)}\|\gamma g\|^{\prime}\ll\|g\|_{P}^{r_{2}},\quad g\in[G]_{P}.

Proof.

This directly follows from
3.7
, since

Z(F)\subset P(F)
and
Z(F)\backslash Z(\mathbb{A})
is compact.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Lemma
3.8
implies that, if we temporarily put

\|x\|^{\prime}_{P}=\inf_{\gamma\in P(F)}\|\gamma x\|^{\prime},

there exists
r_{0}>0
such that
\|g\|_{P}^{1/r_{0}}\ll\|g\|^{\prime}_{P}\ll\|g\|_{P}^{r_{0}}
for all
g\in[G]_{P}
. In particular, since the spaces of functions that we will define in Subsection
3.3
are mostly insensitive to raising the heights to a power, we may interchange the two constructions.

Remark 3.10
.

Recall that
H
is our fixed Levi subgroup of
G
. Set
P_{H}=P\cap H
, which is parabolic subgroup of
H
. Using the projection
G\to H
and the embedding
H\hookrightarrow G
, by
[
8
]
*Proposition A.1.1 we see that there exist
r_{1},r_{2}>0
such that for
x\in H(\mathbb{A})
, we have

\|x\|_{P_{H}}^{r_{1}}\ll\|x\|_{P}\ll\|x\|_{P_{H}}^{r_{2}}.

3.2.3.
Weights

We keep the setting of Subsection
3.2.2
, so that
G
is equipped with a central unipotent subgroup
Z
and that
P
is a D-parabolic subgroup.

By a weight on
[G]_{P}
we mean a positive measurable function on
[G]_{P}

such that there exist a positive number
N
and a constant
C
such that

(3.5)

w(xg)\leq Cw(x)\lVert g\rVert_{P}^{N},\quad x\in[G]_{P},\;g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

For example,
\|\cdot\|_{P}
is a weight function on
[G]_{P}
.

We say that a function
w
on
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
is a weight on

P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
if it is the composition of a weight on
[G]_{P}
with the
map
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\to[G]_{P}
. In particular
\|\cdot\|_{P}
gives a
weight on
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, and we say that it is a height function on
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
.

Remark 3.11
.

The definition of the weight in
[
5
]
differs slightly from ours. The
definition there requires that for all compact subgroups
J
of
G(\mathbb{A})
we have

w(x)\sim w(xk),\quad\text{for all $x\in[G]_{P}$, $k\in J$}.

If
G
is reductive, which is the case considered in
[
5
]
, the two
definitions are equivalent by
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.4.3.1. In general our
definition imposes a stronger condition. Indeed
exponential functions on affine spaces satisfy the condition in
[
5
]

while our condition rules them out.

3.3.
Space of functions

Let
G
be a connected linear algebraic group over
F
.

3.3.1.
Representations

Following
[
4
,
5
]

we introduce certain nice categories of representations. We first consider
representations of
G(F_{\infty})
. A Fréchet representation
V
of

G(F_{\infty})
is called an F-representation if its topology is induced by a
countable family of
G(F_{\infty})
-continuous
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
-bounded
semi-norms. Here a semi-norm
\nu
on
V
is called

G(F_{\infty})
-continuous if the map

G(F_{\infty})\times(V,\nu)\to(V,\nu)

is continuous, where
(V,\nu)
is the vector space
V
endowed with the
topology induced from
\nu
. The semi-norm
\nu
is called

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
-bounded if there is a positive number
A
such that

\sup_{v\in V,\,\nu(v)\not=0}\frac{\nu(g\cdot v)}{\nu(v)}\leq\lVert g\rVert_{\infty}^{A}

for all
g\in G(F_{\infty})
. By
[
4
]
*Lemma 2.10, the Fréchet
representation
V
is an F-representation if and only if it is of
moderate growth, i.e.
if for any continuous semi-norm
\nu
on
V
there exists another continuous
semi-norm
\nu^{\prime}
on
V
and a positive number
A
such that

\nu(g\cdot v)\leq\lVert g\rVert_{\infty}^{A}\nu^{\prime}(v)

for all
g\in G(F_{\infty})
and
v\in V
. An F-representation
V

of
G(F_{\infty})
is called smooth, or is said to be an SF-representation if
for each
v\in V
the map

G(F_{\infty})\to V,\quad g\mapsto g\cdot v

is smooth, and if for every
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
the resulting map

V\to V,\quad v\mapsto X\cdot v

is continuous.

Remark 3.12
.

The
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
-bounded condition is not included
in
[
5
]
*Section 2.5.3. This is because only reductive groups are
considered there, where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
is equivalent to the
“maximal scale”, cf.
[
4
]
*Section 2.1, and thus the condition of

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
-boundedness is automatic. We however need to work with
representations
of nonreductive groups, and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
-boundedness is crucial.

We now consider representations of
G(\mathbb{A})
. An SLF-representation of
G(\mathbb{A})

is a vector space
V
equipped with a
G(\mathbb{A})
-action, with the following
properties.

•

For each open compact subgroup
J
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
the space
V^{J}
is an
SF-representation of
G(F_{\infty})
.

•

We have

V=\bigcup_{J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}V^{J},

where
J
runs over all open compact subgroups of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

•

If
J^{\prime}\subset J
then the natural map
V^{J}\to V^{J^{\prime}}
is a closed embedding.

3.3.2.
Smooth functions and Schwartz space

We now let
Z\subset G
be a
central unipotent subgroup.

Let
\psi:Z(F)\backslash Z(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character. We define

C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
to be the vector space of functions
f:G(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{C}
such
that

•

f
is right invariant under a compact open subgroup
J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,

•

for all
g_{f}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, the function
g_{\infty}\mapsto f(g_{f}g_{\infty})
is a smooth function on the Lie group
G(F_{\infty})
,

•

f(zg)=\psi(z)f(g)
for all
z\in Z(\mathbb{A})
and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
.

Remark that we do not require

f
to be left invariant under some compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A})
. We denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
the subspace of compactly supported functions modulo
Z(\mathbb{A})
. If
\psi
is trivial we omit it from the notation
in all function spaces.

Choose a height function
\lVert\cdot\rVert
on
G(\mathbb{A})
which is the pullback of a height function on
(G/Z)(\mathbb{A})
, as described in Subsection
3.2.2
. For a compact open subset
C
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and a
compact open subgroup
J
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we denote by

\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J,\psi)
the subspace of
C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
consisting
of functions
f
that are bi-invariant under
J
, supported
in
G(F_{\infty})\times CZ(\mathbb{A})
and such that for all
N>0
and
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
the semi-norm

\|f\|_{X,Y,N}:=\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\lVert g\rVert^{N}\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\mathrm{L}(Y)f(g)\rvert

is finite. This family of semi-norms gives
C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
the structure of a Fréchet space. Define the space of Schwartz functions
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
to be the union
of all such
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J,\psi)
as
C
and
J
vary, endowed with the natural topology of an LF space.

Remark 3.13
.

Note that
\mathrm{L}(Y)
and
\mathrm{R}(Y)
differ by the adjoint action, which is an algebraic representation on
G
. Therefore, the topology of
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J,\psi)
is also generated by the semi-norms
\|\cdot\|_{X,N}
where

\|f\|_{X,N}:=\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\lVert g\rVert^{N}\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)f(g)\rvert.

3.3.3.
The subgroup
\Gamma

Let
\Gamma
be a subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A})
such that its intersection with
Z(\mathbb{A})
equals
Z(F)
. We let
C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
be the subspace of
C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
of left
\Gamma
-invariant functions. For any open compact subgroup
J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we denote by
C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
its subspace of right
J
-invariant functions.

We will say that
\Gamma
satisfies the condition (SL) (for sufficiently large) if
\Gamma
contains
P(F)
for some D-parabolic
P
. We will assume that
\Gamma
satisfies this condition for the rest of this section.

In later text, the group
G
will be a reductive group or a Jacobi group (see Section
4
). In these cases, if
\Gamma
satisfies the property (SL) then for any open compact subgroup

J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there exists an open compact subset
C\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such
that the support of any
f\in C^{\infty}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
is contained in

\Gamma(C\times G(F_{\infty}))
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a set of representatives of
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/J
.
Then we see that there is a finite subset
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
C^{\infty}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
can be identified with a space of smooth functions on
\cup_{\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}(P(F)\cap\xi J\xi^{-1})\backslash G(F_{\infty})
, and
C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
is a subspace of it. When
G
is reductive, the existence of the compact subset
C
follows from finiteness of class number.

We slightly extend the definition of heights and weights to the case of
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
where
\Gamma
is as above. We define a height
\|\cdot\|_{\Gamma}
on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
by

\|g\|_{\Gamma}:=\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\|\gamma g\|.

Note that
\|g\|_{\Gamma}\geq 1
for all
g
. We define a weight on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
to be a positive measurable function on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})

such that there exist a positive number
N
and a constant
C
such that

w(xg)\leq Cw(x)\lVert g\rVert_{P}^{N},\quad\text{for all $x\in[G]_{P}$ and $g\in G(\mathbb{A})$}.

When
\Gamma=P(F)
or
M(F)N(\mathbb{A})
, these definitions coincide with the definition of weight on
[G]_{P}
given in (
3.5
).

3.3.4.
Spaces of Schwartz functions

We keep
\Gamma
to be as in Subsection
3.3.3
, so that it satisfies condition (SL).

Let
\mathcal{S}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
be the space of measurable
functions on
\varphi
on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
such that

\varphi(zx)=\psi(z)\varphi(x)
for almost all
(z,x)\in[Z]\times\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, and such that for any
N>0

\|\varphi\|_{\infty,N}:=\sup_{x\in\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}\|x\|_{\Gamma}^{N}|\varphi(x)|<\infty.

It is equipped with the family of seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,N}

which gives it the structure of Fréchet space. Let
\mathcal{S}^{00}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
be the closed subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
consisting of continuous functions.

Let
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
be the space of
Schwartz
functions on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. It is the subspace of

C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
consisting of
\varphi
satisfying
that for all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})

and integers
N>0
,

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{X,N,\infty}:=\sup_{g\in\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}\lVert g\rVert_{\Gamma}^{N}\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(g)\rvert<\infty.

For each compact open subgroup
J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
is a Fréchet space under the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{X,N,\infty}
, and
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
is an SLF representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
for the right translation
\mathrm{R}
.

Let
w
be a weight on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. For each
N\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{S}_{w,N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
be the
space of smooth functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
satisfying

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty,-N,w^{r},X}:=\sup_{g\in\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}\lVert g\rVert_{\Gamma}^{-N}w(g)^{r}\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(g)\rvert<\infty

for all
r\geq 0
and
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
. For each open compact
subgroup
J
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, the space
\mathcal{S}_{w,N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{J}
is a Fréchet space, and hence
\mathcal{S}_{w,N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
is a strict LF space. Put

\mathcal{S}_{w}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)=\bigcup_{N\geq 0}\mathcal{S}_{w,N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi),

which is naturally a non-strict LF space. This is the
\psi
-equivariant
weighted Schwartz
space
. In particular, when
w=\|\cdot\|_{\Gamma}
, we recover the definition
of
\mathcal{S}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
.

3.3.5.
Space of functions of uniform moderate growth

We retain the subgroup
\Gamma
from the previous subsection. Fix a weight
w
on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, and denote by
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{w}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
the space of complex Radon measures
\varphi
on

\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
with the properties that

\int_{\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}f(g)\varphi(g)=\psi(z)\int_{\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}f(zg)\varphi(g)

for any
f\in C_{c}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))
and
z\in[Z]
, and moreover that

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{1,w}:=\int_{\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}w(g)\lvert\varphi(g)\rvert<\infty.

The space
\mathcal{T}_{w}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
is naturally a Banach
space with the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1,w}
. We write
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
for the space

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\|\cdot\|^{-N}_{\Gamma}}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
,
and set

\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi):=\bigcup_{N>0}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi),

which is naturally an LF space.

We set

\mathcal{T}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi):=\mathcal{S}_{1}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)=\bigcup_{N>0}\mathcal{T}_{N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi),

where
\mathcal{T}_{N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi):=\mathcal{S}_{1,N}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
.
It is the

space of
\psi
-equivariant functions of uniform moderate growth
. It is equipped with the corresponding LF topology.

There is a natural pairing

(3.6)

\mathcal{S}^{00}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\times\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathbb{C},\quad(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\mapsto\langle\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle=\int_{\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(x)\overline{\varphi^{\prime}(x)}\mathrm{d}x.

It identifies
\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)

with the topological dual of
\mathcal{S}^{00}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
.

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})
,
we define a function
\mathrm{R}(f)\varphi
on
G(\mathbb{A})
by

\mathrm{R}(f)\varphi(x)=\int_{Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})}f(x^{-1}y)\varphi(y).

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})
, and
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
. Let
w
be a weight on
\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. If
w
is bounded on the
support of
\varphi
, then
\mathrm{R}(f)\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{w}(\Gamma\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.5.1.1.
∎

3.3.6.
Spaces of weighted
L^{2}
-functions on reductive groups

We now assume that
G
is reductive and let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. By taking
\Gamma=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})
and
Z=1
, we get various spaces of functions on
[G]_{P}
, including
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P}),\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})
, etc. These spaces are related to smooth vectors in weighted
L^{2}
spaces as we now explain.

The space
[G]_{P}=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
is equipped with the quotient of the Tamagawa measure on
G(\mathbb{A})
by the product of the counting measure on
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
with the Tamagawa measure on
N_{P}(\mathbb{A})
(see Subsection
2.4
). We denote by
L^{2}([G]_{P})
the space of
L^{2}
functions on
[G]_{P}
. It is a Hilbert space when equipped with the scalar product

\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{P}=\int_{[G]_{P}}\varphi_{1}(g)\overline{\varphi_{2}(g)}\mathrm{d}g.

More generally, if
w
is a weight on
[G]_{P}
, we write
L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})
for the Hilbert space of square-integrable functions with respect to the measure
w(g)\mathrm{d}g
. We denote the resulting norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{w,P,L^{2}}
. If
N\in\mathbb{R}
, we will simply write
L^{2}_{N}([G]_{P})
for
L^{2}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{P}^{N}}([G]_{P})
. It is equipped with a continuous (non-unitary) representation
\mathrm{R}
of
G(\mathbb{A})
by right-translation.

The subspace
L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty}
of smooth vectors consist of smooth functions
\varphi:[G]_{P}\to\mathbb{C}
such that for every
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
we have
\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi\in L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})
. It is equipped with the family of semi-norms
\lVert R(X)f\rVert_{w,P,L^{2}}
. For every open compact subgroup
J\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, the subspace
(L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty})^{J}
is a Frechet space, and we equip
L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty}=\bigcup_{J}(L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty})^{J}
with the corresponding structure of LF-space.

By the Sobolev inequality (
[
5
]
*(2.5.5.4)) and the open mapping theorem, we have an equality of SLF representations (where the right-hand side is equipped with the locally convex projective limit topology)

\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})=\bigcap_{N>0}L^{2}_{N}([G]_{P})^{\infty}.

Moreover, for every weight
w
on
[G]_{P}
,
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
is dense in
L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty}
.

By another use of the Sobolev inequality, we also have the equality of LF spaces

\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})=\bigcup_{N>0}L^{2}_{-N}([G]_{P})^{\infty}.

It follows that
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
is dense in
\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})
(but it is not dense in any
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})
in general). Finally, for every
N>0
there exist
N^{\prime}>0
and a continuous inclusion
L^{2}_{-N}([G]_{P})^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{T}_{N^{\prime}}([G]_{P})
.

3.4.
Pseudo-Eisenstein series and constant terms

Let
G
be a connected linear algebraic group over
F
that satisfies (
SR
). Recall that in Subsection
3.1
we have fixed a Levi
decomposition
G=U\rtimes H
, a maximal split torus
A_{0}\subset H
and a
minimal parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
of
H
which contains
A_{0}
. Let
P,Q
be two
semistandard D-parabolic subgroups of
G
with
P\subset Q
. We keep
Z
to be a central unipotent subgroup of
G
, and
\psi
a character of
[Z]
. Note that
M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\cap Z(\mathbb{A})=Z(F)
. We may therefore take
\Gamma=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})
or
\Gamma=P(F)

in the definitions of the various function spaces of Subsection
3.3
. In particular, we get spaces of
\psi
-equivariant functions on
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
or
[G]_{P}
. The same holds for
Q
.

Like in the case of reductive groups, we have
the construction of pseudo-Eisenstein series and the constant terms, which we
now explain. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
, we define a
pseudo-Eisenstein series

E_{P}^{Q}\varphi(g)=\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\varphi(\gamma g).

Lemma 3.15
.

The pseudo-Eisenstein series
\varphi\mapsto E_{P}^{Q}\varphi
defines a
continuous linear map

E_{P}^{Q}:\mathcal{S}^{?}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{S}^{?}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi),

where
?=0
,
00
, or empty.

Note that by restriction we also obtain a continuous linear map
E_{P}^{Q}:\mathcal{S}^{?}([G]_{P},\psi)\to\mathcal{S}^{?}([G]_{Q},\psi)
for
?=0
,
00
, or empty.

Proof.

It is more convenient to use the height function
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
G(\mathbb{A})
instead of the one pulled back from
G/Z(\mathbb{A})
(see Remark
3.9
). So
\|\cdot\|
in the proof below will denote the height function on
G(\mathbb{A})
. Let
d_{1},d_{2}>0
. For every
\gamma\in Q(F)
we have
\lVert\gamma\rVert_{P}^{-d_{2}}\ll\sum_{\delta\in P(F)}\lVert\delta\gamma\rVert^{-d_{2}}
. Moreover, it follows from
[
9
]
*Theorem A.1.1(1) that there exists
c>0
such that
\lVert xy\rVert^{c}\ll\lVert x\rVert\lVert y\rVert
. Therefore for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\left|\varphi(\gamma g)\right|\ll\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\lVert\gamma g\rVert_{P}^{-d_{1}-d_{2}}

\displaystyle\ll\lVert g\rVert^{-d_{2}}_{Q}\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\lVert\gamma g\rVert_{P}^{-d_{1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lVert g\rVert^{-d_{2}}_{Q}\sum_{\gamma\in Q(F)}\lVert\gamma g\rVert^{-d_{1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lVert g\rVert^{-d_{2}}_{Q}\lVert g\rVert^{d_{1}}\sum_{\gamma\in Q(F)}\lVert\gamma\rVert^{-cd_{1}}.

By
[
9
]
*Proposition A.1.1 (v), the sum
\sum_{\gamma\in Q(F)}\|\gamma\|^{-cd_{1}}
is finite for
d_{1}
large enough. As the LHS only depends on the class of
g
in
Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, we see that

\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\left|\varphi(\gamma g)\right|\ll\lVert\varphi\rVert_{d_{1}+d_{2},\infty}\lVert g\rVert_{Q}^{-d_{2}+d_{1}},\;\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi).

By taking
d_{2}
large enough, this shows that
E_{P}^{Q}
sends
\mathcal{S}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
to
\mathcal{S}^{0}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
, and that it is continuous. By a similar estimate we see that
E_{P}^{Q}
restricts a to continuous
map from
\mathcal{S}^{?}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
to
\mathcal{S}^{?}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
,
where
?=00
or empty.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

If
G
is reductive and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}([G]_{P})
then we have
E_{P}^{Q}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}([G]_{Q})
. This is the usual definition of pseudo-Eisenstein series
in
[
5
]
*Section 2.5.13.

We now define the constant terms. If
\varphi
is a Radon measure on
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, we define its constant term
\varphi_{P}
to be its pushforward to

[G]_{P}
along the projection map
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\to[G]_{P}
. Since the
fibers of
P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\to[G]_{P}
are compact, if
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
, then
\varphi_{P}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P},\psi)
. Note that this map is
the transpose of the pullback of functions
\mathcal{S}^{00}([G]_{P},\psi^{-1})\to\mathcal{S}^{00}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})
under the pairing (
3.6
), where we use the compactness of the fibers again to
ensure that a function on
[G]_{P}
of rapid decay is of rapid decay on

P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. On the subspace
\mathcal{T}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
, the constant
term is given by

\varphi_{P}(x)=\int_{[N_{P}]}\varphi(nx)\mathrm{d}n,

and
\varphi_{P}\in\mathcal{T}([G]_{P},\psi)
. Moreover
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{P}

induces continuous linear maps
\mathcal{T}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{T}([G]_{P},\psi)
and
\mathcal{T}_{N}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P},\psi)
for all
N
.

By Lemma
3.15
, the pseudo-Eisenstein
series
E_{P}^{Q}
gives a continuous linear map
\mathcal{S}^{00}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})\to\mathcal{S}^{00}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})
. Its transpose gives a continuous
linear map

\mathcal{T}^{0}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{T}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi).

Therefore we have a series of continuous linear maps

(3.7)

\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{Q},\psi)\to\mathcal{T}^{0}(Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{T}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\to\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P},\psi).

We denote the composition also by
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{P}
and again call
it the constant term. If
G
is reductive, this is the definition of the
constant term given in
[
5
]
*Section 2.5.13. The composition in (
3.7
) restricts to a continuous map
\mathcal{T}([G]_{Q},\psi)\to\mathcal{T}([G]_{P},\psi)
.

By construction we have

(3.8)

\langle\varphi,E_{P}^{Q}\varphi^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\varphi_{P},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle,\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}([G]_{Q},\psi^{-1}),\ \varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P},\psi),

where
\langle-,-\rangle
stands for the pairing between
\mathcal{S}^{00}
and

\mathcal{T}^{0}
defined in (
3.6
).

3.5.
Reductive groups, reduction theory

In this section, we let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
.

3.5.1.
Main notations

Let
X^{*}(G)
be the group of rational characters of
G
. Put
\mathfrak{a}_{G}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(G),\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}=X^{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
. We
have a canonical pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\times\mathfrak{a}_{G}\to\mathbb{R}.

Let
H_{G}:G(\mathbb{A})\to\mathfrak{a}_{G}
the Harish-Chandra map, with the defining
property that for any
\chi\in X^{*}(G)
, we have

(3.9)

\log\lvert\chi(g)\rvert=\langle\chi,H_{G}(g)\rangle.

Recall that we have fixed a maximal split torus
A_{0}
of
G
and a minimal parabolic
P_{0}=M_{0}N_{0}
. Let
P=MN
be a standard parabolic subgroup and
K
be a maximal compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A})
in good position with respect to
P_{0}
, so that we have the Iwasawa decomposition
G(\mathbb{A})=P(\mathbb{A})K
. We put
\mathfrak{a}_{P}=\mathfrak{a}_{M}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}
. For any
mnk\in G(\mathbb{A})
with
m\in M(\mathbb{A})
,
n\in N(\mathbb{A})
and
k\in K
, set

H_{P}(mnk)=H_{M}(m)\in\mathfrak{a}_{P},

where
H_{M}
is the Harish-Chandra map defined in (
3.9
) with respect to the reductive group
M
. We define
G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
to be the kernel of
H_{G}
. More generally, we define
G(\mathbb{A})_{P}^{1}
be the fiber of
0
of
H_{P}
, and it descends to a subset
[G]_{P}^{1}
of
[G]_{P}
. We also put
P(\mathbb{A})^{1}:=P(\mathbb{A})\cap G(\mathbb{A})^{1}_{P}
, which is a subgroup of
P(\mathbb{A})
.

We put
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\mathfrak{a}_{P_{0}}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P_{0}}^{*}
and
H_{0}=H_{P_{0}}
to shorten notation.

Let
P=MN
be a parabolic subgroup. We denote by
\delta_{P}:P(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the modulus character, and by
\rho_{P}
the half sum of roots of
A_{P}
in
N
. Then
\rho_{P}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
and satisfies

\delta_{P}(m)=e^{\langle 2\rho_{P},H_{M}(m)\rangle},\quad m\in M(\mathbb{A}).

Let
P\subset Q
be parabolic subgroups. Then we have
A_{Q}\subset A_{P}
. The restriction
X^{*}(Q)\to X^{*}(P)
induces
the maps
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
(which is an injection) and
\mathfrak{a}_{P}\to\mathfrak{a}_{Q}
, whose kernel is denoted by
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
. The restriction
X^{*}(A_{P})\to X^{*}(A_{Q})
induces the maps
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
(which is an injection) and
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}
, whose kernel is denoted by
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
. We have canonical
decompositions

\mathfrak{a}_{P}=\mathfrak{a}_{Q}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q},\quad\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}.

Define
\Delta_{P}^{Q}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
to be the set of simple roots of

A_{P}
in
M_{Q}\cap P
. We have the set of coroots
\Delta_{P}^{Q,\vee}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
. By duality, we also have the set of simple weights

\widehat{\Delta}_{P}^{Q}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
. The sets
\Delta_{P}^{Q}
and

\widehat{\Delta}_{P}^{Q}
define open cones in
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
whose characteristic
functions are denoted by
\tau_{P}^{Q}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{P}^{Q}
respectively. If

P=P_{0}
then we replace the subscript
P_{0}
in the notation by
0
. If
Q=G
then we omit the superscript
G
.

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
(G,A_{0})
, that is the quotient of the normalizer of
A_{0}
in
G(F)
by
M_{0}
the Levi subgroup of
P_{0}
. For
P=M_{P}N_{P}
and
Q=M_{Q}N_{Q}
two standards parabolic subgroups of
G
, denote by
W(P,Q)
the set
w\in W
such that
w\Delta_{0}^{P}=\Delta_{0}^{Q}
. In particular, for
w\in W(P,Q)
we have
wM_{P}=M_{Q}
.

3.5.2.
Haar measures

We equip
\mathfrak{a}_{P}
with the Haar measure
giving the lattice
\Hom(X^{*}(P),\mathbb{Z})
covolume
1
, and
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
with the
dual measure. If
Q\supset P
is another parabolic subgroup, then
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}\cong\mathfrak{a}_{P}/\mathfrak{a}_{Q}
and
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}\cong i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}
are equipped
with the quotient Haar measures.

3.5.3.
Truncation parameters

The fixed minimal parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
determine a positive chamber in

\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. By “
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
is sufficiently positive” we mean that “for
T

such that
\inf_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}}\alpha(T)\geq\max\{\epsilon\lVert T\rVert,C\}
”, where
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is an arbitrary norm on the real vector space

\mathfrak{a}_{0}
,
C>0
is a large enough constant and
\epsilon>0
is an arbitrary
(but in practice small enough) constant. We say that
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
is sufficiently
negative if
-T
is sufficiently positive.

For
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
and a standard parabolic subgroup
P
, we write
T_{P}
for the image of
T
under the projection map
\mathfrak{a}_{0}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
. If
P
is more generally semistandard, we write
T_{P}
for the image of
w\cdot T
under the projection map
\mathfrak{a}_{0}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
, where
w
is any element in the Weyl group satisfies
wP_{0}w^{-1}\subset P
.

3.5.4.
Reduction theory

Let
\omega_{0}\subset P_{0}(\mathbb{A})^{1}
be a compact subset such that
P_{0}(\mathbb{A})^{1}=\omega_{0}P_{0}(F)
. Let
P=MN
be a standard parabolic subgroup. By a Siegel domain
\mathfrak{s}^{P}
of
[G]_{P}
we mean a subset of

[G]_{P}
of the form

\mathfrak{s}^{P}=\omega_{0}\{a\in A_{0}^{\infty}\mid\langle\alpha,H_{0}(a)-T_{-}\rangle\geq 0,\ \alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{P}\}K,

where
T_{-}\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, and such that
G(\mathbb{A})=M(F)N(\mathbb{A})\mathfrak{s}^{P}
. We
assume that for different parabolic subgroups of
G
, their Siegel domains
are defined by the same
T_{-}
. In particular if
P\subset Q
then
\mathfrak{s}^{P}\supset\mathfrak{s}^{Q}
.

For
T,T_{-}\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, we define

A_{0}^{P,\infty}(T_{-},T)=\left\{a\in A_{0}^{\infty}\mid\langle\alpha,H_{0}(a)\rangle\geq\langle\alpha,T_{-}\rangle,\forall\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{P}\text{ and }\langle\varpi,H_{0}(a)\rangle\leq\langle\varpi,T\rangle,\forall\varpi\in\widehat{\Delta}_{0}^{P}\right\}.

We put
\mathfrak{s}^{P}(T)=\mathfrak{s}^{P}(T_{-},T,\omega_{0},K)=\omega_{0}A_{0}^{P,\infty}(T_{-},T)K
and call it the truncated Siegel set. Let

F^{P}(\cdot,T)
be the characteristic function on
[G]_{P}
of the set

M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\mathfrak{s}^{P}(T)
. For
T
sufficiently positive and
T_{-}

sufficiently negative, we have the Langlands partition formula
[
1
]
*Lemma 6.4:

(3.10)

\sum_{P_{0}\subset Q\subset P}\sum_{\gamma\in Q(F)\backslash P(F)}F^{Q}(\gamma x,T)\tau_{Q}^{P}(H_{0}(\gamma x)-T)=1.

Let
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}
and
P
be a parabolic subgroup. A weight
d_{P,\lambda}
on
[G]_{P}
is introduced in
[
5
]
*Section 2.4.3. If
g\in\mathfrak{s}^{P}
, then we have

d_{P,\lambda}(g)\sim e^{\langle\lambda,H_{P}(g)\rangle}.

If
P\subset Q
are parabolic subgroups, two weights
d_{P}^{Q}
and
d_{Q}^{P}
on
[G]_{P}
and
[G]_{Q}
respectively are introduced in
[
5
]
*Section 2.4.4 and are given by

d_{P}^{Q}(g)=\min_{\lambda\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}d_{P,\lambda}(g),\quad d_{Q}^{P}(g)=\min_{\lambda\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}d_{Q,\lambda}(g),

where
g\in[G]_{P}
and
[G]_{Q}
respectively. Consider the projections

(3.11)

[G]_{P}\xleftarrow{\pi_{P}^{Q}}P(F)N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\xrightarrow{\pi_{Q}^{P}}[G]_{Q}.

For
C>0
define

\omega_{P}^{Q}[>C]=\{g\in P(F)N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\mid d_{P}^{Q}(\pi_{P}^{Q}(g))>C\}.

We recall
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.4.4.1 which summarizes some classical results
from reduction theory.

Lemma 3.17
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

There is an
\epsilon>0
such that
\pi_{Q}^{P}
maps

\omega_{P}^{Q}[>\epsilon]
onto
[G]_{Q}
.

(2)

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have

d_{P}^{Q}(g)\sim d_{Q}^{P}(g),\quad\lVert g\rVert_{P}\sim\lVert g\rVert_{Q}

for all
g\in\omega_{P}^{Q}[>\epsilon]
.

(3)

For all
\epsilon>0
, the restriction of
\pi_{Q}^{P}
to

\omega_{P}^{Q}[>\epsilon]
has uniformly bounded fibers.

(4)

For all
\epsilon>0
, there is a
C>0
such that if
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\omega_{P}^{Q}[>\epsilon]\times\omega_{P}^{Q}[>C]
and
\pi_{Q}^{P}(g_{1})=\pi_{Q}^{P}(g_{2})
, then
g_{1}=g_{2}
.

(5)

The map
\pi_{Q}^{P}
is a local homeomorphism that locally preserves the
measures. The map
\pi_{P}^{Q}
is proper and the pushforward of the
invariant measure on
P(F)N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
by it is the
invariant measure on
[G]_{P}
.

3.6.
Automorphic forms and Eisenstein series

We assume that
G
is reductive and connected in this subsection.

3.6.1.
Spaces of automorphic forms

Let
P=MN
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Equip
A_{P}^{\infty}
with the Haar measure
\mathrm{d}a
such that the isomorphism
H_{P}:A_{P}^{\infty}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
is measure preserving (see Subsection
3.5.2
). Set
[G]_{P,0}=A_{P}^{\infty}\backslash[G]_{P}
. It is equipped with the quotient of the Tamagawa invariant measure on
[G]_{P}
. More precisely, it is the right-invariant functional on the space of continuous functions
f
on
[G]_{P}
such that
f(ag)=\delta_{P}(a)f(g)
for any
(a,g)\in A_{P}^{\infty}\times[G]_{P}
, such that for any
f\in C_{c}([G]_{P})
we have

\int_{[G]_{P,0}}\int_{A_{P}^{\infty}}f(ag)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}g=\int_{[G]_{P}}f(g)\mathrm{d}g.

We define the space of
automorphic forms
\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
to be the subspace of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
-finite
functions in
\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})
. We define
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
) to be the subspace
of cuspidal automorphic forms (resp. discrete automorphic forms), i.e. functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
such that

\varphi_{Q}=0
for all
Q\subsetneqq P
(resp. such that
\left|\varphi\right|\in L^{2}([G]_{P,0})
). We will simply drop the subscripts
P
when
P=G
.

A cuspidal (resp. discrete) automorphic representation
\pi
of
M(\mathbb{A})
is a topologically
irreducible subrepresentation of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M)
(resp. of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{disc}}(M)
). Let
\pi
be a
cuspidal (resp. discrete) automorphic representation of
M(\mathbb{A})
. We define
\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(M)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(M)
) to be the
\pi
-isotypic component of

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{disc}}(M)
), and set

\Pi=\Ind_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}\pi,\quad\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)=\Ind_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(M),\quad\left(\text{resp. }\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(G)=\Ind_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(M)\right).

Here
\Ind
stands for the normalized smooth induction. These spaces have a natural topology described in
[
5
]
*§2.7 which gives them the structure of SLF representations of
G(\mathbb{A})
. We identify

\Pi
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
,
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
) with the space of
forms
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
such that the function

m\mapsto e^{-\langle\rho_{P},H_{P}(m)\rangle}\varphi(mg),\quad m\in[M]

belongs to
\pi
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}_{\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
) for every
g\in[G]_{P}
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. We define the twist
\pi_{\lambda}
as the
space of functions of the form

m\mapsto e^{\langle\lambda,H_{P}(m)\rangle}\varphi(m),\quad m\in M(\mathbb{A}),\quad\varphi\in\pi.

If
\pi
is cuspidal, for
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
we denote by

I(\lambda,f)
the action of
f
on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi_{\lambda},\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
obtained by transporting the action on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
through the identification

\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\to\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi_{\lambda},\mathrm{cusp}}(G),\quad\varphi\mapsto e^{\langle\lambda,H_{P}(\cdot)\rangle}\varphi(\cdot).

In the same way, if
\pi
is discrete we also get an action on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi_{\lambda},\mathrm{disc}}(G)
still denoted by
I(\lambda,f)
.

If the central character of
\pi
is unitary, we equip
\Pi
and
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
) with the Petersson inner product

(3.12)

\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}=\int_{[G]_{P,0}}\varphi_{1}(g)\overline{\varphi_{2}(g)}\mathrm{d}g.

For every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
,
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have the Eisenstein series

E(g,\varphi,\lambda)=\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash G(F)}\varphi(\gamma g)e^{\langle\lambda,H_{P}(\gamma g)\rangle}.

This sum is absolutely convergent when
\Re\lambda
is in a certain cone,
and
E(g,\varphi,\lambda)
has a meromorphic continuation to all
\lambda
which is regular on
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. By
[
28
]
*Theorem 2.2, for every discrete automorphic representation
\pi
of
M(\mathbb{A})
and for every
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, the map

\varphi\mapsto E(\cdot,\varphi,\lambda)
induces a continuous map
\Pi\to\mathcal{T}([G])
that actually factors through
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
for some
N>0
. The resulting map
\Pi\to\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
is continuous by the closed graph theorem (see Remark
5.9
).

3.7.
Cuspidal data and Langlands decompositions

3.7.1.
Cuspidal data

We continue to assume that
G
is reductive in this subsection. Let
\underline{\mathfrak{X}}(G)
be the set of pairs
(M_{P},\pi)
where

•

P=M_{P}N_{P}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
,

•

\pi
is an (isomorphism class of a) cuspidal automorphic representation of
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
whose central character is trivial on
A_{P}^{\infty}
.

Two elements
(M_{P},\pi)
and
(M_{Q},\tau)
of

\underline{\mathfrak{X}}(G)
are equivalent if there
is a
w\in W(P,Q)
such that
w\pi w^{-1}=\tau
. We define a cuspidal
datum to be an equivalence class of such
(M_{P},\pi)
and denote by
\mathfrak{X}(G)

the set of all cuspidal data. If
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
is represented by
(M_{P},\pi)
we define
\chi^{\vee}
to be the cuspidal datum represented by
(M_{P},\pi^{\vee})
. Note that the natural inclusion
\underline{\mathfrak{X}}(M)\subset\overline{\mathfrak{X}}(G)
descends to a finite-to-one map
\mathfrak{X}(M)\to\mathfrak{X}(G)
.

3.7.2.
Coarse Langlands decomposition

For
(M_{P},\pi)\in\underline{\mathfrak{X}}(G)
, let
\mathcal{S}_{\pi}([G]_{P})
be the space of
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
such that

\varphi_{\lambda}(x):=\int_{A_{P}^{\infty}}e^{-\langle\rho_{P}+\lambda,H_{P}(a)\rangle}\varphi(ax)\mathrm{d}a,\;x\in[G]_{P},

belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi_{\lambda},\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
for every
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.

Let
P\subset G
be a standard parabolic subgroup,
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
be a cuspidal datum and
\{(M_{Q_{i}},\pi_{i})\;|\;i\in I\}
be the inverse image of
\chi
in
\underline{\mathfrak{X}}(M_{P})
. Denote by
L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P})
the closure in
L^{2}([G]_{P})
of the subspace

(3.13)

\sum_{i\in I}E_{Q_{i}}^{P}(\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{i}}([G]_{Q_{i}})).

We define similarly
L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P,0})\subset L^{2}([G]_{P,0})
. The coarse Langlands decomposition (
[
32
]
*Proposition II.2.4) states that we have decompositions in orthogonal direct sums

L^{2}([G]_{P})=\widehat{\bigoplus_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}}L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P})\quad\text{and}\quad L^{2}([G]_{P,0})=\widehat{\bigoplus_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}}L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P,0}).

For any subset
\mathfrak{X}\subset\mathfrak{X}(G)
, set

L^{2}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P})=\widehat{\bigoplus_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}}}L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P}),

and define

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P})=\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})\cap L^{2}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P}).

Let
w
be a weight on
[G]_{P}
. For any
\mathcal{F}\in\{L^{2}_{w},\mathcal{T}_{N},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{S}_{w,N},\mathcal{S}_{w}\}
, define
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P})
to be the orthogonal of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{X}^{c}}([G]_{P})
in
\mathcal{F}([G]_{P})
, where
\mathfrak{X}^{c}
is the complement of
\mathfrak{X}
in
\mathfrak{X}(G)
. By
[
5
]
*Section 2.9.4, for
\mathcal{F}\in\{L^{2}_{w},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{S}\}
there are canonical projections

(3.14)

\mathcal{F}([G]_{P})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P}),\quad\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{\mathfrak{X}}.

The following proposition is contained in
[
5
]
*Theorem 2.9.4.1.

Proposition 3.18
.

There exists an integer
N_{0}
, such that for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{w}([G]_{P})

(resp.
\mathcal{S}_{w,N}([G]_{P})
), the family
(\varphi_{\chi})_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}

is absolutely summable with the sum
\varphi
in
\mathcal{T}_{w,N}([G]_{P})
(resp.

\mathcal{S}_{w,N+N_{0}}([G]_{P})
).

Moreover, we have the following result from
[
5
]
*Section 2.9.5 (which follows from the density of
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
in
L^{2}_{w}([G]_{P})^{\infty}
stated in Subsection
3.3.6
and the continuity of the projections
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{\mathfrak{X}}
).

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a subset of
\mathfrak{X}(G)
. The space
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P})
is dense in
L^{2}_{w,\mathfrak{X}}([G]_{P})^{\infty}
.

3.7.3.
Automorphic kernel functions

The right convolution by
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
on the spaces
L^{2}_{\chi}([G]_{P})
and
L^{2}([G]_{P})
gives rise to integral operators whose kernel are denoted by
K_{f,P,\chi}
and
K_{f,P}
respectively. If
P=G
we omit the subscript
G
.
These kernel functions satisfy the following estimates,
cf.
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.10.1.1.

Lemma 3.20
.

There exists
N_{0}>0
such that for every weight
w
on
[G]_{P}
and every
continuous seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{w,N_{0}}
on
\mathcal{T}_{w,N_{0}}([G]_{P})
, there
exists a continuous seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
such that for
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\lVert K_{f,P,\chi}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{w,N_{0}}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}w(y)^{-1},\quad y\in[G]_{P}.

In particular

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\lvert K_{f,P,\chi}(x,y)\rvert\leq\lVert x\rVert_{P}^{N_{0}}w(x)w(y)^{-1}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{S}},\quad x,y\in[G]_{P}.

3.8.
Approximation by constant terms

If
G
is reductive, functions of uniformly moderate growth are approximated
by their constant terms in a precise sense. This is the “approximation by
constant terms”, cf.
[
5
]
*Theorem 2.5.14.1 (1). We extend this to
the case of possibly nonreductive groups satisfying the
condition (
SR
).

Let
G=U\rtimes H
be a connected algebraic group satisfying the
condition (
SR
) as in Section
3.1
.
Let
P
be a standard D-parabolic subgroup. Put
P_{H}=P\cap H
, which is a parabolic subgroup of
H
. For
P\subset Q
, we define a weight
function
d_{P}^{Q}
on
[H]_{P_{H}}
by

(3.15)

d_{P}^{Q}(h)=\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}d_{P_{H},\alpha}(h),\quad h\in[H]_{P_{H}}.

We recall that
\Psi_{P}^{Q}=\Psi_{P}\backslash\Psi_{Q}
is the set of roots of
A_{0}
action on
\mathfrak{n}_{P}^{Q}=\mathfrak{n}_{P}/\mathfrak{n}_{Q}
. Note that
P,Q
are not parabolic subgroups of
H
, and the weight
d_{P}^{Q}
is not to be confused with
d_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}
which we recall is defined by

d_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}(h)=\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}}d_{P_{H},\alpha}(h),\quad h\in[H]_{P_{H}}.

However we have the following relation.

Lemma 3.21
.

We have
d_{P}^{Q}\leq d_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have
N_{P}\cap H=N_{P_{H}}
and
N_{Q}\cap H=N_{Q_{H}}
, so that
\mathfrak{n}_{P_{H}}/\mathfrak{n}_{Q_{H}}
embeds into
\mathfrak{n}_{P}/\mathfrak{n}_{Q}
as a subspace. It follows that
\Psi_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}\subset\Psi_{P}^{Q}
which implies
d_{P}^{Q}\leq d_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}
.
∎

The “approximation by constant terms” refers to the following theorem.

Theorem 3.22
.

Assume that
G
satisfies condition (
SR
). Let
P\subset Q
. Let
N>0
,
r\geq 0
and
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
. There exists a continuous
seminorm
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{N,X,r}
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{Q},\psi)
and
x\in P_{H}(F)N_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
, we have

(3.16)

\left|\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(x)-\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{P}(x)\right|\leq\|x\|_{P}^{N}d_{P}^{Q}(x)^{-r}\|\varphi\|.

By Remark
3.10
, we can also use
\|x\|_{P_{H}}
in right hand side of
3.16
.
It is however of critical importance that the weight
d_{P}^{Q}
appears on the right hand side, not
d_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}
.

We begin with an elementary lemma.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space over
F
and
U\subset V(\mathbb{A})
be an open compact subgroup. Then there exists a homogeneous
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{Lie}(V(F_{\infty})))
such that for any
f\in C^{\infty}([V])^{U}
and any sufficiently large integer
r
, we have

(3.17)

\|f-\int_{[V]}f(v)\mathrm{d}v\|_{L^{\infty}}\ll_{r}\|\mathrm{R}(X^{r})f\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Note that, by our convention on measures, the measure on
[V]
is the Tamagawa measure, i.e.
\mathrm{vol}([V])=1
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda=V(F)\cap U
which is a lattice in
V(F_{\infty})
. Since
V(F)
is dense in

V(\mathbb{A})
, the natural embedding induces a
V(F_{\infty})
-equivariant
isomorphism

\Lambda\backslash V(F_{\infty})\cong V(F)\backslash V(\mathbb{A})/U.

Thus we are reduced to the same problem for
\Lambda\backslash V(F_{\infty})
.
We pick a suitable basis and identify it with
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}V
. By classical Fourier series theory,
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}
suffices.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.22
.

Up to replacing
\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi
