by
\varphi
, we can assume that
X=1
. Since
the constant term map
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{P}
is continuous from
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{Q},\psi)
to

\mathcal{T}_{N}(P(F)N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
, there exists a continuous semi-norm
\|\cdot\|
on

\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{Q},\psi)
such that for all
x\in P(F)N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, we have

\left|\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(x)-\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{P}(x)\right|\leq\|x\|_{P}^{N}\|\varphi\|.

Thus we only need to consider those
x
such that
d_{P}^{Q}(x)>C
for some
C
,
hence
x\in\omega_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}[>C]
. For
C
large enough,
P_{H}(F)\mathfrak{s}^{Q_{H}}\subset\omega_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}[>C]
, hence we can assume
x\in\mathfrak{s}^{Q_{H}}
at the
beginning.

Note that

\varphi_{P}(x)=\int_{[N_{P}^{Q}]}\varphi(nx)\mathrm{d}n.

Take a filtration of
N_{P}^{Q}

\{0\}=N_{0}\subset N_{1}\subset\cdots\subset N_{k}=N_{P}^{Q}

as in Lemma
3.6
. For each
0\leq i\leq k-1
, set

\varphi_{i}(x):=\int_{[N_{i}]}\varphi(nx)\mathrm{d}n.

Then
\varphi_{0}=\varphi
and
\varphi_{k}=\varphi_{P}
. It suffices
to show that, for each
i
with
0\leq i\leq k-1
, there exists a continuous
semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{i}
on
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{Q},\psi)
such that for
x\in\mathfrak{s}^{Q_{H}}
,
we have

\left|\varphi_{i}(x)-\varphi_{i+1}(x)\right|\ll\|x\|_{P}^{N}d_{P}^{Q}(x)^{-r}\|\varphi\|_{i}.

Once we have this, the semi-norm
\|\cdot\|=\sup_{i}\|\cdot\|_{i}
satisfies the condition.

Since
N_{i+1}/N_{i}
is a vector space, up to enlarging
r
(which is possible as
d_{P}^{Q}(x)>C
), by Lemma

3.23
there exists an
\bar{X}\in\mathfrak{n}_{i+1,\infty}/\mathfrak{n}_{i,\infty}
such that we have

(3.18)

\left|\varphi_{i}(x)-\varphi_{i+1}(x)\right|=\left|\varphi_{i}(x)-\int_{[N_{i+1}/N_{i}]}\varphi_{i}(nx)\mathrm{d}n\right|\ll\left\|\mathrm{R}(\bar{X}^{r})\mathrm{R}(x)\varphi_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([N_{i+1}])}.

Take the complementary subspace
\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}^{i}
of
\mathfrak{n}_{i}
in
\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}
as
in the Lemma
3.6
. Let
X\in\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}^{i}
with image

\overline{X}
, then

\mathrm{R}(\overline{X}^{r})\mathrm{R}(x)\varphi_{i}=\mathrm{R}(X^{r})\mathrm{R}(x)\varphi_{i}=\mathrm{R}(x)\mathrm{R}\left(\mathrm{Ad}(x_{\infty}^{-1})(X^{r})\right)\varphi_{i}.

Therefore, as
[N_{i+1}]
is compact,

(3.19)

\left\|\mathrm{R}(\bar{X}^{r})\mathrm{R}(x)\varphi_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([N_{i+1}])}\ll\|x\|_{P}^{N}\sup_{y\in[G]_{Q}}\left(\|y\|_{P}^{-N}\cdot\left|\mathrm{R}\left(\mathrm{Ad}(x_{\infty}^{-1})(X^{r})\right)\varphi_{i}(y)\right|\right).

For
x\in\mathfrak{s}^{Q_{H}}
,
x_{\infty}
can be written in the form
x_{\infty}=ac
,
where
a\in A_{0}^{Q}(T_{-})
, and
c
lies in a compact subset of
H(F_{\infty})
. Thus

\mathrm{Ad}(x_{\infty}^{-1})X=\mathrm{Ad}(c^{-1})\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X=\mathrm{Ad}(c^{-1})e^{-\langle\alpha,H_{0}(a)\rangle}X,

where
\alpha
is the only root of
A_{0}
acting on
\mathfrak{n}_{i+1}^{i}
. Pick a basis
E_{i}
of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})^{\leq r}
, the elements in

\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
of degree
\leq r
. If we write

(3.20)

\mathrm{Ad}(x_{\infty}^{-1})X^{r}=\sum_{i}c_{i}(x)E_{i},

then we deduce that
|c_{i}(x)|\leq d_{{P_{H}},\alpha}(x)^{-r}
, since we recall that we have

d_{P_{H},\alpha}(x)\sim e^{\langle\alpha,H_{0}(x)\rangle}.

when
x\in\mathfrak{s}^{P_{H}}
.
The theorem then follows from (
3.18
), (
3.19
) and (
3.20
).
∎

3.9.
A mild extension of Arthur’s truncation operator

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
. Fix a minimal parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. We define a space of functions
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
as

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)=\left\{({}_{P}\varphi)\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{T}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))\mid{}_{P}\varphi-{}_{Q}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q}}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))\text{ for any }P\subset Q\right\}.

This space embeds as a closed subspace of the LF space,

\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{T}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\\
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q}}(P(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})),

and as such inherits an LF topology.

We define
\mathcal{S}^{0}([G]_{P}^{1})
to be the Banach space of measurable functions on
[G]_{P}^{1}
such that for any
N
,

(3.21)

\|f\|_{\infty,N}:=\sup_{x\in[G]_{P}^{1}}\|x\|_{P}^{N}\lvert f(x)\rvert<\infty.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let

\underline{\varphi}=({}_{P}\varphi)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
be a collection of functions. For
g\in[G]_{P}^{1}
and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, define

\Lambda^{T}\underline{\varphi}(g)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash G(F)}\widehat{\tau}_{P}(H_{P}(\gamma g)-T_{P}){}_{P}\varphi(\gamma g).

and

\Pi^{T}\underline{\varphi}(g)=F^{G}(g,T)\cdot{}_{G}\varphi(g).

Then for every
c>0,N>0
, there exists a continuous seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{c,N}
on
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
such that

\|\Lambda^{T}\underline{\varphi}-\Pi^{T}\underline{\varphi}\|_{\infty,N}\leq e^{-c\|T\|}\|\underline{\varphi}\|_{c,N},

for
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
sufficiently positive, where the norm on left hand side is as defined in (
3.21
).

Remark 3.25
.

When
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([G])
and
\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}=\varphi_{P}
for
all
P\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
(\varphi_{Q})_{P}=\varphi_{P}
, and hence the family

\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}
is in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
by the approximation by constant terms for
G
.
In this case
\Lambda^{T}\underline{\varphi}
is Arthur’s truncation operator
defined in
[
2
]
.

Following Arthur
[
1
]
*Section 6, for standard parabolic subgroups
P\subset Q
, we let
\sigma_{P}^{Q}
be the characteristic functions of
H\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
that satisfies the following properties.

•

\langle\alpha,H\rangle>0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{P}^{Q}
.

•

\langle\alpha,H\rangle\leq 0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{P}\setminus\Delta_{P}^{Q}
.

•

\langle\varpi,H\rangle>0
for all
\varpi\in\widehat{\Delta}_{Q}
.

We will need the following lemma in the proof of
Proposition
3.24
.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
F
, then for every sufficiently positive

T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
and every
g\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
with

F^{P}(g,T)\sigma_{P}^{Q}(H_{P}(g)-T_{P})\neq 0

there exists
r>0
such that

\displaystyle e^{\|T\|}

\displaystyle\ll\left(\min_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}d_{P,\alpha}(g)\right)^{r}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\|g\|_{P}

\displaystyle\ll\left(\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}d_{P,\alpha}(g)\right)^{r}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
5
]
*Lemma 3.5.1.2. For the convenience of
readers, we reproduce it here.

We can assume
g\in\mathfrak{s}^{P}
. By
[
6
]
*Lemma 2.3.3.1, for any
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\backslash\Delta_{0}^{P}
, we have
\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)\rangle>\langle\alpha,T\rangle\geq\varepsilon\|T\|
. Therefore

d_{P,\alpha}(g)\sim e^{\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)\rangle}>e^{\varepsilon\|T\|}

for any
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{P}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{Q}
, the first inequality is
thus proved.

By
[
27
]
*Lemme 2.10.6, when
g\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
we have

(3.22)

\|H_{P}(g)-T_{P}\|\ll\|H_{P}^{Q}(g)-T_{P}^{Q}\|\sim 1+\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{P}^{Q}}\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)-T\rangle,

where
H_{P}^{Q}(g)
and
T_{P}^{Q}
stands for projection of
H_{P}(g)
and
T_{P}
into

\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
respectively. The condition
F^{P}(H_{0}(g)-T)\neq 0
implies

(3.23)

\|H^{P}(g)\|\ll\|T\|,

where
H^{P}(g)
stands for the projection of
H_{0}(g)
to
\mathfrak{a}^{P}
. For
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}
, let
\alpha=\alpha_{P}+\alpha^{P}
be
the decomposition of
\alpha
according to
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{P,*}
, then
\alpha_{P}
runs through
\Delta_{P}^{Q}
as
\alpha
runs through

\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}
. Since for any
\beta\in\Delta_{0}^{P}
,

\langle\alpha,\beta^{\vee}\rangle=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\leq 0
.
Thus
\alpha^{P}
is a non-positive linear combination of
\widehat{\Delta}_{0}^{P}
,
hence

(3.24)

\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)-T\rangle=\langle\alpha_{P},H_{0}(g)-T\rangle+\langle\alpha^{P},H_{0}(g)-T\rangle\geq\langle\alpha_{P},H_{0}(g)-T\rangle.

Combining (
3.22
),
(
3.23
) and
(
3.24
), we obtain

(3.25)

\|H_{0}(g)\|\ll 1+\|T\|+\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)\rangle.

Combining with the first assertion, we have

\|H_{0}(g)\|\ll 1+\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)\rangle.

Finally note that
g\in\mathfrak{s}^{P}
, thus for some
r>0
,

\|g\|_{P}\sim e^{\|H_{0}(g)\|}\ll\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}e^{r\langle\alpha,H_{0}(g)\rangle}\sim\left(\max_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}}d_{P,\alpha}\right)^{r}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.24
.

Our proof follows the same line as
[
5
]
*Section 3.5. Using Langlands
partition formula (
3.10
) and

\tau_{P}^{R}\widehat{\tau}_{R}=\sum_{Q\supset R}\sigma_{P}^{Q}

we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{T}\underline{\varphi}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{P\subset R}\epsilon_{R}\times

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in R(F)\backslash G(F)}

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in P(F)\backslash R(F)}\widehat{\tau}_{R}(H_{R}(\gamma g)-T_{R}){}_{R}\varphi(\gamma g)F^{P}(\delta\gamma g,T)\tau_{P}^{R}(H_{P}(\delta\gamma g)-T_{P})

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\subset Q}\sum_{\delta\in P(F)\backslash G(F)}F^{P}(\delta g,T)\sigma_{P}^{Q}(H_{P}(\delta g)-T){}_{P,Q}\varphi(\delta g)

where

{}_{P,Q}\varphi(g)=\sum_{P\subset R\subset Q}\epsilon_{R}\cdot{}_{R}\varphi(g)

Hence

\Lambda^{T}\underline{\varphi}(g)-\Pi^{T}\underline{\varphi}(g)=\sum_{P\subsetneq Q}\sum_{\delta\in P(F)\backslash G(F)}F^{P}(\delta g,T)\sigma_{P}^{Q}(H_{P}(\delta g)-T_{P}){}_{P,Q}\varphi(\delta g).

Since
E_{P}^{G}
is a continuous map from
\mathcal{S}^{0}(P(F)\backslash G(F))
to
\mathcal{S}^{0}([G])
, we only
need to show for every
c>0,N>0
, there exists a continuous seminorm on

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
such that

(3.26)

\left|{}_{P,Q}\varphi(g)\right|\ll e^{-c\|T\|}\|g\|_{P}^{-N}\|\underline{\varphi}\|

holds every
g
with
F^{P}(g,T)\sigma_{P}^{Q}(H_{P}(g)-T_{P})\neq 0
and every

\underline{\varphi}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
.

Fix
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{R}
. For a parabolic subgroup
R
with
P\subset R\subset Q
and
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{R}
, define
R^{\alpha}
such that

\Delta_{0}^{R^{\alpha}}=\Delta_{0}^{R}\setminus\{\alpha\}
. Then there exists

N_{0}>0
such that for any
r>0
, there exists a seminorm
\|\cdot\|
on

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
such that

\left|{}_{P,Q}\varphi(g)\right|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset R\subset Q\\
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{R}\end{subarray}}\left|{}_{R}\varphi(g)-{}_{R^{\alpha}}\varphi(g)\right|\leq\|g\|_{P}^{N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset R\subset Q\\
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{R}\end{subarray}}d_{R^{\alpha},\alpha}(g)^{-r}\|\underline{\varphi}\|.

By first assertions of Lemma
3.26
,
g\in\omega_{P}^{Q}[>C]\subset\omega_{P}^{R^{\alpha}}[>C^{\prime}]
, hence

d_{R^{\alpha},\alpha}(g)\sim d_{P,\alpha}(g)
. Hence

\left|{}_{P,Q}\varphi(g)\right|\ll\|g\|_{P}^{N_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset R\subset Q\\
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}^{R}\end{subarray}}d_{P,\alpha}(g)^{-r}\|\underline{\varphi}\|.

Finally (
3.26
) follows if we let
\alpha

vary and use the second assertion of Lemma
3.26
, .
∎

3.10.
Decomposition of Schwartz functions

We begin by some general considerations. Let
G
be an algebraic group over
F

with a morphism
p:G\to\mathbf{A}^{m}_{F}
. Here
\mathbf{A}^{m}_{F}

stands for the
m
-dimensional affine space over

F
, whose
F
-points will also be noted by
F^{m}
. Let
\Lambda\subset F_{\infty}^{m}
be a full lattice (i.e. discrete finitely generated abelian group
which generate
F_{\infty}^{m}
as an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space).

Let
U\subset F^{m}_{\infty}
be a neighbourhood of
0
, such that
U\cap\Lambda=\{0\}
, choose any
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(F^{m}_{\infty})
such that

\supp u\subset U
and
u(0)=1
. For any
\alpha\in\Lambda
, define a
function
u_{\alpha}
on
G(\mathbb{A})
by
u_{\alpha}(g)=u(p(g_{\infty})-\alpha)
, and

f_{\alpha}:=f\cdot u_{\alpha}
. For
\beta\in\Lambda
, and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})

with
p(g)=\beta
, we have

(3.27)

f_{\alpha}(g)=\begin{cases}f(g)&\beta=\alpha\\
0&\beta\neq\alpha\end{cases}

Proposition 3.27
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
, the sequence of functions
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is absolutely summable in
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
.

Proof.

The space
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
is a union of Fréchet spaces
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,K)
(cf. Subsection
3.3
with
Z
being trivial), and if
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,K)
,
then
f_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,K)
for all
\alpha
. It suffices to prove that for all
N>0
and
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\|f_{\alpha}\|_{X,N}<\infty.

(c.f. Remark
3.13
).

By the Leibniz rule,

Xf_{\alpha}=\sum_{Y,Y^{\prime}}c_{Y,Y^{\prime}}\cdot Yu_{\alpha}\cdot Y^{\prime}f

for some universal constants
c_{Y,Y^{\prime}}
, thus it suffices to show for any

Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\|g\|^{N}\left|Yu_{\alpha}(g)\right|\left|Y^{\prime}f(g)\right|<\infty.

By the chain rule,

Yu_{\alpha}(g)=\sum_{Z}c_{Z}\cdot(Zu)(p(g_{\infty})-\alpha)\cdot p_{Z}(g_{\infty})

for some
Z\in\mathcal{U}(g)
, where
c_{Z}
are constants and
p_{Z}
are polynomials.
Choose
N_{1}
such that
\left|p_{Z}(g_{\infty})\right|\ll\|g_{\infty}\|^{N_{1}}\ll\|g\|^{N_{1}}
holds for all
Z
. Then it suffices to show for any
Y^{\prime},Z\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\|g\|^{N+N_{1}}\left|Zu(p(g_{\infty})-\alpha)\right|\left|Y^{\prime}f(g)\right|<\infty.

Choose an norm
\|\cdot\|
on
F_{\infty}^{n}
. There exists
C
such that

u(x-\alpha)\neq 0
implies
\|x\|\geq C\|\alpha\|
,
by
[
25
]
*Proposition 18.1 (1) there exists
N_{2}
such that

Zu(p(g_{\infty})-\alpha)\neq 0
implies
\|g_{\infty}\|^{N_{2}}\gg\|\alpha\|
. Since
f
is Schwartz, for any
M>0

\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\left|Y^{\prime}f(g)\right|\|g\|^{N_{1}+N}\ll\|g\|^{-M}\ll\|g_{\infty}\|^{-M}.

Combining these, for any
N^{\prime}>0

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\|g\|^{N+N_{1}}\left|Zu(p(g_{\infty})-\alpha)\right|\left|Y^{\prime}f(g)\right|\ll\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\|\alpha\|^{-N^{\prime}},

which is finite for
N^{\prime}
large enough.
∎

4.
Jacobi groups

4.1.
Jacobi groups: general linear groups

Put
L=E^{n}
(column vectors) and
L^{\vee}=E_{n}
(row vectors). The usual dot product gives a pairing between
L
and
L^{\vee}
. If
W
is a linear
subspace of
L
we will write
W^{\vee}=\{\prescript{t}{}{x}\in L^{\vee}\mid x\in W\}
and
W^{\perp}=\{y\in L^{\vee}\mid xy=0\text{ for all }x\in W\}
. Denote by

(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
the standard basis of
E^{n}
, and
(\prescript{t}{}{e_{1}},\ldots,\prescript{t}{}{e_{n}})

its dual basis. We always consider
G_{n}
as the subgroup of
G_{n+1}

acting trivially on
e_{n+1}
the last vector of the canonical basis of
E^{n+1}
.

Let
\widetilde{S}=L^{\vee}\times L\times E
be the Heisenberg group over
E
whose
multiplication is given by

(u,v,t)\cdot(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\left(u+u^{\prime},v+v^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}+\frac{uv^{\prime}-u^{\prime}v}{2}\right),

The center of
\widetilde{S}
consists of elements of the form
(0,0,t)
,
t\in E
and
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a,E}
. Put
S=\Res_{E/F}\widetilde{S}
.

The group
G_{n}
acts on the left on
S
as group automorphism by
g\cdot(u,v,t)=(ug^{-1},gv,t)
. We define the Jacobi group to be the semi-direct
product

J_{n}=S\rtimes G_{n}

The center of
J_{n}
is
Z(J_{n})=Z(S)\rtimes\{1\}\subset J_{n}
which is
isomorphic to
\Res_{E/F}\mathbb{G}_{a}
. We denote by
Z
for both centers of
S
and
J_{n}
.

Remark 4.1
.

The group
J_{n}
is not exactly what is usually called a Jacobi group in the literature, but rather the restriction of scalars of them.

The group
J_{n}
satisfies the condition (
SR
), therefore we can
speak of its standard D-parabolic subgroups as in
Subsection
3.1
. Here we recall that we have the upper
triangular minimal parabolic subgroup
B_{n}
of
G_{n}
and standard D-parabolic subgroups
of
J_{n}
are those that contains
B_{n}
. We denote by
\mathcal{F}
the set of standard
D-parabolic subgroups of
J_{n}
.

We now give an explicit description of
\mathcal{F}
. For
P\in\mathcal{F}
, we put
P_{n}=P\cap G_{n}
and assume that
P_{n}
is the stabilizer of the flag

(4.1)

0=L_{0}\subset L_{1}\subset\cdots\subset L_{r}=L.

Then
P
is either of the form

(4.2)

P=(L_{k}^{\perp}\times L_{k}\times E)\rtimes P_{n}

for some
0\leq k\leq r
, in which case we call
P
of type I, or of the form

(4.3)

P=(L_{k}^{\perp}\times L_{k+1}\times E)\rtimes P_{n}

for
0\leq k\leq r-1
, in which case we call
P
of type II. If
P
is of type I,
the D-Levi decomposition for
P
is

M_{P}=(0\times 0\times E)\rtimes M_{P_{n}},\quad N_{P}=(L_{k}^{\perp}\times L_{k}\times 0)\rtimes N_{P_{n}}.

If
P
is of type II, the D-Levi decomposition for
P
is

M_{P}=(L_{k}^{\perp}/L_{k+1}^{\perp}\times L_{k+1}/L_{k}\times E)\rtimes M_{P_{n}},\quad N_{P}=(L_{k+1}^{\perp}\times L_{k}\times 0)\rtimes N_{P_{n}}.

It is readily checked that
P
is of type II if and only if
P=P(\lambda)
with
some component of
\lambda:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to T
being the trivial character, otherwise
it is of type I.

We also define the Heisenberg part of these groups. For
X\in\{P,M_{P},N_{P}\}

put

X_{S}=X\cap S,\quad X_{L^{\vee}}=X\cap L^{\vee},\quad X_{L}=X\cap L.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}
be the set of Rankin-Selberg parabolic subgroups of

G_{n+1}\times G_{n}
introduced in
[
5
]
*Section 3.1. The set

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}
consists of semistandard parabolic subgroups
P_{n+1}\times P_{n}
of
G_{n+1}\times G_{n}
, such that
P_{n}=P_{n+1}\cap G_{n}
and

P_{n}
is standard. Let
P\in\mathcal{F}
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
J_{n}
. We construct
a Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup
P_{n+1}\times P_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}

as follows. First let
P_{n}=P\cap G_{n}
. Assume that
P_{n}
stabilizes the
flag (
4.1
). If
P
is of type I and is of the
form (
4.2
), then let
P_{n+1}
be
the parabolic subgroup of
G_{n+1}
stabilizing the flag

(4.4)

0=L_{0}\subset\cdots\subset L_{k}\subset L_{k}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{n+1})\subset\cdots\subset L_{r}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{n+1}).

If
P
is of type II and is of the form (
4.3
),
then let
P_{n+1}
be the parabolic subgroup of
G_{n+1}

stabilizing the flag

(4.5)

0=L_{0}\subset\cdots\subset L_{k}\subset L_{k+1}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{n+1})\subset\cdots\subset L_{r}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{n+1}).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let the notation be as above. The map
P\mapsto P_{n+1}\times P_{n}
is a bijection
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}
. Moreover, for any
P\in\mathcal{F}
, the map

\Psi_{P_{n+1}}\to\Psi_{P},\quad\alpha\mapsto\alpha|_{A_{n}}

is a bijection. Here we recall that
\Psi_{P}
(resp.
\Psi_{P_{n+1}}
)
stands for the roots of
A_{n}
(resp.
A_{n+1}
) on
\mathfrak{n}_{P}
(resp.
\mathfrak{n}_{P_{n+1}}
).

Proof.

This is clear from the above construction.
∎

Let
P=MN\in\mathcal{F}
. The subgroup
P(F)

or
M(F)N(\mathbb{A})
of
J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
satisfies the condition (SL) in
Subsection
3.3
, and hence it makes sense to speak of the various spaces of functions on
P(F)\backslash J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
or
[J_{n}]_{P}
(with the character
\psi
) defined there. As mentioned in Subsection
3.3.3
, we need to verify that in this case, for any compact subgroup
U\subset J_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there exists an open compact subset
C\subset J_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such
that the support of any
\phi\in C^{\infty}(P(F)\backslash J_{n}(\mathbb{A}),\psi_{E})^{U}
is contained in

\Gamma(C\times J_{n}(F_{\infty}))
. We will see in the argument
that this crucially relies on the fact that
\psi_{E}
is nontrivial.

Since
N(F)\backslash N(\mathbb{A})
is
compact, it is enough to assume that
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M(F)N(\mathbb{A})\backslash J_{n}(\mathbb{A}),\psi)^{U}
. For simplicity of notation, we assume that
P
is of
type I. The type II case can be treated in the same way. Assume that

L_{k}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{a}),\quad L_{k}^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(\prescript{t}{}{e_{1}},\ldots,\prescript{t}{}{e_{a}}).

We view them as subgroups of
S
. We write an element in
J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
as

(u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty}

where
g_{f}\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
\widetilde{g}_{\infty}\in J_{n}(F_{\infty})
,

u\in L_{k}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
v\in L_{k}^{\vee,\perp}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,

u^{\vee}\in L_{k}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
v^{\vee}\in L_{k}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. First we know that there is a compact subset
C_{1}
of

G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
such that

P_{n}(F)(C_{1}\times G_{n}(F_{\infty}))=G_{n}(\mathbb{A}).

Thus we need to explain that for any open compact subgroup
U
of
J_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
there are compact subsets
C_{2}\subset L_{k}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
C_{3}\subset L_{k}^{\vee,\perp}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such that if

\phi\in C^{\infty}(M(F)N(\mathbb{A})\backslash J_{n}(\mathbb{A}),\psi_{E})^{U}
and

\phi((u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty})\not=0,

then
u^{\vee}\in C_{2}
and
v\in C_{3}
. This can be seen as follows. First we may assume
that
g_{f}\in C_{1}
. Then consider

\bigcap_{g_{f}\in C_{1}}g_{f}Ug_{f}^{-1}.

Since
U
is an open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
C_{1}
is compact, this is
essentially a finite intersection and hence an open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. It follows that

U^{\prime}=\bigcap_{g_{f}\in C_{1}}g_{f}Ug_{f}^{-1}\cap N_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})

is a nontrivial open compact subgroup of
N_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.
We pick
(x,y,0)
in
U^{\prime}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\phi((u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\phi((x,y,0)(u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\psi(u^{\vee}x+yv)\phi((u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)(x,y,0)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\phi((u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty}).

Here the first equality is because
\phi
is left
N_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})
-invariant, the second is
because
\phi
is
\psi
-invariant by the central element, and the third is because

g_{f}^{-1}(x,y,0)g_{f}\in U
be our choices. It follows that if

\phi((u+v,u^{\vee}+v^{\vee},t)g_{f}\widetilde{g}_{\infty})\not=0
then
\psi(u^{\vee}x+yv)=1
.
As
(x,y,0)
varies in the group
U^{\prime}
, we conclude that
u^{\vee}
and
v
should lie
in an open compact subgroup of
L_{k}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
L_{k}^{\vee,\perp}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
which only depends
on
U^{\prime}
. Note that this is where the fact that
\psi
is nontrivial is used.
This proves that
M(F)N(\mathbb{A})
, and hence
P(F)
satisfy the condition (SL).

For
P\in\mathcal{F}
, the embedding
G_{n}\hookrightarrow G_{n+1}

induces an embedding

[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}\hookrightarrow[G_{n+1}]_{P_{n+1}}.

We can then restrict any weight function on
[G_{n+1}]_{P_{n+1}}
to obtain a
weight function on
[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}
.
The embedding also induces an embedding
A_{n}^{\infty}\to A_{n+1}^{\infty}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
, we
have
d_{P}^{Q}\sim d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}|_{[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}}
. Here
d_{P}^{Q}
is the weight
on
[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}
defined in (
3.15
).

Proof.

It is enough to prove that

d_{P}^{Q}(a)\sim d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(a),\quad a\in A_{n}^{\infty}.

By the definitions of
d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}
and
d_{P}^{Q}
(see also Remark
4.4
after this proof),
for all
a\in A_{n}^{\infty}
we have

d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(a)\sim\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}}\alpha(a),\quad d_{P}^{Q}(a)\sim\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}\alpha(a).

The lemma then follows from Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

We temporarily denote by
G
a reductive group with a fixed minimal parabolic subgroup

P_{0}=M_{0}N_{0}
and a maximal split torus
A_{0}\subset M_{0}
.
Let
P,Q
be two standard parabolic subgroups. In general, by definition we have

d_{P,\alpha}(x)\sim e^{\langle\alpha,H_{0}(x)\rangle},\quad x\in\mathfrak{s}^{P},

and

d_{P}^{Q}(x)=\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}d_{P,\alpha}(x),\quad x\in[G]_{P}.

Put
W^{P}=\mathrm{Norm}_{M_{P}(F)}(A_{0})/M_{0}(F)
and let
\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}

be a
W^{P}
-invariant subset. Then for all
a\in A_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

(4.6)

\min_{\lambda\in\Lambda}d_{P,\lambda}(a)\sim\min_{\lambda\in\Lambda}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{0}(a)\rangle}.

So in particular, for all
a\in A_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

(4.7)

d_{P}^{Q}(a)\sim\min_{\alpha\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}}e^{\langle\alpha,H_{0}(a)\rangle}

As a consequence, (
4.7
) holds for all semi-standard
P,Q
.

4.2.
Theta functions: general linear groups

We now introduce theta functions on
J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
. Recall that we have
fixed a character
\mu:E^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
whose
restriction to
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
equals
\eta
. Define an action of
J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
on

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
by

(4.8)

\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}((u,v,t)g)\Phi(x)=\mu(\det g)^{-1}\lvert\det g\rvert_{E}^{1/2}\Phi((x+u)g)\psi_{E}\left(xv+\frac{uv}{2}+t\right)

This is an SLF-representation of
J_{n}(\mathbb{A})
.

For
P=MN
a standard D-parabolic subgroup of
J_{n}
, we define the theta
series
\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi)
on
[J_{n}]_{P}
as

(4.9)

\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(j,\Phi)=\int_{N_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})}\sum_{m\in M_{L^{\vee}}(F)}\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(j)\Phi(m+n)dn,\quad j\in[J_{n}]_{P}.

When
P=J_{n}
, we omit the left subscript and write simply
\Theta
. If
g\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
, we have

\Theta(g,\Phi)=\mu(\det g)^{-1}\lvert\det g\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{x\in E_{n}}\Phi(xg),

This is closely related to the mirabolic Eisenstein series, cf.
[
23
]
*Section 4.1, which
differs essentially by a term corresponding to
x=0
and an integral along the center.

Lemma 4.5
.

There is an
N>0
such that

\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([J_{n}]_{P},\psi_{E})
for
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{n})
.

Proof.

The fact that
\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi)
is invariant under

M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})
and transform by
\psi_{E}
under multiplication by
Z
is
a direct calculation.

We next show that
\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}
is of uniform moderate growth.
Recall that a height function
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is fixed on
\mathbb{A}_{E,n}

by (
3.3
). By
[
8
]
*Proposition A.1.1 (v)
we can pick a large
N_{1}
such that

\int_{N_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})}\sum_{m\in M_{L^{\vee}}(F)}\lVert mn\rVert^{-N_{1}}\mathrm{d}n

is convergent. For any
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
and
N_{1}>0
, we put

\lVert\Phi\rVert_{N_{1}}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{A}_{E,n}}\lVert x\rVert^{N_{1}}\lvert\Phi(x)\rvert

Then we are reduced to showing that we can find an
N_{2}
such that
for all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{j}_{\infty})
(where
\mathfrak{j}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Lie}(J_{n}(F_{\infty})))
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
we have

\sup_{j\in J_{n}(\mathbb{A})}\lVert j\rVert^{-N_{2}}\lVert\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(j)(\mathrm{R}(X)\Phi)\rVert_{N_{1}}<\infty.

This can be checked directly from the definition (
4.8
)
the action
\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

\int_{[N_{P_{S}}]}\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(nj,\Phi)\mathrm{d}n=\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(j,\Phi),\quad\sum_{\gamma\in N_{P_{S}}(F)\backslash N_{Q_{S}}(F)}\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\gamma j,\Phi)=\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta(j,\Phi)}.

Proof.

This is a direct calculation using (
4.8
).
∎

4.3.
Jacobi groups: unitary groups

Let
(V,q_{V})
be a nondegenerate
n
-dimensional skew-Hermitian vector
space over
E
and
\U(V)
the corresponding unitary group.

Let
\Res V
be the symplectic space over
F
whose underline vector space is

V
, and the symplectic form
\mathrm{Tr}_{E/F}\circ q_{V}
.
Let
S(V)=\Res V\times F
be the Heisenberg group, where the
multiplication is given by

(v_{1},t_{1})\cdot(v_{2},t_{2})=\left(v_{1}+v_{2},t_{1}+t_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(v_{1},v_{2})\right).

The group
\U(V)
acts on
S(V)
by
x\cdot(v,t)=(xv,t)
for
x\in\U(V)

and
(v,t)\in S(V)
. Define the Jacobi group to be the semi-direct product

J(V)=S(V)\rtimes\U(V).

The center of
S(V)
and
J(V)
are both isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, we use
Z
to
denote either of them.

Let
m
be the Witt index of
V
, and
V_{\mathrm{an}}
be an anisotropic
kernel of
V
. Choose a basis

e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},e_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{m}^{\vee}

of the orthogonal complement of
V_{\mathrm{an}}
, such that

q_{V}(e_{i},e_{j})=q_{V}(e_{i}^{\vee},e_{j}^{\vee})=0,\quad q_{V}(e_{i},e_{j}^{\vee})=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq m.

Let
P_{0}
be the minimal parabolic subgroup of
\U(V)
stabilizing the
flag

(4.10)

0\subset\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1})\subset\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},e_{2})\subset\cdots\subset\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\cdots,e_{m}).

Let
A_{0}
be the maximal split torus contained in
P_{0}
. As in the
general linear group case, the Jacobi group
J(V)
satisfies the
condition (
SR
), hence we can speak of the standard D-parabolic
subgroups of
J(V)
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{V}
be the set of standard D-parabolic subgroups
of
J(V)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{V}
be the set of standard parabolic subgroups of
\U(V)
.
If
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, then we put
P^{\prime}=P\cap\U(V)\in\mathcal{F}_{V}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The map

\mathcal{F}_{V}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{V},\quad P\mapsto P^{\prime}

is a bijection.

Proof.

By definition,
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{V}
is standard parabolic subgroup. Assume that
it stabilizes an isotropic flag of the form

(4.11)

0=X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset\cdots\subset X_{r}

in
V
. Then there is a unique
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
such that
P\mapsto P^{\prime}

given by
P=(X_{r}^{\perp}\times F)\rtimes P^{\prime}
.
∎

Assume that
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
P^{\prime}=P\cap\U(V)
stabilizes the isotropic
flag (
4.11
). Assume that
X_{r}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{a_{r}})
. We put

X_{r}^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{a_{r}}^{\vee}),\quad W_{r}=(X_{r}+X_{r}^{\vee})^{\perp}.

Then the D-Levi decomposition for
P
is given by

M_{P}=(W_{r}\times F)\rtimes M_{P^{\prime}},\quad N_{P}=V_{r}\rtimes N_{P^{\prime}}

In particular,
M_{P}
is a product of general linear groups and of the Jacobi group
attached to the skew-hermitian space
W_{r}
.

We also define the Heisenberg part of these groups. For
X\in\{P,M_{P},N_{P}\}

put

X_{S}=X\cap S(V),\quad X_{V}=X\cap V.

Then we have

P_{V}=X_{r}+W_{r},\quad M_{P_{V}}=W_{r},\quad N_{P_{V}}=X_{r}.

Recall that we have defined weights
d_{P}^{Q}
and
d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}

at the end of Subsection
3.1
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
with
P\subset Q
, then as weights on

[\U(V)]_{P^{\prime}}
, we have

\min\{d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}},\,(d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\}\ll d_{P}^{Q}\ll d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.21
we have

d_{P}^{Q}\ll d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}
, which gives the second inequality.

Assume that
P^{\prime}
stabilizes the flag (
4.11
), and
that
Q^{\prime}
stabilizes another flag

0=X_{0}\subset X_{k_{1}}\cdots\subset X_{k_{s}},

with
1<k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq r
, obtained by deleting some of the terms in the
flag (
4.11
). If
X_{k_{s}}=X_{r}
,
then one checks that
\mathfrak{n}_{P}^{Q}=\mathfrak{n}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}
, and hence the
lemma holds automatically. Assume that
X_{k_{s}}\not=X_{r}
, and write

V_{k_{s}}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{a_{s}}),\quad V_{r}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{a_{r}}).

Then
\mathfrak{n}_{P}^{Q}=\mathfrak{n}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{a_{s}+1},\ldots,e_{a_{r}})
.
For
i=a_{s}+1,\ldots,a_{r}
, let
\lambda_{i}\in\Psi_{P}^{Q}

be the root that appears in
\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{i})\subset\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{a_{s}+1},\ldots,e_{a_{r}})
. Then
we observe that
2\lambda_{i}\in\Psi_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}
for all
i
.
It follows that we have

e^{\langle\lambda_{i},H_{0}(x)\rangle}=\left(e^{\langle 2\lambda_{i},H_{0}(x)\rangle}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\gg d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(x)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad x\in\mathfrak{s}^{P^{\prime}}.

Therefore for
x\in\mathfrak{s}^{P^{\prime}}
we have

d_{P}^{Q}(x)\sim\min\{d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(x),\min_{a_{s}+1\leq i\leq a_{r}}e^{\langle\lambda_{i},H_{0}(x)\rangle}\}\gg\min\{d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(x),d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\}.

This gives the first inequality and concludes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Theta functions: unitary groups

Let
V
be a nondegenerate
n
-dimensional skew-Hermitian space. Let
S(V)
be the associated Heisenberg group. Fix a polarization
\Res V=L\oplus L^{\vee}
,
i.e.
L
and
L^{\vee}
are maximal isotropic subspaces of
\Res V
such that the
pairing
\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}
is nondegenerate when restricted to
L\times L^{\vee}
. We
denote by
\rho=\rho_{\psi}
the oscillator representation of
S(V)(\mathbb{A})

on
\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. For
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})),
it is characterized by

(4.12)

\rho((l+l^{\prime},z))\phi(x)=\psi\left(z+(x,l)+\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(l^{\prime},l)\right)\phi(x+l^{\prime}),\quad l\in L(\mathbb{A}),\quad x,l^{\prime}\in L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}).

A different choice of the polarization gives another model, and the isomorphism
between the two models are given by a partial Fourier transform,
cf.
[
33
]
*Chapitre 2, I. 7.

Let
\Mp(\Res V)(\mathbb{A})
be the metaplectic group
attached to
\Res V
, which sits in a central extension

1\to\mathbb{C}^{1}\to\Mp(\Res V)(\mathbb{A})\to\Sp(\Res V)(\mathbb{A})\to 1,

where
\mathbb{C}^{1}
stands for the complex numbers of norm
1
.
Note that even though we use this notation,
\Mp(\Res V)
is not an algebraic
group, and indeed does not make sense on its own. A theorem of Weil implies
that the oscillating representation canonically extends to a representation
of the group

S(V)(\mathbb{A})\rtimes\Mp(\Res V)(\mathbb{A}).

By definition there is an embedding
\U(V)\to\Sp(\Res V)
. Recall that we
have fixed a character
\mu:E^{\times}\to\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}
extending the
quadratic character
\eta
. Given such a
\mu
, there is an explicit lift of
this embedding
\iota_{\mu}:\U(V)(\mathbb{A})\to\Mp(\Res V(\mathbb{A}))
, cf.
[
26
]
.
In this way we obtain a representation of
J(V)(\mathbb{A})
, realized on

\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. This representation depends on two characters
\psi
and

\mu
, and we denoted it by
\omega_{\psi,\mu}
, or simply
\omega
when the
characters are clear from the context.

We define the theta function

(4.13)

\theta_{\psi,\mu}(j,\phi)=\sum_{x\in L^{\vee}(F)}\omega_{\psi,\mu}(j)\phi(x),\quad j\in J(V)(\mathbb{A}),\quad\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})).

As the different models are related by partial Fourier transforms, the Poisson summation formula ensures that the theta function is independent of the choice of polarization.

For our purposes, it is more convenient to choose a specific polarization
V=L\oplus L^{\vee}
and interpret the model

\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
as a mixed model,
where the actions of parabolic subgroups are transparent. We follow the exposition
in
[
16
]
. Though only local non-Archimedean cases were considered in
[
16
]
, the
formulae listed there are also valid in the Archimedean case, and taking product
gives the formulae in the global situation. Recall
that we have fixed a minimal parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
of
\U(V)

stabilizing the maximal isotropic flag (
4.10
). We fix a polarization
\Res V_{\mathrm{an}}=L_{\mathrm{an}}\oplus L_{\mathrm{an}}^{\vee}
. Put

L=L_{\mathrm{an}}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}),\quad L^{\vee}=L_{\mathrm{an}}^{\vee}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{m}^{\vee}).

Then
\Res V=L\oplus L^{\vee}
is a polarization of
\Res V
.

Let
1\leq k\leq m
be a fixed integer. We put

\displaystyle X=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}),\quad X^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{k}^{\vee}),

\displaystyle Y=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{k+1},\ldots,e_{m}),\quad Y^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}_{E}(e_{k+1}^{\vee},\ldots,e_{m}^{\vee}).

Set
V_{0}=Y\oplus V_{\mathrm{an}}\oplus Y^{\vee}
. We have a decomposition
V=X\oplus V_{0}\oplus X^{\vee}
. We view
X,X^{\vee},Y,Y^{\vee}

as
F
-subspaces of
\Res V
. Put

L_{0}=L_{\mathrm{an}}\oplus Y,\quad L_{0}^{\vee}=L_{\mathrm{an}}^{\vee}\oplus Y^{\vee}.

Then
\Res V_{0}=L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\vee}
is a polarization of
\Res V_{0}
.

We denote by
\omega_{0}
the Weil representation of
J(V_{0})
, realized on

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{S}(L_{0}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. Via the canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\simeq\mathcal{S}(X^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimeshat\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we view elements in

\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
as Schwartz functions on
X^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})

valued in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. We now describe the action. Let
P^{\prime}=MN\subset\U(V)

be the maximal parabolic subgroup stabilizing the isotropic subspace
X
in

V
. We write elements in
P
as
m_{P}(a)g_{0}n_{P}(b)n_{P}(c)
where
g_{0}\in\U(V_{0})
,
a\in\GL(X)
,
b\in\Hom(V_{0},X)
,
c\in\mathsf{Herm}(X^{\vee},X)
, and

m_{P}(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
&\id_{V_{0}}\\
&&(a^{*})^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\quad n_{P}(b)=\begin{pmatrix}\id_{X}&b&-\frac{1}{2}bb^{*}\\
&\id_{V_{0}}&-b^{*}\\
&&\id_{X^{\vee}}\end{pmatrix},\quad n_{P}(c)=\begin{pmatrix}\id_{X}&0&c\\
&\id_{V_{0}}&0\\
&&\id_{X^{\vee}}\end{pmatrix},

and

\mathsf{Herm}(X^{\vee},X)=\{c\in\Hom(X^{\vee},X)\mid c^{*}=-c\}.

Here
a^{*}\in\GL(X^{\vee})
,
b^{*}\in\Hom(X^{\vee},V_{0})
and
c^{*}\in\Hom(X^{\vee},X)
are defined by

q_{V}(ax,x^{\vee})=q_{V}(x,a^{*}x^{\vee}),\quad q_{V}(bv,x^{\vee})=q_{V}(v,b^{*}x^{\vee}),\quad q_{V}(cx^{\vee},y^{\vee})=q_{V}(x^{\vee},c^{*}y^{\vee}),

for
v\in V_{0}
,
x\in X
and
x^{\vee},y^{\vee}\in X^{\vee}
. We list
the actions of various elements in
J(V)
on
\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. Take
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
and
x\in X^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})
. The action of element in
P

are given as follows.

(4.14)

\displaystyle\omega(g_{0})\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(g_{0})(\phi(x)),

(4.15)

\displaystyle\omega(m_{P}(a))\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\mu(\det a)\lvert\det a\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(a^{*}x),

(4.16)

\displaystyle\omega(n_{P}(b))\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}((b^{*}x,0))(\phi(x)),

(4.17)

\displaystyle\omega(n_{P}(c))\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\psi(\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(cx,x))(\phi(x)).

Define an isomorphism
I_{X}:X^{\vee}\to X
by
I_{X}(e_{i}^{\vee})=e_{i}
, and put

w_{P}=\begin{pmatrix}&&-I_{X}\\
&\id_{V_{0}}\\
I_{X}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\U(V).

Then

(4.18)

\omega(w_{P})\phi(x)=\int_{X(\mathbb{A})}\phi(-I_{X}^{-1}y)\psi(\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(y,x))\mathrm{d}y.

Finally elements in
S(V)
acts as follows. For
u\in X
,
u^{\vee}\in X^{\vee}
,
v_{0}\in V_{0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(X^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
we have

(4.19)

\omega((u+v_{0}+u^{\vee},0))\phi(x)=\psi(\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(x,u)+\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(u^{\vee},u))\omega_{0}((v_{0},0))(\phi(x+u^{\vee})),

We write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{an}}=\mathcal{S}(L_{\mathrm{an}}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
for a model of the representation

\omega_{\mathrm{an}}
of
J(V_{\mathrm{an}})(\mathbb{A})
.
In a similar fashion, via the canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{S}(L_{0}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\simeq\mathcal{S}(X_{0}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimeshat\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{an}}
,
we may interpret elements in
\mathcal{S}(L_{0}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
as Schwartz functions on
X_{0}(\mathbb{A})

valued in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{an}}
and
\mathcal{S}(L_{0}^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
as a mixed model.

We now define theta functions in general. Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
be a standard parabolic
subgroup of
J(V)
and let
X\in\{P,M_{P},N_{P}\}
. We set

X_{L}=X\cap L,\quad X_{L^{\vee}}=X\cap L^{\vee}.

We define

\prescript{}{P}{\theta(j,\phi)}=\sum_{x\in M_{P_{L^{\vee}}}(F)}\omega(j)\phi(x),\quad j\in J(V)(\mathbb{A}).

Proposition 4.9
.

For any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
, we have

\prescript{}{P}{\theta}(\cdot,\phi)\in\mathcal{T}([J(V)]_{P},\psi)
. Moreover
for any
j\in J(\mathbb{A})
, we have

\int_{[N_{P_{V}}]}\theta(nj,\phi)\mathrm{d}n={}_{P}\theta(j,\phi).

In particular, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\U(V)])
, the constant
term of the function
\varphi(\cdot)\theta(\cdot,\phi)
on
[J(V)]

along
P
equals
\varphi_{P^{\prime}}(\cdot)\prescript{}{P}{\theta}(\cdot,\phi)
.

Proof.

The invariance of the theta function by
M(F)N(\mathbb{A})
, and the
constant term calculation can be checked directly using mixed models.
That it is of uniformly moderate growth can be proved in exactly the same way
as Lemma
4.5
.
∎

For
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, we also put

(4.20)

{}_{P}\theta^{\vee}(j,\phi)=\sum_{x\in M_{P_{L^{\vee}}}(F)}\omega^{\vee}(j)\phi(x).

Then
{}_{P}\theta^{\vee}\in\mathcal{T}([J]_{P},\psi^{-1})
and exhibits similar properties as in Proposition
4.9
. We have

{}_{P}\theta^{\vee}(j,\phi)=\overline{{}_{P}\theta(j,\overline{\phi})}.

5.
The coarse spectral expansion: general linear groups

5.1.
Notation

We first list notation that will be used throughout this section.

•

Put
L=E^{n}
and
L^{\vee}=E_{n}
.

•

Put
G=G_{n}\times G_{n}
,
G^{\prime}=G^{\prime}_{n}\times G^{\prime}_{n}
, and

H=G_{n}
which embeds in
G
diagonally.
If
g\in G
or
G^{\prime}
, we always write
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
where
g_{i}\in G_{n}
or
G_{n}^{\prime}
. We view
G
as a subgroup of

G_{n+1}\times G_{n}
via the embedding
G_{n}\to G_{n+1}
.

•

We write (see Subsection
3.5
)
K_{G}
,
K_{H}
and
K_{G^{\prime}}
for the standard maximal compact subgroups of
G(\mathbb{A})
,
H(\mathbb{A})
and
G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
respectively.

•

Define two characters of
G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
by

(5.1)

\eta_{n+1}(g_{1},g_{2})=\eta(\det g_{1}g_{2})^{n+1},\quad\eta_{G^{\prime}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\eta(\det g_{1})^{n}\eta(\det g_{2})^{n+1}.

•

For
k=n,n+1
, we have the subgroups
B_{k},T_{k},A_{k}
of
G_{k}
, and subgroups

B_{k}^{\prime},T_{k}^{\prime}
of
G_{k}^{\prime}
, cf. Subsection
2.3
.

•

For
k=n,n+1
, put
\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{B_{k}}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{a}_{B_{k}^{\prime}}
.
A truncation parameter
T
is an element in
\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}^{\prime}
.

•

Recall that we define the Jacobi group
J_{n}=S\rtimes G_{n}
and we put

\widetilde{G}=J_{n}\times G_{n}
. There is a natural embedding

J_{n}\to\widetilde{G}
which is identity on the first coordinate and the
natural projection on the second coordinate. The image is denoted by

\widetilde{H}
.

•

Recall the convention that if
Q
is a D-parabolic subgroup, without saying
the contrary, we write
Q=M_{Q}N_{Q}
for its D-Levi decomposition.

•

Recall that
\mathcal{F}
is the set of standard D-parabolic subgroup of
J_{n}
.
Let

P\mapsto P_{n+1}\times P_{n}

be the bijection defined in Lemma
4.2
, where

P_{k}
is a semistandard parabolic subgroup of
G_{k}
,
k=n,n+1
, and

P_{n}=P_{n+1}\cap G_{n}
is standard.

•

If
P\in\mathcal{F}
, we put

\widetilde{P}=P\times P_{n},\quad P_{G}=\widetilde{P}\cap G=P_{n}\times P_{n},\quad P_{H}=\widetilde{P}\cap H.

and

P_{G^{\prime}}=\widetilde{P}\cap G^{\prime},\quad P_{n}^{\prime}=P_{n}\cap G_{n}^{\prime},\quad P^{\prime}_{n+1}=P_{n+1}\cap G_{n+1}^{\prime}.

They are parabolic subgroups of
\widetilde{G},G,H,G^{\prime},G_{n}^{\prime},G_{n+1}^{\prime}

respectively. We also put
M_{G}=M_{n}\times M_{n}
and
N_{G}=N_{n}\times N_{n}

and hence
P_{G}=M_{G}N_{G}
is a Levi decomposition.

•

Put
G_{+}=G\times E_{n}
. This is viewed as a group over
F
, with the
groups structure given by simply the product of the group
G
and the
additive group
E_{n}
. Note that this is not a subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
, but
merely a subvariety of it. For
P\in\mathcal{F}
, we
define
M_{P,+}=M_{G}\times M_{L^{\vee}}
,
N_{P,+}=N_{G}\times N_{L^{\vee}}
,
and
P_{+}=P_{G}\times P_{L^{\vee}}
, where
M_{L^{\vee}},N_{L^{\vee}}
and

P_{L^{\vee}}
are defined in
Subsection
4.1
. An element in
P_{+}
is often written as

(x,u)
where
x\in P_{G}
and
u\in P_{L^{\vee}}
, or as
m_{+}n_{+}
where

m_{+}\in M_{P,+}
and
n_{+}\in N_{P,+}
. Note that the product
m_{+}n_{+}
is
taken in
G_{+}
, not in
\widetilde{G}
.

•

There is a right action of
H\times G
on
G_{+}
given by

(x,u)\cdot(h,g)=(h^{-1}xg,uh),

and it restricts to an action of
M_{P_{H}}\times M_{P_{G}}

on
P_{+}
for any
P\in\mathcal{F}
.

5.2.
Technical preparations

Let
P\in\mathcal{F}
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
J_{n}
and
w
be a weight on
[G]_{P_{G}}
. We pull
w
back to a
function on
[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}
, which we still denote by
w
.
Let
N
be a nonnegative integer. Let
Z\simeq\Res_{E/F}\mathbb{G}_{a,E}
be the central unipotent subgroup

Z\times 1\subset\widetilde{G}
and
\psi:[Z]\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a non trivial
character. We then have various spaces of functions as defined in
Subsection
3.3
.

For
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
and
P\subset Q
, we define weights on

[G]_{P_{G}}
by

\Delta_{P}(g)=\inf_{\gamma\in M_{P_{n}}(F)N_{P_{n}}(\mathbb{A})}\|g_{1}^{-1}\gamma g_{2}\|,\quad d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)=\min\left(d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1}),d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})\right)

Pulling back under the natural projection
[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}\to[G]_{P_{G}}
, we get two weights on

[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}
, which we still denote by
\Delta_{P}
and

d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}
.

The “approximation by constant terms” for the group
\widetilde{G}

takes the following form.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
N>0,r\geq 0
and for
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
with
P\subset Q
. There exists a
continuous semi-norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{N,X,r}
on
\mathcal{T}_{N}(\widetilde{Q}(F)\backslash\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
such that

\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(g)-\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{\widetilde{P}}(g)\rvert\leq\lVert g\rVert_{P_{G}}^{N}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)^{-r}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{N,X,r}

holds for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{Q}},\psi)

and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})\subset\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A})
.

Proof.

For
g=(g_{1},g_{2})\in[G]_{P_{G}}
we have

d_{\widetilde{P}}^{\widetilde{Q}}(g_{1},g_{2})\sim d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})\gg d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g_{1},g_{2})^{2}.

Hence the result follows from
Theorem
3.22
.
∎

We recall some function spaces introduced in
[
5
]
*Section 3.4.1.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n})
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G^{\prime}_{n})
) be the space of tuples of functions

({}_{P}\varphi)_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{T}([G_{n}]_{P_{n}}),\quad\text{resp. }({}_{P}\varphi)_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{T}([G^{\prime}_{n}]_{P^{\prime}_{n}})

such that

{}_{P}\varphi-({}_{Q}\varphi)_{P_{n}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q}}([G_{n}]_{P_{n}}),\quad\text{resp. }{}_{P}\varphi-({}_{Q}\varphi)_{P^{\prime}_{n}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q}}([G^{\prime}_{n}]_{P^{\prime}_{n}}),

for all
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
.
These spaces agree with the spaces
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}}(G_{n})

(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}}(G^{\prime}_{n})
) defined in defined in
[
5
]

by similar formulae,
where the product ranges over
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}

and the weights are
d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}
and
d_{P_{n+1}^{\prime}}^{Q_{n+1}^{\prime}}

respectively.
This is because there is a bijection between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RS}}
, and
d_{P}^{Q}\sim d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}|_{[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}}
(cf. Lemma
4.3
), and

d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}|_{[G_{n}^{\prime}]_{P_{n}^{\prime}}}\sim d_{P^{\prime}_{n+1}}^{Q^{\prime}_{n+1}}|_{[G_{n}^{\prime}]_{P_{n}^{\prime}}}

(cf.
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.4.4.2).

By the approximation by constant terms for the
groups
G
and
G^{\prime}
respectively,
cf. Theorem
3.22
, a family of functions

({}_{P}\varphi)_{P\in\mathcal{F}}
is in

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n})
(resp.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G^{\prime}_{n})
) if and only if there exists an
integer
N_{0}\geq 0
, such that for all
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
and for any
g\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
(resp.
G_{n}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}))
,

X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{n,\infty})
(resp.
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{n,\infty}))
, and
r>0
we have

(5.2)

\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(g)-\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi}(g)\rvert\ll\lVert g\rVert_{P_{n}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q}(g)^{-r}\quad\text{resp. }\lVert g\rVert_{P^{\prime}_{n}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q}(g)^{-r}.

As explained in
[
5
]
*Section 3.4.1,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n})
and

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G^{\prime}_{n})
are LF spaces.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(\widetilde{G})
be the space of tuples of
functions

(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)

such that

\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}-(\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi})_{\widetilde{P}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)
for all
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
. By Proposition

5.1
, this condition is equivalent
to the existence of
N_{0}
such that for all
\widetilde{g}\in\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A})
, all

X\in\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\infty})
and all
r\geq 0
we have

(5.3)

\left|\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(\widetilde{g})-\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi}(\widetilde{g})\right|\ll\lVert\widetilde{g}\rVert_{\widetilde{P}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)^{-r}.

In the same vein, we define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)
to be the
space of tuples of functions

(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([G]_{P_{G}})

such that
\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}-(\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi})_{P_{G}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([G]_{P_{G}})
if
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
. By
Theorem
3.22
, this condition is
equivalent to the existence of an
N_{0}
such that for all
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
, all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and all

r\geq 0
we have

(5.4)

\left|\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(g)-\mathrm{R}(X)\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi}(g)\right|\ll\lVert g\rVert_{P_{G}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)^{-r}

The space
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(\widetilde{G})

embeds in

\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)\times\prod_{P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)

as a closed subspace, and hence inherits an LF topology. Similarly
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)

is an LF space.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(\widetilde{G})
, then

\left(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}|_{[G]_{P_{G}}}\right)_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G).

Proof.

This follows directly from the characterizations (
5.3
)
and (
5.4
).
∎

Let
w
be a weight on
[G]_{P_{G}}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{w}^{0}([G]_{P_{G}})
be a Radon measure. We define a Radon measure
\alpha\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})
on
[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}
as follows. For
f\in C_{c}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}})
, we put

(5.5)

\langle\alpha\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}),f\rangle=\int_{[G]_{P_{G}}}\int_{[S]_{P_{S}}}f(sg)\prescript{}{P}{\Theta(sg_{1},\Phi_{0})}\mathrm{d}s\alpha(g),

where
[S]_{P_{S}}=N_{P_{S}}(\mathbb{A})M_{P_{S}}(F)\backslash S(\mathbb{A})
, and where we recall that
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
,
g_{1},g_{2}\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
.

Lemma 5.3
.

There is an
N_{0}>0
such that for all
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
we have

\alpha\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta(\cdot,\Phi_{0})}\in\mathcal{T}_{w,N_{0}}^{0}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

Moreover the map

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{w}([G]_{P_{G}})\to\mathcal{T}_{w,N_{0}}^{0}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi),\quad\alpha\mapsto\alpha\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta(\cdot,\Phi_{0})}

is continuous.

Proof.

We may pick an
N_{0}
such that for all
\Phi_{0}
we have

\sup_{g_{1}\in[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}}\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N_{0}}\int_{[S]_{P_{S}}}\lvert\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(sg_{1},\Phi_{0})\rvert\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}}w(g)^{-1}\lVert g\rVert_{P_{G}}^{-N_{0}}\lvert\alpha(g)\rvert\lvert\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(sg_{1},\Phi_{0})\rvert\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{g_{1}\in[G]_{P_{G}}}\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{G_{n}}}^{-N_{0}}\int_{[S]_{P_{S}}}\lvert\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(sg_{1},\Phi_{0})\rvert\mathrm{d}s\int_{[G]_{P_{G}}}w(g)^{-1}\lvert\alpha(g)\rvert<\infty.

The assertion on continuity follows from the same estimate.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

If
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P_{G}})
,
then

(\alpha\cdot\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))_{\widetilde{P}}=\alpha_{P_{G}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}).

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation using Lemma
4.6
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([H])
,
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
, and
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}),\psi^{-1})
, then the family

P\mapsto\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})(\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))

belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}}(\widetilde{G})
.

Proof.

We first note that the support of
\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})
is contained in

[\widetilde{H}]_{P_{\widetilde{H}}}
, and
\Delta_{P}
is bounded on it by
definition. Thus by Lemma
3.14
we have

\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})(\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))\in\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

We need to show that

\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})\left(\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})-(\varphi_{Q_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))_{\widetilde{P}}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

Indeed we have a slightly stronger result. Define on
[G]_{P_{G}}
a weight

d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(g)=\min\{d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{1}),d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})\},

which pulls back to a weight on
[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}
. We have

d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}\ll d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}
by
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.4.4.2. We
will show that

\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})\left(\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})-(\varphi_{Q_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))_{\widetilde{P}}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}}([\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

By Lemma
3.14
, it suffices to show there
is a positive real number
C
such that
\varphi_{P_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})
and
(\varphi_{Q_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))_{\widetilde{P}}
coincide on the set

\{\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}\mid d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(\widetilde{g})>C\}
.

By Lemma
5.4
we have

(\varphi_{Q_{H}}\cdot\prescript{}{Q}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}))_{\widetilde{P}}=(\varphi_{Q_{H}})_{P_{G}}\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0}).

We are thus reduced to show that there is a constant
C
such that

\varphi_{P_{H}}
and
(\varphi_{Q_{H}})_{P_{G}}
coincide on the set
\{g\in[G]_{P_{G}}\mid d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(g)>C\}
.

This is proved in the same way as
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.4.2.1(1). Using
the adjunction relation (
3.8
), we are reduced
to show that if
\lambda\in\mathcal{S}^{0}([G]_{P_{G}})
is supported in
\{g\in[G]_{P_{G}}\mid d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(g)>C\}
, then we have

E_{P_{H}}^{Q_{H}}(\lambda|_{H})=\left(E_{P_{G}}^{Q_{G}}\lambda\right)|_{[H]_{Q_{H}}}.

This in turn is equivalent to the fact that there is a
C>0
such that for
g\in P_{G}(F)N_{Q_{G}}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
with
d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(g)>C
,
\pi_{Q_{G}}^{P_{G}}(g)\in[H]_{Q_{H}}
implies
g\in P_{H}(F)Q_{H}(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
(recall
\pi_{Q_{G}}^{P_{G}}
is the map defined in (
3.11
)).
By definition,
d_{P}^{Q,\Delta_{n}}(g)>C
means
d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{i})>C
,
i=1,2
, and
\pi_{Q_{G}}^{P_{G}}(g)\in[H]_{Q_{H}}
means
\pi_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}(g_{1})=\pi_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}(g_{2})
. By Lemma
3.17
(4)
(applied to
G_{n}
), if
C
is sufficiently large, this implies
g_{1}=g_{2}
,
or equivalently
g\in P_{H}(F)Q_{H}(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
.
∎

Let
({}_{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)
and let
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
.
For
g_{1}\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
, we define a pairing

(5.6)

\langle\prescript{}{P}{\varphi},\varphi^{\prime}_{P_{n}^{\prime}}\rangle(g_{1})=\int_{[G_{n}^{\prime}]_{P_{n}^{\prime}}}\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(g_{1},g_{2}^{\prime})\varphi^{\prime}_{P_{n}^{\prime}}(g_{2}^{\prime})dg_{2}^{\prime}

We note that by definition for any weight
w
on

[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}
, there exists
N_{0}>0
such that

(5.7)

w(g_{2})\ll w(g_{1})\Delta_{P}(g)^{N_{0}}.

By taking
w=\|\cdot\|_{P_{n}}
in (
5.7
), we see that

\Delta_{P}(g)^{-N_{0}}\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}\|g_{2}\|^{-1}_{P_{n}}
. Therefore, there
exists
N_{1}>0
such that for any
N>0

{}_{P}\varphi(g_{1},g_{2})\ll\|g_{1}\|^{N_{1}+N}_{P_{n}}\|g_{2}\|^{-N}_{P_{n}}.

Since
\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}\sim\|g_{2}^{\prime}\|_{P_{n}^{\prime}}
, we see that the integral in
(
5.6
) is convergent.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let the notation be as above. Then the family

P\mapsto\langle\prescript{}{P}{\varphi},\varphi^{\prime}_{P_{n}^{\prime}}\rangle

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}([G_{n}])
.

Proof.

Using Dixmier–Malliavin theorem for the smooth Fréchet representation
\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)^{J}
for sufficiently small open compact subgroup
J\subset G_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we see that any
({}_{P}\varphi)\in\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)

is of the form
(\mathrm{R}(f){}_{P}\varphi_{1})
for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{n}(\mathbb{A}))
and
\prescript{}{P}{\varphi_{1}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
. Thus we are reduced to prove the same statement, but for

\langle\prescript{}{P}{\varphi_{1}},\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{P_{n}^{\prime}}\rangle

where
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G_{n}(\mathbb{A}))
. By
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.4.2.1
(2), the family of functions

P\in\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}_{n}}

satisfies

(5.8)

\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}_{n}}\in\mathcal{T}([G_{n}]_{P_{n}})

with

(5.9)

\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}_{n}}-(\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}_{n}})_{P_{n}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}}([G_{n}]_{P_{n}}),\quad\text{for $P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}$}.

Note that there is a slight inconsistency of notation in
[
5
]
here. In
the notation of
[
5
]
, we indeed apply
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.4.2.1
(2) to the case
\mathbf{G}=G_{n+1}
and replace
n+1
by
n
at all
places.

For each
P\in\mathcal{F}
, we define a function

\prescript{}{P}{\beta}
on
[G]_{P_{G}}
by

\prescript{}{P}{\beta}(g_{1},g_{2})=\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{R}(f^{\vee})\varphi^{\prime}_{P_{n}^{\prime}}(g_{2})

By the characterization (
5.4
)
and (
5.8
), (
5.9
), the family of functions
\beta=(\prescript{}{P}{\beta})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}
belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)
. Thus we are reduced to show that the family of functions

P\mapsto\left(g_{1}\mapsto\int_{[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}}\prescript{}{P}{\beta}(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}\right)

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n})
.

Again by the characterization (
5.4
), we will need to show that there is an integer
N_{0}>0
such that for all
({}_{P}\beta)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)
,
P\subset Q
, all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
, and
r\geq 0
we have

(5.10)

\left|\int_{[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}}\mathrm{R}_{1}(X){}_{P}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}-\int_{[G_{n}]_{Q_{n}}}\mathrm{R}_{1}(X){}_{Q}{\beta}(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}\right|\ll_{r,X}\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}.

Here
\mathrm{R}_{1}
stands for the action of the universal enveloping algebra
action on the first variable
g_{1}
. Up to replacing
\beta
by
\mathrm{R}_{1}(X)\beta
, we can assume
X=1
.

The rest of the argument is similar to
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.4.3.1.
By (
5.7
) applied to
w=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{P_{n}}
and

w=d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}
we see that there exists
N_{1}>0
such that for any

N>0
and
r\geq 0
,

(5.11)

\lvert\prescript{}{P}{\beta}(g)\rvert\ll\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})^{r}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{1})^{-r}

and

(5.12)

\lvert{}_{Q}\beta(g)\rvert\ll\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{Q_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{Q_{n}}^{-N}d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}(g_{2})^{r}d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}(g_{1})^{-r}\ll\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{Q_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{Q_{n}}^{-N}d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}(g_{2})^{r}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{1})^{-r}.

Here in the second inequality of (
5.12
) we have made use of the fact that
d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}\ll d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}
, cf.
[
5
]
*(2.4.4.19).

For
C>0
, let
\omega=\omega_{C}=\{g\in P_{n}(F)N_{Q_{n}}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G_{n}(\mathbb{A})\mid d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g)>C\}
. Let
\omega_{P}
and
\omega_{Q}

be the image of
\omega
under the projections
\pi_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}
and
\pi_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}
respectively. By Lemma
3.17
(1)(2),

d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}
and

d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}
are bounded above on
[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}\backslash\omega_{P}
and

[G_{n}]_{Q_{n}}\backslash\omega_{Q}
respectively. Thus by (
5.11
),
for any
r>0

\int_{[G_{n}]_{P_{n}}\backslash\omega_{P}}{}_{P}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{1})^{-r}\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}

for
N
large enough. Here the second inequality follows from the fact that
d_{P}^{Q}\sim d_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}\ll d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}
, cf. Lemma
4.3
and
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.4.4.2.
Similarly by (
5.12
), for any
r>0
, we have

\int_{[G_{n}]_{Q_{n}}\setminus\omega_{Q}}{}_{Q}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}.

It remains to estimate

\int_{\omega_{P}}{}_{P}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}-\int_{\omega_{Q}}{}_{Q}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}.

We choose
C
large enough so that
\omega\to\omega_{Q}
is injective.
This is possible by Lemma
3.17
(4).
Thus the above difference equals to

(5.13)

\int_{\omega}{}_{P}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})-{}_{Q}\beta(g_{1},g_{2})\mathrm{d}g_{2}.

We assume
g_{2}\in\omega
for the rest of the proof.
Since
d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}\sim d_{Q_{n}}^{P_{n}}
on
\omega
by
Lemma
3.17
(2), thus by (
5.11
)
and (
5.12
), we have for any
r\geq 0
and
N>0

\left|{}_{P}\beta(g)-{}_{Q}\beta(g)\right|\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})^{r}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{1})^{-r}\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})^{r}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}.

In particular, taking
r=0
in the first inequality, we have

\left|{}_{P}\beta(g)-{}_{Q}\beta(g)\right|\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}.

Thus we conclude that

\left|{}_{P}\beta(g)-{}_{Q}\beta(g)\right|\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{1}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}\max\{1,d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})^{-1}\}^{-r}.

Since the family
({}_{P}\beta)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)
, there is an
N_{0}>0
such that for any
r\geq 0
we have

\lvert\prescript{}{P}{\beta}(g)-\prescript{}{Q}{\beta}(g)\rvert\ll\lVert g\rVert_{P_{G}}^{N_{0}}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)^{-r}.

Note that for any real numbers
a,b>0
, we have

\max\{1,b/a\}\min\{a,b\}=b
.
Thus there is an
N_{2}
such that for any
r\geq 0
and
N>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{}_{P}\beta(g)-{}_{Q}\beta(g)\right|

\displaystyle\ll\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{2}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}\left(\max\{1,d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})d_{P_{n}}^{Q_{n}}(g_{2})^{-1}\}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)\right)^{-r}

\displaystyle=\|g_{1}\|_{P_{n}}^{N_{2}+N}\|g_{2}\|_{P_{n}}^{-N}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}.

Fix a large
N
. It follows that there is an
N_{3}
such that

\eqref{eq:integrate_over_omega}\ll\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{N_{3}}d_{P}^{Q}(g_{1})^{-r}

for any
r>0
. This proves the estimate (
5.10
).
∎

5.3.
A modified kernel

For
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
,
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
, and
P\in\mathcal{F}
,
we let
K_{f,P_{G}}
and
K_{f,P_{G},\chi}
be the kernel functions defined in
Subsection
3.7
. If
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
, we have the theta series
\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi)
on

[J]_{P}
defined in (
4.9
). For
(h,g)\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G]_{P_{G}}
we define kernel functions for the test function
f\otimes\Phi
by

(5.14)

K_{f\otimes\Phi,P}(h,g)=K_{f,P_{G}}(h,g)\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(h,\Phi),\quad K_{f\otimes\Phi,P,\chi}(h,g)=K_{f,P_{G},\chi}(h,g)\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(h,\Phi).

We will need to extend these definitions to all test functions
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
, not necessarily pure tensors. The case of
K_{f_{+},P}
is
straightforward. Put

K_{f_{+},P}(h,g)=\mu(\det h)^{-1}\lvert\det h\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{m_{+}\in M_{P,+}(F)}\int_{N_{P,+}(\mathbb{A})}f_{+}(m_{+}n_{+}\cdot(h,g))\mathrm{d}n_{+}.

Then it is clear that if
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
then
K_{f_{+},P}(h,g)=K_{f,P_{G}}(h,g)\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(h,\Phi)
. Moreover, for
fixed
h,g
the linear form on
\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
given by
f_{+}\mapsto K_{f_{+},P}(h,g)
is continuous. The case of
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
needs some work.
First we note that for all
N_{1}>0
there is an
N_{2}
and a semi-norm

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{N_{1},N_{2}}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
h\in[H]_{P_{H}}
,
g\in[G]_{P_{G}}
we have

(5.15)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\lvert K_{f,P,\chi}(h,g)\right\rvert\leq\lVert g_{1}\rVert^{N_{2}}_{P_{n}}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N_{1}}\lVert h\rVert_{P_{H}}^{-N_{1}}\lVert f\rVert_{N_{1},N_{2}},

and a similar estimate with
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
swapped. This is a consequence of
Lemma
3.20
applied to the weight
w=\Delta_{P}^{2N_{1}}\lVert\cdot\rVert^{-N_{1}}
. As
\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}\in\mathcal{T}([H]_{P_{H}})
, we arrive at the following estimates: for all
N_{1}>0
there
is an
N_{2}
and a semi-norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{N_{1},N_{2}}
on
\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
such
that for all
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
(algebraic tensor)
we have

(5.16)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\lvert K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(h,g)\right\rvert\leq\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{N_{2}}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N_{1}}\lVert h\rVert_{P_{H}}^{-N_{1}}\lVert f_{+}\rVert_{N_{1},N_{2}},

and

(5.17)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\lvert K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(h,g)\right\rvert\leq\lVert g_{1}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N_{2}}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{P_{n}}^{-N_{2}}\lVert h\rVert_{P_{H}}^{N_{1}}\lVert f_{+}\rVert_{N_{1},N_{2}}.

If
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
is approximated by a sequence of functions
f_{+,i}\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
, by the
estimate (
5.16
), the sequence
K_{f_{+,i},P,\chi}
is convergent to a function on
[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G]_{P_{G}}
, and this
convergence along with all the derivative is locally uniformly for
(h,g)\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G]_{P_{G}}
.
We denote this function by
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
. It is clearly independent of
the choice of the sequence approximating
f_{+}
. Because the convergence of all the derivative is
locally uniform,
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
is a smooth function. Moreover the
estimates (
5.16
)
and (
5.17
) continue to hold for
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
.

We now give another interpretation of
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
. Fix a Schwartz
function
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
with
\lVert\Phi_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
. For any

\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([H]_{P_{H}})
, we push forward
\varphi
to
a measure on
[G]_{P_{G}}
and we let
\varphi\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(\cdot,\Phi_{0})
be the measure on

[\widetilde{G}]_{\widetilde{P}}
defined in (
5.5
). If
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
we define a function
\widetilde{f_{+}}\in\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}))
by

\widetilde{f_{+}}(gs)=\langle f_{+}(g^{-1},\cdot),\ \mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(s)\Phi_{0}(\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}),\ s\in S(\mathbb{A}).

This notation means that we evaluate
f_{+}
at
g^{-1}
to obtain a Schwartz function
on
\mathbb{A}_{E,n}
and then take the
L^{2}
-inner product with

\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(s)\Phi_{0}
.
If
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
, then
\widetilde{f_{+}}(gs)=f^{\vee}(g)\langle\Phi,\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(s)\Phi_{0}\rangle
, where we recall that
f^{\vee}(g)=f(g^{-1})
.

We first note that the composition of the right translation

\varphi\mapsto\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f_{+}})(\varphi\cdot\prescript{}{P}{\Theta(\cdot,\Phi_{0})})

and restriction to
[G]_{P_{G}}
induces a continuous linear map

(5.18)

\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}}):\mathcal{T}^{0}([H]_{P_{H}})\to\mathcal{T}([G]_{P_{G}}),\quad

Let
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
and define
\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
to be the
composition of
\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
followed by projection to the

\chi
-component
\mathcal{T}_{\chi}([G]_{P_{G}})
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The maps
\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
and

\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
are represented by the kernel functions

K_{f_{+},P}
and
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
respectively, i.e. for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([H]_{P_{H}})
and
g\in[G]_{P_{G}}
we have

(5.19)

\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})(\varphi)(g)=\int_{[H]_{P_{H}}}K_{f_{+},P}(h,g)\varphi(h),\quad\mathrm{R}^{G}_{\chi}(\widetilde{f_{+}})(\varphi)(g)=\int_{[H]_{P_{H}}}K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(h,g)\varphi(h).

In particular the map
\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
is independent
of the choice of
\Phi_{0}
.

Note that the integrations appear in the first variable of the kernel function because we used
g^{-1}

in the definition of
\widetilde{f_{+}}
.

Proof.

Since both sides of (
5.19
) are continuous linear
forms in
f_{+}
, we may assume that
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
where
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
.

Let us prove (
5.19
) for

\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
. The equality for

\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})
follows from it. For

\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([H]_{P_{H}})
and
g\in[G]_{P_{G}}
, we have

\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})(\varphi)(g)=\int_{H(\mathbb{A})}\int_{Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash S(\mathbb{A})}\widetilde{f_{+}}(g^{-1}uh)\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(uh,\Phi_{0})\mathrm{d}u\varphi(h).

Then by the definition of
\widetilde{f_{+}}
, this equals

\int_{H(\mathbb{A})}\int_{Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash S(\mathbb{A})}f(h^{-1}g)\langle\Phi,\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(h^{-1}uh)\Phi_{0}\rangle\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(uh,\Phi_{0})\mathrm{d}u\varphi(h),

which simplifies to

\int_{[H]_{P_{H}}}K_{f,P_{G}}(h,g)\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(h,\Phi)\varphi(h).

This proves the lemma.
∎

If
P\subset Q
,
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
, we define a sign

\epsilon_{P}^{Q}=(-1)^{\dim\mathfrak{a}^{Q_{n+1}}_{P_{n+1}}}.

We simply write
\epsilon_{P}=\epsilon_{P}^{J_{n}}
. Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
be a truncation parameter and
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]\times[G^{\prime}]
. We
define a modified kernel

K_{f_{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},P}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

For each
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
, we also define

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

By
[
1
]
*Lemma 5.1, for
(h,g^{\prime})
fixed, in each sum the component
\delta_{1}
can be taken in a finite
set depending on
g_{1}^{\prime}
. The absolute convergence of the sums therefore follows from the
estimate (
5.15
).

Let
F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(\cdot,T)
be the characteristic function of Arthur
(characteristic function of the truncated Siegel set) for
G_{n+1}^{\prime}
defined
in Subsection
3.5
. For a fixed
T
, it is
compactly supported modulo the center of
G_{n+1}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
. In particular it is
compactly supported when restricted to
[G_{n}^{\prime}]
.

Theorem 5.8
.

For every
N>0
, there is a continuous semi-norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S},N}
on

\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that

(5.20)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\lvert K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})-F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(g_{1}^{\prime},T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(h,g^{\prime})\right\rvert\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\lVert h\rVert_{H}^{-N}\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\prime}}^{-N}\lVert f_{+}\rVert_{\mathcal{S},N}.

holds for all sufficiently positive
T
. In particular, for
T
sufficiently
positive

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h

is convergent and defines a continuous seminorm on
\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
.

Proof.

We first note that the second statement follows directly from (
5.16
) and the
estimate (
5.20
) by the fact that

F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(\cdot,T)
is compactly supported when restricted to
[G_{n}^{\prime}]
. So
we only need to prove the estimate (
5.20
).

We recall that
\mathcal{S}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
is the space of (measurable) rapid decreasing function on

[G_{n}^{\prime}]
, and it is a Fréchet space with the seminorms

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty,N}=\sup_{g\in[G_{n}^{\prime}]}\lVert g\rVert_{G_{n}^{\prime}}^{N}\lvert\varphi(g)\rvert.

We also recall that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n}^{\prime})
is an LF space. Two
relative truncation operators

\Lambda^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}},\Pi^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{S}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}]).

are defined in
[
5
]
*Section 3.5. For

\varphi=(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n}^{\prime})
, and
g^{\prime}\in[G_{n}^{\prime}]
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}\varphi(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\delta\in P_{n}^{\prime}(F)\backslash G_{n}^{\prime}(F)}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta g^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}}){}_{P}\varphi(\delta g),

\displaystyle\Pi^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}\varphi(g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(g^{\prime},T)(\prescript{}{J_{n}}{\varphi})(g^{\prime}).

By
[
5
]
*Theorem 3.5.1.1, for every
c>0
and
N>0
, there exists a
continuous seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{c,N}
on
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n}^{\prime})

such that for all sufficiently positive truncation parameter
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
we have

(5.21)

\lVert\Lambda^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}\varphi-\Pi^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}\varphi\rVert_{\infty,N}\ll e^{-c\|T\|}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{c,N},

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty,N}
is the norm on
\mathcal{S}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
.

For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A})\times\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
we consider the composition of
the following sequence of linear maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}^{0}([H])\otimes\mathcal{T}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\Delta}(G)\otimes\mathcal{T}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(G^{\prime}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|_{G_{n}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{T}}

where the first map is
\mathrm{R}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})\otimes\id
(defined
using any fixed
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
), the second is the pairing,
the third is the restriction, and the last is the truncation
\Lambda^{T,G^{\prime}_{n}}
(resp.
\Pi^{T,G_{n}^{\prime}}
), cf.
Lemmas
5.2
,
5.5
,
and
5.6
for the description of these maps. Their composition will
be denoted by
L_{f_{+}}^{T}
(resp.
P_{f_{+}}^{T}
). The fact that these maps are continuous is an easy consequence of the closed
graph theorem, see Remark
5.9
after the proof.

By the definition of
K_{f_{+},P}
we see that the function
K_{f_{+}}^{T}
(resp.

F^{G_{n+1^{\prime}}}(\cdot,T)K_{f_{+}}
) is the kernel function of the map

L_{f_{+}}^{T}
(resp.
P_{f_{+}}^{T}
). More precisely for any
\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([H])\otimes\mathcal{T}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
, we have

\displaystyle L_{f_{+}}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})(g_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{[H]}\int_{[G_{n}^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+}}^{T}(h;g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})\varphi(h)\varphi^{\prime}(g_{2}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle P_{f_{+}}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})(g_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{[H]}\int_{[G_{n}^{\prime}]}F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(g_{1}^{\prime},T)K_{f_{+}}(h;g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})\varphi(h)\varphi^{\prime}(g_{2}^{\prime}).

For
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
, define

L_{f_{+},\chi}^{T},P_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}:\mathcal{T}^{0}([H])\otimes\mathcal{T}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])\to\mathcal{S}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])

the same way as
L_{f_{+}}^{T}
and
P_{f_{+}}^{T}
respectively, except that we
replace the first map by
\mathrm{R}_{\chi}^{G}(\widetilde{f_{+}})\otimes\id
.
Then
L_{f_{+},\chi}^{T},P_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
are separably continuous, and the functions
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
and
F^{G_{n+1^{\prime}}}(\cdot,T)K_{f_{+},\chi}
are the kernel functions of the operators
L_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
and
P_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
respectively.

Fix
N>0
. By (
5.21
), for all
\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}^{0}([H])\otimes\mathcal{T}_{N}^{0}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
, we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\|L_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})-P_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})\right\|_{\infty,N}\ll e^{-N\lVert T\rVert}.

As in
[
5
]
*Section 3.6, the uniform boundedness principle (applied to

\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
) implies that

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\left\|L_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})-P_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime})\right\|_{\infty,N}\ll e^{-N\|T\|}\|\varphi\|_{1,-N}\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{1,-N}

holds for all
\varphi\otimes\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}([H])\otimes\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}([G_{n}^{\prime}])
and
T
sufficiently positive. We apply this estimate to
\varphi=\delta_{h}
and
\varphi^{\prime}=\delta_{g_{2}^{\prime}}
and conclude that

\sum_{\chi}\left\lvert K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})-F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(g_{1}^{\prime},T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(h,g^{\prime})\right\rvert\ll e^{-N\lVert T\rVert}\lVert h\rVert_{H}^{-N}\lVert g_{2}\rVert_{G^{\prime}}^{-N}.

By the uniform bounded principle again (applied to
f_{+}
), we see that there
exists a seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S},N}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
such that

\sum_{\chi}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})-F^{G_{n+1}^{\prime}}(g_{1}^{\prime},T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\|h\|_{H}^{-N}\|g^{\prime}\|_{G^{\prime}}^{-N}\|f_{+}\|_{\mathcal{S},N}.

This proves the estimate (
5.20
).
∎

Remark 5.9
.

We explain how the closed graph theorem is used in the proof to check continuity.
We temporarily denote by
X
and
Y
two topological spaces, and by
F(X)
and
F(Y)
certain
spaces of functions on
X
and
Y
respectively. Let
T:F(X)\to F(Y)
be a linear map. Assume that

•

T
is continuous when
F(Y)
is equipped with the pointwise convergence topology,

•

the topology on
F(Y)
is finer than the pointwise convergence topology,

•

F(X)
and
F(Y)
satisfy the closed graph theorem, i.e.
T
is continuous if and only if its graph is closed (which is the case if
F(X)
and
F(Y)
are LF spaces).

Then we claim that
T
is continuous. Indeed, if
(f_{a})
is a net in
F(X)
such
\lim_{a\in A}f_{a}=f
and
\lim_{a\in A}T(f_{a})=g
, then we have for all
y\in Y
the equality
T(f)(y)=\lim_{a\in A}T(f_{a})(y)=g(y)
, which implies that
T(f)=g
and therefore that
T
is continuous.

5.4.
The coarse spectral expansion

Let
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
. We put

I^{T}(f_{+})=\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h

and

I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h.

By Theorem
5.8
, these integrals are absolutely
convergent.

Theorem 5.10
.

For
T
sufficiently positive, the functions
I^{T}(f_{+})
and
I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})
are the
restrictions of exponential-polynomial functions whose purely polynomial parts
are constants. We denote them by
I(f_{+})
and
I_{\chi}(f_{+})
respectively.
Then
I
and
I_{\chi}
are continuous as distributions on
\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))

and for any
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}I_{\chi}(f_{+})=I(f_{+}),
