where the sum is absolutely convergent.

Before we delve into the proof of this theorem, let us first explain a
variant of the construction of the modified kernel for parabolic subgroups.
For
f^{\prime}_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
and
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}
,
and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
, we
define a kernel function
K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P\cap M_{Q}}(m_{H},m_{G})
on

[M_{Q_{H}}]_{P_{H}\cap M_{Q_{H}}}\times[M_{Q_{G}}]_{P_{G}\cap M_{Q_{G}}}
to be

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mu^{-1}(\det m_{H})\sum_{m_{+}\in M_{P_{+}}(F)}\int_{(N_{P_{+}}\cap M_{Q_{+}})(\mathbb{A})}f_{+}^{\prime}(m_{+}n_{+}\cdot(m_{H},m_{G}))\mathrm{d}n_{+},&&\text{$Q$ is of type I};\\
&|\det m_{H,k}|_{E}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-1}(\det m_{H})\sum_{m_{+}\in M_{P_{+}}(F)}\int_{(N_{P_{+}}\cap M_{Q_{+}})(\mathbb{A})}f_{+}^{\prime}(m_{+}n_{+}\cdot(m_{H},m_{G}))\mathrm{d}n_{+},&&\text{$Q$ is of type II}.\end{aligned}\right.

Here we assume that
Q_{n}
stabilizes the flag (
4.1
). The Levi subgroup of
Q_{H}\simeq Q_{n}
is isomorphic to
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\GL(L_{i}/L_{i-1})
, and we write
m_{H}\in M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})
as
(m_{H,1},\ldots,m_{H,r})
where

m_{H,i}\in\GL(L_{i}/L_{i-1})(\mathbb{A}_{E})
, and
\det m_{H}
means taking the determinant
of
m_{H}
as an element in
G_{n}
.

Similar to what we have done in Subsection
5.3
,

K_{f^{\prime}_{+},P\cap M_{Q}}(m_{H},m_{G})
is the kernel function of a continuous map

\mathcal{T}^{0}([M_{Q_{H}}]_{P_{H}\cap M_{Q_{H}}})\to\mathcal{T}([M_{Q_{G}}]_{P_{G}\cap M_{Q_{G}}})
.
For
\chi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{Q_{G}})
, composing this map with the projection to the

\chi^{\prime}
-component of
\mathcal{T}([M_{Q_{G}}]_{P_{G}\cap M_{Q_{G}}})
is given by another
kernel function, denoted by
K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P\cap M_{Q},\chi^{\prime}}(m_{H},m_{G})
. Recall
that there is a natural finite-to-one map
\mathfrak{X}(M_{Q_{G}})\to\mathfrak{X}(G)
which we
temporarily denote by
\iota_{Q}
. Then for
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
, we define

K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}(m_{H},m_{G})=\sum_{\chi^{\prime}\in\iota_{Q}^{-1}(\chi)}K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P\cap M_{Q},\chi^{\prime}}(m_{H},m_{G}).

For
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
and
(m_{H},m^{\prime})\in[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]
we put

\displaystyle K_{f^{\prime}_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(m_{H},m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{F}\\
P\subset Q\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{P}^{Q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in(P_{H}(F)\cap M_{Q_{H}}(F))\backslash M_{Q_{H}}(F)\\
\delta\in(P_{G^{\prime}}(F)\cap M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(F))\backslash M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}m_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f^{\prime}_{+},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}(\gamma m_{H},\delta m^{\prime}).

The group
A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}
which embeds in
M_{Q_{H}}\times M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}
diagonally.
Note if
Q=J_{n}
, then
A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}
is trivial. By the same
method of proof of Theorem
5.8
, one can show that
for any
f_{+}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T
sufficiently positive, the
expression

(5.22)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}\left\lvert K_{f_{+}^{\prime},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(m_{H},m^{\prime})\right\rvert\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}

is finite and defines a continuous seminorm on
\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
. Define
a distribution on
\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
by

I^{M_{Q},T}_{\chi}(f^{\prime}_{+})=\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}K_{f^{\prime}_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(m_{H},m^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime})\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}.

Here and below in the proof,
\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime})
means that we evaluate
\eta_{n+1}

at
m^{\prime}
when
m^{\prime}
is viewed as an element in
G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
.

Proof of Theorem
5.10
.

By
[
3
]
*Section 2, there exist functions
\Gamma_{P_{n+1}}^{\prime}
on

\mathfrak{a}_{P_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}\times\mathfrak{a}_{P_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}
, for
P\in\mathcal{F}
, that are compactly supported in the first variable when
the second variable stays in a compact set and such that

(5.23)

\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H-X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q\supset P\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H)\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H,X),\quad H,X\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}.

Here following Arthur, by
\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H,X)
we mean evaluating

\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}
at the projection of
H
and
X
to

\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}
.

We set
\underline{\rho}_{Q}=\rho_{Q_{n+1}}-\rho_{Q_{n}}\in\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1},*}
. If
Q_{n}
stabilizes the
flag (
4.1
), then for
m\in M_{Q_{n}}(\mathbb{A})
of the form
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})
with
m_{i}\in\GL(L_{i}/L_{i-1})(\mathbb{A}_{E})
, one checks
directly that

(5.24)

e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m)\rangle}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\prod_{i\leq k}\left|\det m_{i}\right|_{E}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\prod_{i>k}\left|\det m_{i}\right|_{E}^{-\frac{1}{2}},&&\text{$Q$ is of type I};\\
&\prod_{i\leq k}\left|\det m_{i}\right|_{E}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\prod_{i>k+1}\left|\det m_{i}\right|_{E}^{-\frac{1}{2}},&&\text{$Q$ is of type II}.\end{aligned}\right.

Define a function
p_{Q}
on
\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}
by

(5.25)

p_{Q}(X)=\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H,X)\mathrm{d}H.

By
[
47
]
*Lemma 3.5,
p_{Q}
is an exponential polynomial on

\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}
whose exponents are contained in the set

\{\underline{\rho}_{R}\mid R\supset Q\}
and whose pure polynomial term is
the constant
\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\theta}_{Q}(\underline{\rho}_{Q})^{-1}
.
Here
\widehat{\theta}_{Q}
is the homogeneous polynomial function on

\mathfrak{a}_{P_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}
defined in
[
3
]
*Section 2. We do not
need its precise definition.

For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
we define for
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]_{Q_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}
a modified kernel function

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{F}\\
P\subset Q\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{P}^{Q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash Q_{H}(F)\\
\delta\in P_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash Q_{G^{\prime}}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}),

By (
5.23
), for
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
, we have an inversion
formula

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in Q_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in Q_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T^{\prime}}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

We integrate both sides over
[H]\times[G^{\prime}]
. For
T
sufficiently
positive, the integral of
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})
is absolutely convergent by
Theorem
5.8
. Moreover, when
T^{\prime}
is also sufficiently
positive, the computation below will show that the integral of each terms in
the sum over
Q
is also absolutely convergent. It follows that
I_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
equals

(5.26)

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{[H]_{Q_{H}}}\int_{[G^{\prime}]_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(g_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T^{\prime}}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

We now relate
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}
and
K_{f_{+}^{\prime},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}
via
parabolic descent. Recall that we have fixed good maximal compact subgroups

K_{H}
and
K_{G^{\prime}}
of
H(\mathbb{A})
and
G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
respectively. For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
, we define its parabolic descent as a function on

M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A})
given by

(5.27)

\displaystyle f_{+,Q}(m,l)=e^{\langle\rho_{Q_{G}},H_{Q_{G}}(m)\rangle}\times

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}\int_{{N_{Q_{G}}}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{N_{Q_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})}}f_{+}(k_{H}^{-1}mnk^{\prime},(l+u)k_{H})\eta_{n+1}(k^{\prime})\mu(\det k_{H})^{-1}\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}n\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}.

Here
m\in M_{G}(\mathbb{A})
and
l\in M_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})
, and we take the convention that
l=0
if
Q
is of type I.
Then
f_{+,Q}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A}))
. For
(m_{H},m^{\prime})\in[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G}}]
we have

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(m_{H}k_{H},m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\langle\rho_{Q_{G}},H_{Q_{G}}(m^{\prime})+H_{Q_{G}}(m_{H})\rangle}\cdot e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}(m_{H},m_{G}).

Indeed, using (
5.24
), we directly check this
identity without the
\chi
. The argument in
[
47
]
*Lemma 1.3 shows
that this implies the identity with the
\chi
.
Therefore for
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
and
(m_{H},m^{\prime})\in[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]
,
we have

(5.28)

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(m_{H}k_{H},m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\langle\rho_{Q_{G}},H_{Q_{G}}(m^{\prime})+H_{Q_{G}}(m_{H})\rangle}\cdot e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(m_{H},m^{\prime}).

Fix a
Q\in\mathcal{F}
. By the Iwasawa decomposition, the summand
corresponding to
Q
in (
5.26
) equals

\displaystyle\int_{[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{H}},H_{Q_{H}}(m_{H})\rangle}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{G^{\prime}}},H_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(m^{\prime})\rangle}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})

\displaystyle K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T^{\prime}}(m_{H}k_{H},m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\mathrm{d}k_{H}.

By (
5.28
), it equals

\displaystyle\int_{[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}

\displaystyle e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})

\displaystyle K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(m_{H},m^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime})\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}.

We break the integral into an integral over the split center
A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}

and another over
A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]
to write
the above integral as

(5.29)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(m_{H},m^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle\left(\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(am_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(am_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})\mathrm{d}a\right)\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}

Note that the composition of the embeddings
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n}}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}
with the projection
\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}\to\mathfrak{a}^{G_{n+1}}_{Q_{n+1}}
yields an isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}\simeq\mathfrak{a}^{G_{n+1}}_{Q_{n+1}}
whose Jacobian we denote by
c_{Q}
. The inner integral
in (
5.29
) thus equals

c_{Q}\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},X+H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(X+H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}},T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})\mathrm{d}X.

Changing the variable
X\mapsto X-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})+T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}
, we obtain that
this equals

c_{Q}\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}^{G_{n+1}}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},X+T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(X,T_{Q_{n+1}}-T^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}})\mathrm{d}X.

By definition, cf. (
5.25
), this equals

c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T-T^{\prime}).

In particular it is independent of
m^{\prime}
. We then have

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:polynomial_exponential_final_step}=

\displaystyle c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T-T^{\prime})

\displaystyle\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(m_{H},m^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime})\mathrm{d}m_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}.

This integral is absolutely convergent by (
5.22
). If we
define

\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}(m,l)=e^{\langle-\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m)\rangle}f_{+,Q}(m,l),\quad(m,l)\in M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A}),

then

(5.30)

K_{\widetilde{f_{+,Q}},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(m_{H},m^{\prime})=e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(m_{H},m^{\prime}).

Thus

\eqref{eq:polynomial_exponential_final_step}=c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T-T^{\prime})I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}).

In conclusion, we have

I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T-T^{\prime})I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}).

That is,
I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})
is an exponential-polynomial in
T
and the pure
polynomial term is the constant

(5.31)

I_{\chi}(f_{+})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}c_{Q}\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\theta}_{Q}(\underline{\rho}_{Q})^{-1}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T^{\prime}\rangle}I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}).

This proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Using the same method, one can show that for any
Q\in\mathcal{F}
, and
any
f_{+}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T
sufficiently positive, as a function
of
T
,
I^{M_{Q},T}_{\chi}(f_{+}^{\prime})
is an exponential polynomial whose exponents
are contained in
\{\underline{\rho}_{P}-\underline{\rho}_{Q}\mid P\subset Q\}
and only depend on the image
T_{Q_{n+1}}
of projection of
T
to

\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{n+1}}
, but the pure polynomial term is not necessarily a constant.

Proposition 5.12
.

The distribution
I_{\chi}
is left
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant and right

(G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}),\eta_{G^{\prime}})
-equivariant. More precisely for all
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
,
h\in H(\mathbb{A})
and
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
we have

I_{\chi}((h,g^{\prime})\cdot f_{+})=\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})I_{\chi}(f_{+})

where
((h,g^{\prime})\cdot f_{+})(g,u)=\lvert\det h\rvert_{E}^{1/2}\mu(\det h)^{-1}f_{+}(h^{-1}gg^{\prime},uh)
.

Proof.

Fix an
(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\in H(\mathbb{A})\times G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
. Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
be
sufficiently positive. By (
5.23
) again we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})=

\displaystyle\sum_{P\subset Q}\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime})).

Therefore we see that
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(hh_{0},g^{\prime}g_{0}^{\prime})
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{P\subset Q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma hh_{0},\delta g^{\prime}g_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime})).

Note that

K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},P,\chi}(h,g^{\prime})=K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(hh_{0},g^{\prime}g_{0}^{\prime}).

Thus by summing over
P\in\mathcal{F}
first in the above expression, we see that
it simplifies to

(5.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in Q_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in Q_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

We integrate
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(hh_{0},g^{\prime}g_{0}^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})
over
[H]\times[G^{\prime}]
.
On the one hand by definition it equals

\int_{[H]\times[G^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(hh_{0},g^{\prime}g_{0}^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h=\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime}_{0})I^{T}_{\chi}(f_{+}).

On the other hand, by (
5.32
),
it equals the sum over all
Q\in\mathcal{F}
of the terms

(5.33)

\displaystyle\int_{[H]_{Q_{H}}}\int_{[G^{\prime}]_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{Q_{n+1}}^{\prime}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(g_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

We calculate this as in the proof of
Theorem
5.10
. By the Iwasawa decomposition
it equals

(5.34)

\displaystyle\int_{[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]}\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{H}},H_{Q_{H}}(m_{H})\rangle}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{G^{\prime}}},H_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(m^{\prime})\rangle}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},-H_{Q_{n+1}}(k_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(m_{H}k_{H},m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(m^{\prime}k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}m^{\prime}\mathrm{d}m_{H}.

For
(m,l)\in M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A})
, we put

\displaystyle f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}(m,l)=e^{\langle\rho_{Q_{G}},H_{Q_{G}}(m)\rangle}

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K^{\prime}}\int_{{N_{Q_{G}}}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{N_{Q_{L^{\vee}}(\mathbb{A})}}((h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+})(k_{H}^{-1}mnk^{\prime},(l+u)k_{H})

\displaystyle p_{Q}(-H_{Q_{n+1}}(k_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))\eta_{n+1}(k^{\prime})\mu^{-1}(k_{H})\mathrm{d}n\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}.

One check directly that for an
(m_{H},m^{\prime})\in[M_{Q_{H}}]\times[M_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}]
, the expression

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{H}},H_{Q_{H}}(am_{H})\rangle}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{G^{\prime}}},H_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(am^{\prime})\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(am_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},-H_{Q_{n+1}}(k_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},P}(am_{H}k_{H},am^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(am^{\prime}k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}

equals to

e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}},P\cap M_{Q}}^{M_{Q}}(m_{H},m^{\prime}).

Then for any
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
,

\displaystyle\int_{K_{H}\times K_{G^{\prime}}}\int_{A_{M_{Q}}^{\infty}}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{H}},H_{Q_{H}}(am_{H})\rangle}e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q_{G^{\prime}}},H_{Q_{G^{\prime}}}(am^{\prime})\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(am_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},-H_{Q_{n+1}}(k_{1}^{\prime}g_{0,1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle K_{(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+},P,\chi}(am_{H}k_{H},am^{\prime}k^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(am^{\prime}k^{\prime})\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}k_{H}\mathrm{d}k^{\prime}

equals to

e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{H})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(m_{1}^{\prime})\rangle}c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}^{M_{Q}}(m_{H},m^{\prime}).

Therefore (
5.34
) equals

c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T\rangle}I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}}),

where

\widetilde{f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}}(m,l)=e^{\langle-\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q_{n+1}}(m)\rangle}f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}(m,l),\quad(m,l)\in M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A})

In conclusion, we have

\eta_{n+1}(g_{0}^{\prime})I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T\rangle}I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}}).

Each
I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}})
is a exponential polynomial,
whose exponents are in the set
\{\underline{\rho}_{P}-\underline{\rho}_{Q}\mid P\subset Q\}
. Since
\underline{\rho}_{P}
is not trivial unless

P=J_{n}
, the only term on the right hand side
that has a (possibly) nonzero purely polynomial part correspond to
Q=J_{n}
. In this case
f_{+,Q,h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime}}=(h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+}
and
the pure polynomial part of the right hand side equals

I_{\chi}((h_{0},g_{0}^{\prime})\cdot f_{+})
.
∎

5.5.
A second modified kernel

For later use we will need another modified kernel. For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
,
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
, and
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]\times[G^{\prime}]
, we
define

\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\gamma h)-T_{P_{n+1}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

Proposition 5.13
.

For every
N>0
, there is a continuous seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S},N}
on

\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A})\times\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
such that for all
h\in[H]
and
g^{\prime}\in[G^{\prime}]
we have

(5.35)

\sum_{\chi}\left\lvert\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})-F^{G_{n+1}}(h,T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(h,g^{\prime})\right\rvert\leq e^{-N\lVert T\rVert}\lVert h\rVert_{H}^{-N}\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\prime}}^{-N}\lVert f_{+}\rVert_{\mathcal{S},N},

In particular

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\left|\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h

for
T
sufficiently large.

Proof.

As in the case of Theorem
5.8
, we only need to
prove the estimate (
5.35
), and second
assertion on the absolute convergence follows from it. The proof is very
similar to (and in fact simpler than) that of
Theorem
5.8
, so we will only sketch the
differences.

We first note that we only need to prove that there are continuous semi-norms

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S},1}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S},2}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
respectively, such that

\sum_{\chi}\left\lvert\kappa_{f\otimes\Phi,\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})-F^{G_{n+1}}(h,T)K_{f\otimes\Phi,\chi}(h,g^{\prime})\right\rvert\leq e^{-N\lVert T\rVert}\lVert h\rVert_{H}^{-N}\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{G^{\prime}}^{-N}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{S},1}\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\mathcal{S},2}.

Once we have this, the estimate (
5.35
) holds
for
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
.
For fixed
h
and
g^{\prime}
, the left hand side
of (
5.35
) is continuous with respect to

f_{+}
by definition. Thus the
estimate (
5.35
) is obtained by taking
limits.

We assume
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
from now on. Define

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)
to be the space of tuples

(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}}
such that

\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\in\mathcal{T}([G]_{P_{G}}),\quad\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}-(\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi})_{P_{G}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P_{G}}^{Q_{G}}}([G]_{P_{G}}).

Let us consider the series of linear maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}^{0}([G])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(\widetilde{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{0}([H])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{T,H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{T,H}}

The first map is

\varphi\mapsto\mathrm{R}(f)\varphi_{P_{G^{\prime}}}.

The second map is given by

\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\mapsto\left((j,g_{2})\mapsto\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}(j,g_{2})\prescript{}{P}{\Theta}(j,\Phi)\right),

where
(j,g_{2})\in J_{n}\times G_{n}
and
g_{1}
is the image of

j
in
G_{n}
. The third one is the restriction to
[H]_{P_{H}}
.
The last map is one of the two truncation operators

\Lambda^{T,H},\Pi^{T,H}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}}(H)\to\mathcal{S}^{0}([H]).

defined in
[
5
]
*Section 3.5. The fact that the family
P\mapsto\mathrm{R}(f)\varphi_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(G)

follows from
[
5
]
*Proposition 3.4.2.1.2. The fact that rest of the
maps make sense follows directly from the definition. Continuity of these maps
follow from the closed graph theorem,
cf. Remark
5.9
. The compositions of
these maps are given by integral kernels
\kappa_{f\otimes\Phi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})

and
F^{G_{n+1}}(h,T)\kappa_{f\otimes\Phi}(h,g^{\prime})
respectively.

Let
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
. We modify the maps slightly, by using the map

\mathrm{R}_{\chi}(f)
in the first one, where
\mathrm{R}_{\chi}(f)
is the
composition of
\mathrm{R}(f)
followed by projection to the

\chi
-component. The kernel function associated to the resulting maps are

\kappa_{f\otimes\Phi,\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})
and
F^{G_{n+1}}(h,T)\kappa_{f\otimes\Phi,\chi}(h,g^{\prime})
respectively.

The rest of the proof is exactly the same as that of
Theorem
5.8
.
∎

Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
be sufficiently large and put

i_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{n+1}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}h

Proposition 5.14
.

As a function of
T
, the function
i_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})
is the restriction of
an exponential polynomial function whose purely polynomial part is a constant
and equals
I_{\chi}(f_{+})
.

Proof.

By (
5.23
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\gamma h)-T_{P_{n+1}})=

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\supset P}\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}^{Q_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{P_{n+1}})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(\gamma h)).

Plugging into the definition of
\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\kappa_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in Q_{H}(F)\backslash G(F)\\
\delta\in Q_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q_{n+1}}(H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-T_{Q_{n+1}},H_{Q_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g_{1}^{\prime})-H_{Q_{n+1}}(\gamma h))K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

Define
f_{+,Q}
by (
5.28
) and

\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}

by (
5.30
) as in the proof of
Theorem
5.10
, then the same computation as
in the proof of Theorem
5.10
gives (we
follow the same notation there)

i_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}c_{Q}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},T\rangle}I_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}),

As in the proof of Theorem
5.10
and
Proposition
5.12
, cf. also
Remark
5.11
,
we see that
i_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})
is an
exponential polynomial function of
T
. The only term on the right hand side
with a (possibly) nonzero purely polynomial part is the one that correspond
to
Q=J_{n}
. This term equals
I_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})
and
the purely polynomial part is the constant
I_{\chi}(f_{+})
.
∎

6.
The coarse geometric expansion: general linear groups

6.1.
Geometric modified kernels

We keep the notation from the previous section. We will need in addition the
following list of notation.

•

Recall that
L=E^{n}
and
L^{\vee}=E_{n}
. Let
L^{-}
and
L^{\vee,-}

be the purely imaginary part, i.e.
L^{-}=E^{-,n}
and
L^{\vee,-}=E_{n}^{-}
. If
A
is a subset of
L^{\vee}\times L
, we define
A^{-}
to be
its intersection with
L^{\vee,-}\times L^{-}
.

•

We put
G^{+}=G\times L^{\vee,-}\times L^{-}
, the group structure being
the product of
G
and
L^{\vee,-}\times L^{-}
. Note that this is not a
subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
, but merely a subvariety.
The group
H\times G^{\prime}
acts from the right on
G^{+}
by

(6.1)

(g,w,v)\cdot(h,g^{\prime})=(h^{-1}gg^{\prime},wg_{1}^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime-1}v).

•

Let
P=MN\in\mathcal{F}
. Put
M^{+}=M_{G}\times M_{L^{\vee}}^{-}\times M_{L}^{-}
,
N^{+}=N_{G}\times N_{L^{\vee}}^{-}\times N^{-}_{L}
, and
P^{+}=M^{+}N^{+}
. We often write an element in
P^{+}
as

m^{+}n^{+}
, but one should note that the product is the one in
G^{+}
, not
the one in
\widetilde{G}
.

•

Let
q:G^{+}\to\mathcal{A}=G^{+}//(H\times G^{\prime})
be the GIT quotient. We define a morphism
G^{+}:\to\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
by

(6.2)

((g_{1},g_{2}),w,v)\mapsto(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n};b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}),

where
\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
denotes the
2n
-dimensional affine space over
E
, and we put

s=(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2})^{\mathsf{c},-1},\quad a_{i}=\Trace\wedge^{i}s,\quad b_{i}=ws^{i}v,\quad i=1,2,\ldots,n.

This map descends to a locally closed embedding
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
. We always consider
\mathcal{A}
as a locally closed subscheme of
\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
.

•

If
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
, we define
G^{+}_{\alpha}
to be the inverse
image of
\alpha
(as a closed subscheme of
G^{+}
). For
P=MN\in\mathcal{F}
, we put

M^{+}_{\alpha}=G^{+}_{\alpha}\cap M^{+}
.

Let us now introduce the geometric counterparts of the modified kernels. For

\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
,
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
, and
P\in\mathcal{F}
,
we define kernel functions on
[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
by

k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{m^{+}\in M^{+}_{\alpha}(F)}\int_{N^{+}(\mathbb{A})}f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))\mathrm{d}n^{+},

and

k_{f^{+},P}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{m^{+}\in M^{+}(F)}\int_{N^{+}(\mathbb{A})}f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))\mathrm{d}n^{+}.

Lemma 6.1
.

There is an
N>0
and a seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))

such that for all
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
, we have

\sum_{m^{+}\in M^{+}(F)}\int_{N^{+}(\mathbb{A})}\lvert f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))\rvert\mathrm{d}n\mathrm{d}l\leq\lVert f^{+}\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}\lVert h\rVert_{P_{H}}^{N}\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{P_{G^{\prime}}}^{N}.

In particular the defining expressions of
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
and
k_{f^{+},P}
are absolutely convergent and we have

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(h,g^{\prime})=k_{f^{+},P}(h,g^{\prime}).

Proof.

For any large enough

d
and
N
, we can find a continuous seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{d}
on

\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that

\lvert f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))\rvert\leq\lVert m^{+}n^{+}\rVert_{P^{+}(\mathbb{A})}^{-d}\lVert f^{+}\rVert_{d}\lVert h\rVert_{P_{H}}^{N}\lVert g^{\prime}\rVert_{P_{G^{\prime}}}^{N}

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{P^{+}(\mathbb{A})}
stands for a height function on

P^{+}(\mathbb{A})
. The lemma then follows
from
[
8
]
*Proposition A.1.1.(v-vi).
∎

We make the following key observation. We identify
\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
with the
4n
-dimensional affine space
\mathbf{A}^{4n}
over
F
, and denote the morphism
G^{+}\to\mathbf{A}^{4n}
again by
q
. We extend the definition of
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
to all
\alpha\in F^{4n}
by setting
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\not\in\mathcal{A}(F)
.

Let
C\subset G^{+}(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})
be an open compact subset such that
\supp f^{+}\subset C\times G^{+}(F_{\infty})
. Since
q(C)\subset\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^{4n}
is compact, there exists

d\in F
depending only on
C
such that
q(C)\cap F^{4n}\subset(d\mathcal{O}_{F})^{4n}
. Then if
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
is not identically zero for some
\alpha\in F^{4n}
and
P\in\mathcal{F}
, then
\alpha\in(d\mathcal{O}_{F})^{4n}
.

Put
\Lambda=(d\mathcal{O}_{F})^{4n}
which is a lattice in
F_{\infty}^{4n}
. For each

\alpha\in\Lambda
, take
u_{\alpha}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(F^{4n}_{\infty})
and define
the functions
f^{+}_{\alpha}=f^{+}\cdot u_{\alpha}
as in
Subsection
3.10
.

Lemma 6.2
.

We have

k_{f^{+}_{\alpha},P}(h,g^{\prime})=k_{f^{+}_{\alpha},P,\alpha}(h,g^{\prime})=k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(h,g^{\prime})

for all
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
. In particular,
k_{f^{+}_{\alpha},P}(h,g^{\prime})
is independent of the choice of the bump function

u_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Take
\beta\in\mathcal{A}(F)
.
For any
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
, and any
m^{+}\in M^{+}_{\beta}(F)
,
n^{+}\in N^{+}(\mathbb{A})
, we have
m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime})\in q^{-1}(\beta)
. By the definition of
f^{+}_{\alpha}
, we
have
f^{+}_{\alpha}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))=f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(h,g^{\prime}))
if
\alpha=\beta
and
0
if

\alpha\not=\beta
. The result follows.
∎

The kernel
K_{f_{+},P}
introduced in
Subsection
5.3
is closely related to
k_{f^{+},P}
. First we recall
that the action
\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}
of
G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
given
by

\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(g)\Phi(x)=\mu(\det g)^{-1}\lvert\det g\rvert_{E}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Phi(xg),\quad g\in G_{n}(\mathbb{A}),\quad\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n}).

We define a Fourier transform
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}_{E}^{n,-})
by

(6.3)

\Phi^{\dagger}(w,v)=\int_{\mathbb{A}_{n}}\Phi(x+w)\psi((-1)^{n}\tau xv)\mathrm{d}x,\quad(w,v)\in\mathbb{A}_{E,n}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}_{E}^{n,-}.

Then there is a unique action
\mathrm{R}^{\dagger}_{\mu^{-1}}
of
G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
on

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}_{E}^{n,-})
such that the Fourier transform is
equivariant. Direct computation gives that if
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
and

\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
then

\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}^{{\dagger}}(g^{\prime})\Phi^{\dagger}(w,v)=\eta(\det g^{\prime})\Phi^{\dagger}(wg^{\prime},g^{\prime-1}v).

This Fourier transform extends to a continuous linear
isomorphism

\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))\to\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A})),\quad f_{+}\mapsto f_{+}^{\dagger},

given by

(6.4)

f_{+}^{\dagger}(g,w,v)=\left(\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}^{\dagger}(g_{1}^{-1})f_{+}(g,\cdot)^{\dagger}\right)(w,v),\quad(g,w,v)\in G^{+}(\mathbb{A}).

Here the expression on the right hand side is interpreted as follows. We
evaluate
f_{+}
at
g
first to obtain a Schwartz function on
\mathbb{A}_{E,n}
, then take the Fourier transform
-^{\dagger}
, then make
g_{1}^{-1}
act on it via

\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}^{\dagger}
, and finally evaluate the result at
(w,v)
. We denote by

-_{\dagger}
the inverse integral transform of
-^{\dagger}
. If
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
where
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
then we have a
cleaner expression

f_{+}^{\dagger}(g,w,v)=f(g)\left(\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(g_{1}^{-1})\Phi\right)^{\dagger}(w,v)=f(g)\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}^{\dagger}(g_{1}^{-1})\Phi^{\dagger}(w,v).

Lemma 6.3
.

For all
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]_{P_{H}}\times[G^{\prime}]_{P_{G^{\prime}}}
and
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
, we have

K_{f_{+},P}(h,g^{\prime})=k_{f_{+}^{\dagger},P}(h,g^{\prime})\eta(\det g_{1}^{\prime}).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
, for fixed
h

and
g^{\prime}
, both sides are continuous linear forms on
f_{+}
. Therefore we only
need to prove the lemma when
f_{+}=f\otimes\Phi
where
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{E,n})
. To best illustrate the ideas,
we prove the lemma in the case
P=G
. The general case follows by the same
computation but with messier notation. By definition
K_{f\otimes\Phi}(h,g^{\prime})

equals

\sum_{\gamma\in G(F)}\sum_{x\in E_{n}}f(h^{-1}\gamma g^{\prime})\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(h)\Phi(x).

This equals

\sum_{\gamma\in G(F)}\sum_{x\in E_{n}}f(h^{-1}\gamma g^{\prime})\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(g_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{-1}h)\Phi(xg_{1}^{\prime})\lvert\det g_{1}^{\prime}\rvert\eta(\det g_{1}^{\prime}).

The Poisson summation formula then gives

\sum_{\gamma\in G(F)}\sum_{(w,v)\in E_{n}^{-}\times E^{n,-}}f(h^{-1}\gamma g^{\prime})(\mathrm{R}_{\mu^{-1}}(g_{1}^{\prime-1}\gamma_{1}^{-1}h)\Phi)^{\dagger}(wg_{1},g_{1}^{-1}v)\eta(\det g_{1}^{\prime}),

which equals
k_{f_{+}^{\dagger},P}(h,g^{\prime})\eta(\det g_{1}^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
. We have

\eta(g_{1}^{\prime})((h,g^{\prime})\cdot f^{+})_{{\dagger}}=(h,g^{\prime})\cdot(f^{+})_{\dagger},

where on the left hand, the action is the one induced from the
action (
6.1
),
and on the right hand side the action is the one defined in
Proposition
5.12
.

Proof.

This is a direct computation.
∎

Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}
be a truncation parameter, and let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
. We
define

k_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g^{\prime}_{1})-T_{P_{n+1}})k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}),

and

k_{f^{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P_{H}(F)\backslash H(F)\\
\delta\in P_{G^{\prime}}(F)\backslash G^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P_{n+1}}(H_{P_{n+1}}(\delta_{1}g^{\prime}_{1})-T_{P_{n+1}})k_{f^{+},P}(\gamma h,\delta g^{\prime}).

Lemma 6.5
.

For
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
, if
T
is sufficiently positive the
integral

\int_{[H]\times[G^{\prime}]}\left\lvert k_{f^{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right\rvert\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

is convergent and defines a seminorm on
\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
. Put

i^{T}(f^{+})=\int_{[H]\times[G^{\prime}]}k_{f^{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{G^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

Then
i^{T}(f^{+})
is the restriction of a polynomial exponential whose purely
polynomial part is a constant that equals
I((f^{+})_{\dagger})
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
6.3
that if
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
then

k_{f^{+}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\eta(\det g_{1}^{\prime})=K_{f^{+}_{{\dagger}}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})

for all
(h,g^{\prime})\in[H]\times[G^{\prime}]
. The lemma then follows directly from
Theorem
5.8
.
∎

6.2.
The coarse geometric expansion

We now develop the coarse geometric expansion.

Theorem 6.6
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

For
T
sufficiently positive, the expression

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\int_{[H]}\left|k_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}

is convergent and defines a continuous semi-norm on
\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
.

(2)

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
, we define

i_{\alpha}^{T}(f^{+})=\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\int_{[H]}k^{T}_{f^{+},\alpha}(h,g^{\prime})\eta_{G^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

Then as a function of
T
, when
T
is sufficiently positive,
i^{T}_{\alpha}(f)
is the restriction of a
polynomial exponential function whose purely polynomial part is a constant. We
denote this constant by
i_{\alpha}(f^{+})
.

(3)

The distribution
f^{+}\mapsto i_{\alpha}(f^{+})
satisfies the invariance
property that

i_{\alpha}((h,g^{\prime})\cdot f^{+})=\eta_{G^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})i_{\alpha}(f^{+}),

where the action
(h,g^{\prime})\cdot f^{+}
is induced from the
action (
6.1
).

Proof.

Recall that we introduced a lattice
\Lambda\subset F_{\infty}^{N}
, and defined functions
u_{\alpha}
and
f^{+}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Lambda
before Lemma
6.2
. By Proposition
3.27
,
(f^{+}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is absolutely summable in
\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
. Therefore by
Lemma
6.5
, we have

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f^{+}_{\alpha}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}<\infty.

By Lemma
6.2
, we have

K_{f^{+}_{\alpha}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})=K_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(h,g^{\prime}).

By the construction of the lattice
\Lambda
we conclude that

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\int_{[H]}\int_{[G^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f_{\alpha}}^{T}(h,g^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}g^{\prime}.

This proves the first assertion on absolute convergence. By the uniform boundedness principle, it defines a continuous semi-norm.

By
Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.3
we have

i^{T}_{\alpha}(f^{+})=i^{T}(f^{+}_{\alpha})=I^{T}((f^{+}_{\alpha})_{{\dagger}}).

This implies the second assertion. The third assertion follows from
Lemma
6.4
and
Proposition
5.12
.
∎

6.3.
Synthesis of the results: the coarse relative trace formula

We now summarize what we have done. For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
we put

I_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+})=i_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+}^{\dagger}),\quad I_{\alpha}(f_{+})=i_{\alpha}(f_{+}^{\dagger}).

Then Theorem
6.6
tells us that if
T
is sufficiently
positive then
I_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+})
is the restriction of a polynomial
exponential and its purely polynomial part is a constant that equals

I_{\alpha}(f_{+})
. By Lemma
6.4
, the distribution

I_{\alpha}
is left
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant and right

(G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}),\eta)
-equivariant in the sense of
Proposition
5.12
.

We summarize the coarse relative trace formulae on the general linear groups
as the following theorem.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G_{+}(\mathbb{A}))
be a test function. Then we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(G)}I_{\chi}(f_{+})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}I_{\alpha}(f_{+}).

The summations on both sides are absolutely convergent and each summand is left
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant and right

(G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}),\eta)
-equivariant.

This is simply a combination of
Theorem
5.10
,
Proposition
5.12
,
Lemma
6.5
and
Theorem
6.6
.

7.
The coarse spectral expansion: unitary groups

7.1.
Setup

The following notation will be used throughout this section.

•

Let
(V,q_{V})
be a nondegenerate skew-Hermitian space of dimension
n
. Put
\U_{V}=\U(V)\times\U(V)
. If
g\in\U_{V}
, without mentioning
explicitly the contrary, we will denote by
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
where

g_{i}\in\U(V)
. Let
\U^{\prime}_{V}\subset\U_{V}
denote the diagonal
subgroup, which is isomorphic to
\U(V)
.

•

Let
S(V)
be the Heisenberg group attached to
V
, and
J(V)=S(V)\rtimes\U(V)
be the Jacobi group (see Subsection
4.3
). Put
\widetilde{\U_{V}}=\U(V)\times J(V)
. If
g\in\U(V)
, an element in
J(V)
whose
image in
\U(V)
is
g
is usually denoted by
\widetilde{g}
.
An element in
\widetilde{\U_{V}}
is usually denoted by
\widetilde{x}
.
This means
\widetilde{x}=(x_{1},\widetilde{x_{2}})
,

x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\U_{V}
and the image of

\widetilde{x}
in
\U_{V}
is
x
.

•

The group
\U_{V}^{\prime}
diagonally embeds in
\widetilde{\U_{V}}
.
Its image is
again denoted by
\U_{V}^{\prime}
. There is a natural map
J(V)\to\widetilde{\U_{V}}
and we let
\widetilde{\U^{\prime}_{V}}
its image.

•

We keep the notation from Subsection
4.3
. In
particular, we fix a minimal parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
of
\U(V)

which fixes the maximal isotropic flag (
4.10
).
Standard parabolic subgroups of
\U(V)
are those containing
P_{0}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{V}
be the subset of standard
D-parabolic subgroups of
J(V)
, and let

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{V}
be the set of standard parabolic subgroup of
\U^{\prime}_{V}
. We put

P^{\prime}=P\cap\U(V)
for
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
. Then by
Lemma
4.7
, the map

P\mapsto P^{\prime}
is a bijection from
\mathcal{F}_{V}
to
\mathcal{F}_{V^{\prime}}
.

•

Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
. We denote by
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
respectively
the subgroup
P^{\prime}
of the first and second factor of
\U_{V}
. We put

(7.1)

\widetilde{P}=P_{1}\times P,\quad P_{\U}=P_{1}\times P_{2},

which are D-parabolic subgroups of
\widetilde{\U_{V}}
and

\U_{V}
respectively. The notation
P^{\prime}
usually specifically means
the parabolic subgroup of
\U_{V}^{\prime}
.

•

Let
\Res V
be the symplectic space defined in
Subsection
4.3
. We fix a polarization

\Res V=L\oplus L^{\vee}
as in Subsection
4.4
.
We have the Weil representation
\omega=\omega_{\psi,\mu}
of

J(V)(\mathbb{A})
realized on
\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. For
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
and
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
we have theta theta function
\prescript{}{P}{\theta}(\cdot,\phi)
. We also have

\omega^{\vee}=\omega_{\psi^{-1},\mu^{-1}}
and
\prescript{}{P}{\theta}^{\vee}(\cdot,\phi)
defined in terms of
\omega^{\vee}
.

•

Put
\U_{V,+}=\U_{V}\times L^{\vee}\times L^{\vee}
.
The group structure is given by the product group structure of

\U(V)
and the additive group
L^{\vee}\times L^{\vee}
. Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
.
We put

M_{P,+}=M_{P_{\U}}\times M_{L^{\vee}}\times M_{L^{\vee}},\quad N_{P,+}=N_{P_{\U}}\times N_{L^{\vee}}\times N_{L^{\vee}}=N_{P_{\U}},\quad P_{+}=M_{P,+}N_{P,+},

where we recall that
M_{L^{\vee}}
and
N_{L^{\vee}}
are defined in Subsection
4.4
.
These are subgroups of
\U_{V,+}
. We often write an element in
M_{P,+}

as
(m,l_{1},l_{2})
where
m\in M_{P_{\U}}
and
l_{1},l_{2}\in L^{\vee}
.

•

Put
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\mathfrak{a}_{P_{0}}
. A truncation parameter is an element in

\mathfrak{a}_{0}
.

7.2.
Technical preparations

Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
w
be a weight on
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}
.
We can define various function spaces as in
Subsection
3.3
. Of particular interest to us are

\mathcal{S}_{w}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)
and

\mathcal{T}_{w}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)
.

The “approximation by the constant term” for the group
\widetilde{\U_{V}}
takes the following form.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
N>0,r\geq 0
,
X\in\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}_{V}}_{\infty})
and
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
. Then there exists a continuous seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{N,X,r}
on

\mathcal{T}_{N}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{Q}},\psi)
, such that

\left|\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi(g)-\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{\widetilde{P}}(g)\right|\leq\|g\|^{N}_{P}d_{P_{\U}}^{Q_{\U}}(g)^{-r}\|\varphi\|_{N,X,r}

holds for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{Q}},\psi)
and

g\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
.

Proof.

For
g\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A})
, we have
d_{\widetilde{P}}^{\widetilde{Q}}(g)=d_{P_{1}}^{Q_{1}}(g_{1})d_{P}^{Q}(g_{2})
and
d_{P_{\U}}^{Q_{\U}}(g)=d_{P_{1}}^{Q_{1}}(g_{1})d_{P_{2}}^{Q_{2}}(g_{2})
.
Therefore by Lemma
4.8
, we have
\min\{d_{P_{\U}}^{Q_{\U}}(g),d_{P_{1}}^{Q_{1}}(g_{1})d_{P_{2}}^{Q_{2}}(g_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\}\ll d_{\widetilde{P}}^{\widetilde{Q}}(g)\ll d_{P_{\U}}^{Q_{\U}}(g)
,
and hence the proposition follows from Theorem
3.22
.
∎

Similarly to what we have done in the case of general linear groups, we introduce
various auxiliary spaces of functions.

Recall that we have the space of functions
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}^{\prime}}(\U^{\prime}_{V})
introduced in
Subsection
3.9
. Because there is a bijection between

\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
\mathcal{F}_{V}^{\prime}
, we denote it by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U^{\prime}_{V})
and use
\mathcal{F}_{V}

as indices. By Theorem
3.22
, the
space
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U^{\prime}_{V})
consists of tuples

(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\mathcal{T}([\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}),

such that

(7.2)

\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}-(\prescript{}{Q}{\varphi})_{P^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}}([\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}})

for any
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
({}_{P}\varphi)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
,
then the family of products

P\mapsto({}_{P}\varphi\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi)})

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.9
and the above
characterization (
7.2
), the family
P\mapsto\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi)}
belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
. We thus
prove a stronger statement: if
(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})
,
(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
, then the family

P\mapsto(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\prescript{}{P}{\varphi^{\prime}})

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
. Using the Leibniz rule, we are reduced to
that there exists
N_{0}>0
such that for any
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}((\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{V})_{\infty})

and any
r>0
, we have

\left|\mathrm{R}(X){}_{P}\varphi(g)\mathrm{R}(Y){}_{P}\varphi^{\prime}(g)-\mathrm{R}(X){}_{Q}\varphi(g)\mathrm{R}(Y){}_{Q}\varphi^{\prime}(g)\right|\ll_{X,Y,r}\|g\|_{P^{\prime}}^{N}d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(g)^{-r}

for any
g\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}
. The left hand side is bounded by

\left|\mathrm{R}(X){}_{P}\varphi(g)\left(\mathrm{R}(Y){}_{P}\varphi^{\prime}(g)-\mathrm{R}(Y){}_{Q}\varphi^{\prime}(g)\right)\right|+\left|\mathrm{R}(Y){}_{Q}\varphi^{\prime}(g)\left(\mathrm{R}(X){}_{P}\varphi(g)-\mathrm{R}(X){}_{Q}\varphi(g)\right)\right|.

The desired inequality then follows.
∎

For
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, define a weight function
\Delta_{P}
on
[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
by

\Delta_{P}(g)=\inf_{\gamma\in M_{P^{\prime}}(F)N_{P^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})}\lVert g_{1}^{-1}\gamma g_{2}\rVert_{P^{\prime}}.

For
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
with
P\subset Q
, define a weight

d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}
on
[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
by

d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)=\min\{d_{P_{1}}^{Q_{1}}(g_{1}),d_{P_{2}}^{Q_{2}}(g_{2})\}.

Pulling back under the projection
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}\to[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
, we get two weights on
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}
,
which are still denoted by
\Delta_{P}
and
d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}
.

We define the space
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}^{\Delta}(\U_{V})
(resp.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}^{\Delta}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi)
) to be the space of functions

(7.3)

(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi})\in\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}})\quad\text{resp.}\quad\prod_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

such that for any
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, we have

{}_{P}\varphi-({}_{Q}\varphi)_{P}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}})\quad\text{resp.}\quad{}_{P}\varphi-({}_{Q}\varphi)_{\widetilde{P}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi).

Since
d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}\ll d_{P}^{Q}
, by
Theorem
3.22
and
Proposition
7.1
respectively,
that a family
({}_{P}\varphi)
belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}^{\Delta}(\U_{V})
(resp.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}^{\Delta}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi)
) is equivalent to

\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}})
(resp.

\mathcal{S}_{\Delta_{P}}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)
for each
P
and that
for all
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, there exists an
N>0
such that for all
X\in\mathcal{U}((\mathfrak{u}_{V})_{\infty})
and all
r>0
, we have

(7.4)

\left|\mathrm{R}(X){}_{P}\varphi(g)-\mathrm{R}(X){}_{Q}\varphi(g)\right|\ll_{r,X}\|g\|_{P}^{N}d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}(g)^{-r}.

holds for all
g\in[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
(resp.
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}
).

Lemma 7.3
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

For
({}_{P}\varphi)\in\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi)
, then
the family of restrictions
P\mapsto({}_{P}\varphi|_{[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}})

belong to
\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V})
.

(2)

For
({}_{P}\varphi)\in\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V})
, then the family of
restrictions
P\to({}_{P}\varphi|_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}})

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The first follows from the characterizations (
7.4
)
of elements in
\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V})
and

\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi)
. The second follows from the
additional fact that
d_{P}^{Q,\Delta}|_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}}=d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
∎

We fix a
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
such that
\lVert\phi_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
. For

f\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}))
and
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
, we define a function

\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{\U_{V}}(\mathbb{A}),\psi)
by

(7.5)

\widetilde{f}(gs)=f(g^{-1})\langle\omega(s)\phi_{0},\overline{\phi_{1}}\rangle,\quad s\in S(V)(\mathbb{A}),\ g\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}).

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([\U_{V}]_{P_{V}})
, as in the case of general linear groups, we define a measure
\varphi\cdot\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{0})}}\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}},\psi)
by

(7.6)

\langle\varphi\cdot\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{0})}},\beta\rangle=\int_{[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{P_{\U}}}\int_{[S(V)]_{P_{S}}}\beta(sg)\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(sg,\phi_{0})}}\mathrm{d}s\varphi(g),

where
\beta\in C_{c}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}})
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([\U_{V}^{\prime}])
, then the family

P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}\mapsto\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})\left(\varphi_{P^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{0})}}\right)

belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi^{-1})
. Moreover if

y\in[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
we have

\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})(\varphi\cdot\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{0})}})(y)=\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}}K_{f,P}(x,y)\theta^{\vee}(x,\phi_{1})\varphi(x).

In particular the composition of this map followed by the restriction to

[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
is independent from the choice of
\phi_{0}
.

Proof.

This is proved in the same way as in the case of general linear groups, and in
particular Lemma
5.3
,
Lemma
5.5
and
Lemma
5.7
.
∎

7.3.
A modified kernel

Let
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
be a cuspidal datum. For
f_{+}=f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
as above, we put

(7.7)

K_{f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2},P,\chi}(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})=K_{f,P_{\U},\chi}(x,y)\prescript{}{P}{\theta}^{\vee}(\widetilde{x}_{2},\phi_{1})\prescript{}{P}{\theta}(\widetilde{y}_{2},\phi_{2}),\quad\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y}\in[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}.

Where we denote
x,y
to be the image of
\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y}
in
[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}
respectively and
\widetilde{x}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{2}\in[J(V)]
are the second component of
\widetilde{x}
and
\widetilde{y}
.
Let us now explain that the function
K_{f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2},P,\chi}
extends continuously to a smooth function
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
for
all
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
. By Lemma
3.20
and
Proposition
4.9
, there exists an
N_{0}>0
such that for
any
N>0
and
X\in\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{u}}_{V,\infty})
, there is a continuous semi-norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that

(7.8)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U)}\left\lvert\mathrm{R}_{\widetilde{y}}(X)K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\right\rvert\leq\|f_{+}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\|\widetilde{x}\|_{\widetilde{P}}^{-N}\lVert\widetilde{y}\rVert_{\widetilde{P}}^{N+N_{0}}.

holds for all
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
(algebraic tensor product). Now let
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
f_{+,n}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
a sequence of functions approaching
f_{+}
. Because of the
estimate (
7.8
), the sequence

K_{f_{+,n},P,\chi}

is convergent to a function on
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}\times[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}
, and this convergence is locally
uniform for
(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\in[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}\times[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\widetilde{P}}
. We denote this function by
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
. It is clearly independent of the choice of the sequence approximating

f_{+}
. Because the convergence is locally uniform,
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
is a
smooth function. Moreover the estimate (
7.8
)
continues to hold for
K_{f_{+},P,\chi}
. By the symmetry of
\widetilde{x}

and
\widetilde{y}
, the estimate (
7.8
) holds when

\widetilde{x}
and
\widetilde{y}
on the right hand side are switched (and with a possibly different
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S}}
).

For
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
,
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
, and
x,y\in[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]
, we
define modified kernels

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(x,y)=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma x,\delta y).

and

K_{f_{+}}^{T}(x,y)=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})K_{f_{+},P}(\gamma x,\delta y).

Here
\epsilon_{P}=(-1)^{\dim\mathfrak{a}_{P^{\prime}}}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}
is the
characteristic function of a certain cone in
\mathfrak{a}_{P^{\prime}}
defined in
Subsection
3.5
. In these definitions, the
convergence of the inner sum can be seen as follows. For fixed
x
and
y
,
there are only finitely many
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)
(depending
on
y
) such that
\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})\neq 0
,
cf.
[
1
]
*Lemma 5.1. Hence the
estimate (
7.8
) implies that the summations defining
K_{f_{+}}^{T}
and

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}
are absolutely convergent.

Proposition 7.5
.

For every
N>0
, there exists a continuous semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S},N}
on

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that for every
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and

T
sufficiently positive, we have

(7.9)

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(x,y)-K_{f_{+},\chi}(x,y)F^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}(x,T)\right|\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\|x\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N}\|y\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N}\|f_{+}\|_{\mathcal{S},N}.

In particular for
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T
sufficiently positive, the
expression

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(x,y)\right|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is finite and defines a continuous semi-norm on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
.

Proof.

First, since the center of
\U^{\prime}_{V}
is anisotropic, for a fixed
T
the
function
x\mapsto F^{\U^{\prime}_{V}}(x,T)
is compactly supported. Therefore the
second assertion on the absolute convergence follows from the
estimate (
7.9
).

Next we note that each summand in (
7.9
) is
continuous in
f_{+}
. Thus by continuity we only need to
prove (
7.9
) when
f_{+}=f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
where
f\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}))
,
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
. We will assume that this is the case from now on.

We fix a
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
with
\lVert\phi_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
. Consider
the following sequence of map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}^{0}([\U_{V}^{\prime}])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\widetilde{\U_{V}},\psi^{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\Delta}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U^{\prime}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}}(\U^{\prime}_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{0}([\U^{\prime}_{V}])},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|_{\U_{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|_{\U^{\prime}_{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi^{T}}

where the first map sends
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([\U_{V}^{\prime}])
to the family

P\mapsto\mathrm{R}(\widetilde{f})\left(\varphi_{P^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{0})}}\right).

We recall that
\widetilde{f}
is defined in (
7.5
).
The second and the third maps are to restrict the family
\left(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\right)_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}
to

\left(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}|_{[\U_{V}]_{P_{\U}}}\right)_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}
and then
further to
\left(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}|_{[\U^{\prime}_{V}]_{P^{\prime}}}\right)_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}
. The fourth map sends a family
(\prescript{}{P^{\prime}}{\varphi})_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}
to the family
\left(\prescript{}{P}{\varphi}\prescript{}{P}{\theta(\cdot,\phi_{2})}\right)_{P\in{\mathcal{F}_{V}}}
. By
Lemma
7.4
,
Lemma
7.3
and
Lemma
7.2
, the targets of these maps are as
described. Using the closed graph theorem, one checks that all these maps are
continuous, cf. Remark
5.9
.
The last map is one of the truncation operators defined in
Proposition
3.24
.
We denote by
L_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}
(resp.
P_{f,\phi_{1},\phi}
) the composite of the sequence of the maps
above, where we use
\Lambda^{T}
(resp.
\Pi^{T}
) in the last step.

Similarly, for
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
, we consider the same chain of continuous
linear maps as above, but we project to the
\chi^{\vee}
-component before
restricting to
\U_{V}^{\prime}
. The resulting maps are denoted by

L_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\chi}
and
P_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\chi}
respectively.

As in the proof of Theorem
5.8
, the functions

K^{T}_{f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}}(x,y)
and
K_{f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}}(x,y)F^{\U^{\prime}_{V}}(x,T)
are the kernel functions of
L_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}

(resp.
P_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}
) respectively. The same is true with a

\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
in the subscript. By Proposition

3.24
, for any fixed
N
and for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([\U^{\prime}_{V}])
, we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(U)}\|L_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\chi}(\varphi)-P_{f,\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\chi}(\varphi)\|\ll e^{-N\|T\|}\|\varphi\|_{1,-N}.

The rest of the argument is to show that the implicit constant in this
estimate can be taken to be continuous seminorms of
f
,
\phi_{1}
and

\phi_{2}
. This is a consequence of the uniform boundedness principle, and the
argument is the same as the proof of Theorem
5.8
.
∎

7.4.
The coarse spectral expansion

For
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
,
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
a truncation parameter, we put

J^{T}(f_{+})=\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+}}^{T}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,\quad J^{T}_{\chi}(f_{+})=\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

By Proposition
7.5
, these integrals are
absolutely convergent when
T
is sufficiently positive.

We define an action
\mathrm{L}_{+}
of
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
by

\mathrm{L}_{+}(h)f_{+}(m,l_{1},l_{2})=\left(\omega^{\vee}(h)f_{+}(h^{-1}m,\cdot,l_{2})\right)(l_{1}),\quad m\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}),\ l_{1},l_{2}\in L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}).

The right hand side means that we first evaluate
f_{+}
at
h^{-1}m
and
l_{2}

to obtain a Schwartz function in the variable
l_{1}
. We apply the Weil
representation
\omega^{\vee}(h)
to this Schwartz function and finally
evaluate at
l_{1}
. We similarly define an action
\mathrm{R}_{+}
of

\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
by

\mathrm{R}_{+}(h)f_{+}(m,l_{1},l_{2})=\left(\omega(h)f_{+}(mh,l_{1},\cdot)\right)(l_{2}),\quad m\in\U_{V}(\mathbb{A}),\ l_{1},l_{2}\in L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}).

The right hand side is interpreted similarly as in the case
\mathrm{L}_{+}
.

Using the action
L_{+}
and
R_{+}
, for
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
, the kernel function
K_{f_{+},P}
can also be written as

(7.10)

K_{f_{+},P}(x,y)=\sum_{m\in M_{P_{+}}(F)}\int_{N_{P_{+}}(\mathbb{A})}\mathrm{L}_{+}(x)\mathrm{R}_{+}(y)f_{+}(mn)\mathrm{d}n

where
x,y\in[\U^{\prime}_{V}]
.

Theorem 7.6
.

As a function of
T
, the functions
J^{T}(f_{+})
and
J^{T}_{\chi}(f_{+})
are the restrictions of
exponential polynomials whose purely polynomial term are constants denoted by

J(f_{+})
and
J_{\chi}(f_{+})
respectively. The linear forms
f_{+}\mapsto J(f_{+})
and
f_{+}\mapsto J_{\chi}(f_{+})
are continuous and bi-
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
-invariant, i.e.

J_{\chi}(\mathrm{L}_{+}(h_{1})\mathrm{R}_{+}(h_{2})f_{+})=J_{\chi}(f_{+}),\quad J(\mathrm{L}_{+}(h_{1})\mathrm{R}_{+}(h_{2})f_{+})=J(f_{+}),\quad h_{1},h_{2}\in\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}).

Finally we have

J(f_{+})=\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}J_{\chi}(f_{+})

where the sum is absolutely convergent.

Before we delve into the proof of this theorem, let us first explain a
variant of the construction of the modified kernel for parabolic subgroups.
Let us take
Q=M_{Q}N_{Q}\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
. Then
Q_{\U}=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
where
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
are parabolic subgroup of
\U(V)
and
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}=Q_{2}
is a parabolic
subgroup of
\U_{V}^{\prime}
. Assume that
Q^{\prime}
is the stabilizer of the isotropic flag

(7.11)

0=X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset\cdots\subset X_{r}

in
V
. Recall that we constructed a decomposition

V=X\oplus V^{\prime}\oplus X^{\vee}
in
Subsection
4.3
, where
X=X_{r}
,

V^{\prime}=M_{Q_{V}}
, and
V^{\prime}
is perpendicular to
X\oplus X^{\vee}
. We have a polarizations
\Res V=L\oplus L^{\vee}
and another polarization
\Res V^{\prime}=L^{\prime}\oplus L^{\prime\vee}
where
L^{\prime}=V^{\prime}\cap L
and
L^{\prime\vee}=V^{\prime}\cap L^{\vee}
. In particular
L=L^{\prime}\oplus X

and
L^{\vee}=L^{\prime\vee}+X^{\vee}
.

The Levi subgroup
M_{Q^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to

\prod_{i=1}^{r}\GL(X_{i}/X_{i-1})\times\U(V_{0}).

We write
m\in M_{Q^{\prime}}
as
(m_{\bullet},m_{*})
where
m_{\bullet}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})
,
m_{i}\in\GL(X_{i}/X_{i-1})
, and
m_{*}\in\U(V_{0})
. Recall that in Subsection
7.1
, we have defined

M_{Q_{2},+}=M_{Q_{2}}\times M_{Q_{L^{\vee}}}\times M_{Q_{L^{\vee}}}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\GL(X_{i}/X_{i-1})\times\U(V^{\prime})\right)\times L^{\prime\vee}\times L^{\prime\vee}.

We have the D-Levi component
M_{\widetilde{Q}}=M_{Q_{1}}\times M_{Q}

of
\widetilde{Q}
, where

M_{Q}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\GL(X_{i}/X_{i-1})\times J(V^{\prime})

is the D-Levi component of
Q
. If
\widetilde{m}\in M_{Q}
, we still denote by
\widetilde{m}_{*}
its component
in
J(V_{0})
.

Take a
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
with
P\subset Q
. Then
P\cap M_{Q_{\U}}
is a parabolic subgroup of
M_{Q_{\U}}
. Denote temporarily by
\iota_{Q_{\U}}:\mathfrak{X}(M_{Q_{\U}})\to\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})

the natural finite-to-one map. For
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{\U}}(\mathbb{A}))
, we put

K_{f,P\cap M_{Q_{\U}},\chi}=\sum_{\chi^{\prime}\in\iota_{Q_{\U}}^{-1}(\chi)}K_{f,P\cap M_{Q_{\U}},\chi^{\prime}}

where
K_{f,P\cap M_{Q_{\U}},\chi^{\prime}}
is the kernel function on
M_{Q_{\U}}
.
Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\prime\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
and
f_{+}^{\prime}=f\otimes\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q,+}(\mathbb{A}))
be a test function.
We define

K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}(x,y)=K_{f,P\cap M_{Q_{\U}},\chi}(x,y)\cdot{}_{P\cap J(V^{\prime})}\theta^{\vee}(x,\phi_{1})\cdot{}_{P\cap J(V^{\prime})}\theta(y,\phi_{2})\mu^{-1}(\det x_{\bullet})\mu(\det y_{\bullet}),

where
(x,y)\in[M_{Q^{\prime}}]_{P^{\prime}\cap M_{Q^{\prime}}}\times[M_{Q^{\prime}}]_{P^{\prime}\cap M_{Q^{\prime}}}
, and the intersection
P\cap J(V^{\prime})
is taken inside
J(V)
.
As in
Subsection
7.3
, we define
K_{f^{\prime}_{+},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}
for all
f^{\prime}_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
by continuity.

For
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, define

K_{f^{\prime}_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(x,y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\\
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{P}^{Q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in(M_{Q^{\prime}}\cap P^{\prime})(F)\backslash M_{Q^{\prime}}(F)\\
\delta\in(M_{Q^{\prime}}\cap P^{\prime})(F)\backslash M_{Q^{\prime}}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})K_{f_{+}^{\prime},P,\chi}(\gamma x,\delta y).

where
(x,y)\in[M_{Q^{\prime}}]\times[M_{Q^{\prime}}]
. Using similar methods as the
proof of Proposition
7.5
, we can show
that when
T
is sufficiently positive, for any
f_{+}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
,
the integral

\int_{A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q^{\prime}}]\times[M_{Q^{\prime}}]}\left|K_{f^{\prime}_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(x,y)\right|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is convergent and defines a continuous seminorm on
\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
. Here
A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\infty}
embeds in
M_{Q^{\prime}}\times M_{Q^{\prime}}
diagonally. We
thus define a distribution

J_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(f_{+}^{\prime})=\int_{A_{Q^{\prime}}^{\infty}\backslash[M_{Q^{\prime}}]\times[M_{Q^{\prime}}]}K_{f^{\prime}_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Proof of Theorem
7.6
.

We define
\underline{\rho}_{Q}
be the unique element in
\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}^{*}
such that for any
m\in M_{Q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})
we have

e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q^{\prime}}(m)\rangle}=\lvert\det m_{\bullet}\rvert.

One check directly that
\underline{\rho}_{Q}
concides with the definition of
[
47
]
*Lemma 4.3.

By
[
3
]
*Section 2, there exist functions
\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}}
on

\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}\times\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}
, for
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, that are
compactly supported in the first variable when the second variable stays in a
compact and such that

(7.12)

\widehat{\tau}_{Q^{\prime}}(H-X)=\sum_{P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\epsilon_{Q^{\prime}}^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(H)\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}}(H,X).

Define a function
p_{Q}
on
\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}
by

(7.13)

p_{Q}(X)=\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q},H\rangle}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}}(H,X)\mathrm{d}H.

By
[
47
]
*Lemma 4.3
p_{Q}
is an exponential polynomial on

\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}
with exponents contained in the set
\{\underline{\rho}_{R}\mid R\supset Q\}
and the pure polynomial term is the constant
\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\theta}_{Q^{\prime}}(\underline{\rho}_{Q})^{-1}
where
\widehat{\theta}_{Q^{\prime}}
is a homogeneous polynomial on
\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\prime}}
defined
in
[
3
]
*Section 2.

For
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V},f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
, define

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T}(x,y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\\
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{P}^{Q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash Q^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash Q^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(\gamma x,\delta y),

where
(x,y)\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}
. Here
\epsilon_{P}^{Q}=(-1)^{\dim\mathfrak{a}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}
is the characteristic
function defined in Subsection
3.5
.
Using the inversion formula (
7.12
), for
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
we have

K_{f_{+},\chi}^{T}(x,y)=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in Q^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in Q^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}}(H_{Q^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}},T_{Q^{\prime}}-T^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}})K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T^{\prime}}(\gamma x,\delta y).

We now relate
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{Q,T^{\prime}}
to
K_{f_{+},\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}
via parabolic
descent. For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
, we define its parabolic descent as

f_{+,Q}(m,l_{1},l_{2})=e^{\langle-2\rho_{Q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q^{\prime}}(m_{1})\rangle}\int_{K^{\prime}}\int_{K^{\prime}}\int_{N_{Q_{\U}}(\mathbb{A})}\left(\mathrm{L}_{+}(k_{1})\mathrm{R}_{+}(k_{2})f_{+}\right)(mn_{Q},l_{1},l_{2})\mathrm{d}n_{Q}\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2},

where
m\in M_{Q_{\U}}(\mathbb{A})
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in L^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
. The element
m_{1}
stands for the first component of
m
and we regard it as an element of
M_{Q^{\prime}}
under the natural identification
M_{Q_{1}}\cong M_{Q^{\prime}}
. We have
f_{+,Q}\in\mathcal{S}(M_{Q_{+}}(\mathbb{A}))
, and for
x,y\in[M_{Q^{\prime}}]
and
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, we
have

\int_{K^{\prime}}\int_{K^{\prime}}K_{f_{+},P,\chi}(xk_{1},yk_{2})\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}=e^{\langle 2\rho_{Q^{\prime}},H_{Q^{\prime}}(x)\rangle}e^{\langle 2\rho_{Q^{\prime}}+\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q^{\prime}}(y)\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},P\cap M_{Q},\chi}(x,y).

Indeed using (
7.10
) and the mixed model described in Subsection
4.4
, direct calculations give the identity without
\chi
. The argument
in
[
47
]
*Lemma 1.3 shows that this implies the identity with the

\chi
. It follows that

\int_{K^{\prime}}\int_{K^{\prime}}K^{T}_{f_{+},\chi}(xk_{1},yk_{2})\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}=e^{\langle 2\rho_{Q^{\prime}},H_{Q^{\prime}}(x)\rangle}e^{\langle 2\rho_{Q^{\prime}}+\underline{\rho}_{Q},H_{Q^{\prime}}(y)\rangle}K_{f_{+,Q},\chi}^{M_{Q},T}(x,y).

The rest of the calculation is the same as that in the proof of
Theorem
5.10
. We omit the details and only record the final
outcome. We have

J_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q^{\prime}},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T_{Q^{\prime}}-T^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}})J_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(f_{+,Q}).

It is an exponential polynomial in
T
whose purely polynomial term is a constant that equals

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\epsilon_{Q}\widehat{\theta}_{Q^{\prime}}(\underline{\rho}_{Q})^{-1}e^{\langle\underline{\rho}_{Q^{\prime}},T^{\prime}\rangle}p_{Q}(T_{Q^{\prime}}-T^{\prime}_{Q^{\prime}})J_{\chi}^{M_{Q},T^{\prime}}(\widetilde{f_{+,Q}}).

The invariance of the linear form
J_{\chi}
is shown as that of
Proposition
5.12
. This concludes the proof.
∎

7.5.
An alternative truncation operator

To compute explicit spectral terms of the relative trace formula in
[
11
]
, we require a new truncation operator for Fourier-Jacobi periods. As its construction relies on the machinery developed herein, we present it in this independent subsection. This operator serves as an analogue to the regularized periods introduced by Ichino and Yamana
[
22
]
.

Take
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
. Then
\U(V)\times P
is a D-parabolic subgroup of

\widetilde{\U_{V}}
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
, we can speak of the constant term

\varphi_{\U(V)\times P}
. This amounts to viewing the function

\varphi
as a function in two variables
(x_{1},\widetilde{x_{2}})\in[\U(V)]\times[J(V)]
, fixing

x_{1}
, and taking the constant term along
P
in the second variable

\widetilde{x_{2}}
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
and
x\in[\U^{\prime}_{V}]
, we
define

(7.14)

\Lambda^{T}_{u}\varphi(x)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U^{\prime}_{V}(F)}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta x)-T_{P^{\prime}})\varphi_{\U(V)\times P}(\delta x).

Proposition 7.7
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

\Lambda_{u}^{T}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{0}([\U^{\prime}_{V}])
. And the map
\varphi\mapsto\Lambda_{u}^{T}\varphi
induces a continuous map

\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)\to\mathcal{S}^{0}([\U_{V}^{\prime}])
.

(2)

For every
N>0
, there exists a continious seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{N}
on

\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
such that for all
x\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]
and

\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
, we have

\left|\Lambda^{T}_{u}\varphi(x)-F^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}(x,T)\varphi(x)\right|\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\|x\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{N}.

Proof.

First note
d_{\U(V)\times P}^{\U(V)\times Q}|_{\U^{\prime}_{V}}=d_{P}^{Q}
. Thus by Theorem
3.22
and
Lemma
4.8
, there exists an
N_{0}>0
such that for any
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
X\in\mathcal{U}((\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{V})_{\infty})
, we have

(7.15)

\lvert\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{\U(V)\times P}(x)-\mathrm{R}(X)\varphi_{\U(V)\times Q}(x)\rvert\ll d_{P^{\prime}}^{Q^{\prime}}(x)^{-r}\|x\|_{P^{\prime}}^{N_{0}}.

By (
7.2
),
this is equivalent to the fact that the family
P\mapsto\left(\varphi_{\U(V)\times P}\right)|_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}}

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{F}_{V}^{\prime}}(\U_{V}^{\prime})
. The second assertion follows from this by Proposition
3.24
. The first assertion follows from the second since the function
x\mapsto F^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}(x,T)
is compactly supported.
∎

Recall that for
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})
, we have a
kernel function
K_{f_{+},\chi}(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})
for

\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y}\in[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]
(cf. (
7.7
) for the definition for pure tensors). Applying
\Lambda_{u}^{T}
to
the second variable, we get a function on
[\widetilde{\U_{V}}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]

denoted by
K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda^{T}_{u}
.

Proposition 7.8
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
T
sufficiently positive,
the expression

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda^{T}_{u}(x,y)\right|\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is finite and defines a continuous seminorm on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
.

(2)

For any
r>0
, there exists a continuous seminorm

\lVert\cdot\rVert
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that

\left|J_{\chi}^{T}(f_{+})-\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda^{T}_{u}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq e^{-r\|T\|}\|f_{+}\|.

for all
T
sufficiently positive and
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
. In particular, the absolutely convergent integral

\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda^{T}_{u}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is asymptotic to an exponential-polynomial in
T
, whose purely
polynomial term is a constant that equals
J_{\chi}(f_{+})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.7
, for every
N>0
there exists a
continuous semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{T}_{N}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
such for all
x,y\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]
, we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda_{u}^{T}(x,y)-F^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}(y,T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(x,y)\right|\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\|y\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N}\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\|K_{f_{+},\chi}(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{T}},

where
K_{f_{+},\chi}(x,\cdot)
is regarded as an element in
\mathcal{T}_{N}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
.
By (
7.8
), there exists
N_{0}>0
and a continuous
semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S}}
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\|K_{f_{+},\chi}(\widetilde{x},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{T}}\ll\|f_{+}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\|\widetilde{x}\|_{\widetilde{\U_{V}}}^{-N+N_{0}}.

Combining the above two equations we obtain

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}\left|K_{f_{+},\chi}\Lambda_{u}^{T}(x,y)-F^{\U_{V}^{\prime}}(y,T)K_{f_{+},\chi}(x,y)\right|\leq e^{-N\|T\|}\|f_{+}\|_{\mathcal{S}}\|x\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N+N_{0}}\|y\|_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{-N}.

This proves the first assertion. The second assertion follows from the first
and Proposition
7.5
.
∎

We also introduce a variant of
\Lambda_{u}^{T}
for parabolic subgroup. Let
R\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}]_{\U(V)\times R},\psi)
and
x\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{R^{\prime}}
, we define

\Lambda^{R,T}_{u}\varphi(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}\\
P\subset R\end{subarray}}\epsilon_{P}^{R}\sum_{\delta\in{P^{\prime}(F)\backslash R^{\prime}(F)}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}^{R^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta x)-T_{P^{\prime}})\varphi_{\U(V)\times P}(\delta x).

It has similar properties as listed in Proposition
7.7
and moreover, for
T
and
T^{\prime}
sufficiently positive and
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}([\widetilde{\U_{V}}],\psi)
, we have

(7.16)
