\Lambda^{T+T^{\prime}}_{u}\varphi(x)=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\sum_{\gamma\in R^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U^{\prime}(F)}\Gamma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}(H_{R^{\prime}}(\gamma x)-T_{R^{\prime}},T^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}})\Lambda^{R,T}_{u}\varphi_{\U(V)\times R}(\gamma x),

where
\Gamma^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
is a function on
\mathfrak{a}_{R^{\prime}}\times\mathfrak{a}_{R^{\prime}}
introduced in
[
3
]
*Section 2.

8.
The coarse geometric expansion: unitary groups

8.1.
Geometric modified kernels

We keep the notation from the previous section. We also need the following
additional notation.

•

We put
\U_{V}^{+}=\U_{V}\times V
. The group structure is given by the
product of those of
\U_{V}
and of the additive group
V
. Note that this is not a subgroup
of
\widetilde{\U_{V}}
but merely a closed subvariety.
The group
\U_{V}^{\prime}\times\U_{V}^{\prime}

acts on
\U_{V}^{+}
from the right by
(g,v)\cdot(x,y)=(x^{-1}gy,y^{-1}v)
.

•

Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, we put

P^{+}=P_{\U}\times P_{V},\quad M_{P}^{+}=M_{P_{\U}}\times M_{P_{V}},\quad N_{P}^{+}=N_{P_{\U}}\times N_{P_{V}}.

These are subgroups of
\U_{V}^{+}
.

•

By
[
12
]
*Lemma 15.1.4.1 the categorical quotient
\U_{V}^{+}//(\U^{\prime}_{V}\times\U^{\prime}_{V})
is canonically identified with
\mathcal{A}=G^{+}//(H\times G^{\prime})
. The canonical morphism
q_{V}:\U_{V}^{+}\to\mathcal{A}
is given by

((g_{1},g_{2}),v)\mapsto(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n};b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})

where

a_{i}=\Trace\wedge^{i}(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}),\quad b_{i}=2(-1)^{n-1}\tau^{-1}q_{V}(g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}v,v).

Here
\mathcal{A}
is viewed as a locally closed subscheme of
\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
as in Subsection
6.1
.

•

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
, let
\U^{+}_{V,\alpha}
be the preimage of
\alpha
in

\U^{+}_{V}
as a closed subscheme. We also put

M^{+}_{P,\alpha}=\U^{+}_{V,\alpha}\cap M^{+}_{P}.

For
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, we define

k_{f^{+},P}(x,y)=\sum_{m^{+}\in M_{P}^{+}(F)}\int_{N_{P}^{+}(\mathbb{A})}f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(x,y))\mathrm{d}n^{+},\quad x,y\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}.

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
, we define similarly

k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(x,y)=\sum_{m^{+}\in M_{P,\alpha}^{+}(F)}\int_{N_{P}^{+}(\mathbb{A})}f^{+}(m^{+}n^{+}\cdot(x,y))\mathrm{d}n^{+}.

Lemma 8.1
.

There are an integer
N
and a semi-norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}
on

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
such that for all

\sum_{m\in M_{P}^{+}(F)}\int_{N_{P}^{+}(\mathbb{A})}\left\lvert f^{+}(mn\cdot(x,y))\right\rvert\mathrm{d}n\leq\lVert f^{+}\rVert_{\mathcal{S}}\lVert x\rVert_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{N}\lVert y\rVert_{\U_{V}^{\prime}}^{N}.

In particular the defining expressions of
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
and
k_{f^{+},P}
are absolutely convergent and we have

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(x,y)=k_{f^{+},P}(x,y).

Proof.

The proof is the same as
Lemma
6.1
.
∎

Similar to what we have done in Subsection
6.1
, we identify
\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{A}_{2n,E}
with the affine space
\mathbf{A}^{4n}
over
F
, and denote again by
q_{V}
the morphism
\U_{V}^{+}\to\mathbf{A}^{4n}
. We extend the definition of
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
to all
\alpha\in F^{4n}
by setting
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}=0
if
\alpha\not\in\mathcal{A}(F)
.
There is a
d\in F^{\times}
such that if
k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}
is not identically zero for some
\alpha\in F^{4n}
and
P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}
, then
\alpha\in(d\mathcal{O}_{F})^{4n}
. Put
\Lambda=(d\mathcal{O}_{F})^{4n}\subset F^{4n}
. For each

\alpha\in\Lambda
, take
u_{\alpha}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(F^{4n}_{\infty})
and define
the function
f^{+}_{\alpha}=f^{+}u_{\alpha}
as in
Subsection
3.10
.

Lemma 8.2
.

We have

k_{f^{+}_{\alpha},P}(x,y)=k_{f^{+}_{\alpha},P,\alpha}(x,y)=k_{f^{+},P,\alpha}(x,y)

for all
x,y\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}
.

The proof is the same as
Lemma
6.2
.

We now relate the kernel function
k_{f^{+},P}
and the kernel function

K_{f_{+},P}
defined in Subsection
7.3
. We
first define a partial Fourier transform, cf.
[
29
]
*Section 2.
For
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))
we define a partial Fourier transform

(8.1)

\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\otimes\mathcal{S}(L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A}))\to\mathcal{S}(V(\mathbb{A})),\quad\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}\mapsto(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\ddagger},

by

(8.2)

(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{{\ddagger}}(v)=\int_{L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})}\phi_{1}(x+l^{\prime})\phi_{2}(x-l^{\prime})\psi(-2\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(x,l))\mathrm{d}x,

where we write
v=l+l^{\prime}
where
l\in L(\mathbb{A})
and

L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})
. In particular we have

(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{{\ddagger}}(0)=\langle\phi_{1},\overline{\phi_{2}}\rangle_{L^{2}}

where
\langle-,-\rangle_{L^{2}}
stands for the
L^{2}
-inner product on
L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})
.
We also have

(\omega^{\vee}(g)\phi_{1}\otimes\omega(g)\phi_{2})^{\ddagger}(v)=(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\ddagger}(g^{-1}v)

for
g\in\U(V)(\mathbb{A})
and
v\in V(\mathbb{A})
.

The partial Fourier transform (
8.1
) extends to a
continuous isomorphism

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))\to\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A})),

which we still denote by
-^{\ddagger}
. Let
f^{+}\mapsto f^{+}_{{\ddagger}}
be its
inverse. Concretely for
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
,
we have

(8.3)

f_{+}^{\ddagger}((g_{1},g_{2}),v)=\int_{L^{\vee}}\omega^{\vee}_{(1)}(g_{1})f_{+}((g_{1},g_{2}),x+l^{\prime},x-l^{\prime})\psi(-2\Tr_{E/F}q_{V}(x,l))\mathrm{d}x,

where we write
v=l+l^{\prime}
where
l\in L(\mathbb{A})
and

L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})
. The notation is interpreted as follows. We first evaluate
f_{+}
at

(g_{1},g_{2})
to obtain a Schwartz function on
L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})\times L^{\vee}(\mathbb{A})
.
Then the Weil representation
\omega^{\vee}(g_{1})
acts on this Schwartz function
on the first variable (this is what the subscript
(1)
indicates).
Finally we take the partial Fourier transform.

Lemma 8.3
.

For all
x,y\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]_{P^{\prime}}
, we have

K_{f_{+},P}(x,y)=k_{f_{+}^{\dagger},P}(x,y).

The proof is the same as Lemma
6.3
.

Recall that we have defined in Subsection
7.4
the
actions
\mathrm{L}_{+}
and
\mathrm{R}_{+}
of
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
on
\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
. We also have the left and right translation of
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
on

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
which we denote by

\mathrm{L}^{+}
and
\mathrm{R}^{+}
respectively. More precisely we have

\mathrm{L}^{+}(h)f^{+}(g,v)=f^{+}(h^{-1}g,v),\quad\mathrm{R}^{+}(h)f^{+}(g,v)=f^{+}(gh,h^{-1}v),

for
h\in\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
f^{+}\in\mathcal{S}(G^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
and
x,y\in\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
. We have

(\mathrm{L}^{+}(x)\mathrm{R}^{+}(y)f^{+})_{{\dagger}}=\mathrm{L}_{+}(x)\mathrm{R}_{+}(y)(f^{+}_{\dagger}).

Proof.

This is a direct computation.
∎

For
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}
and
x,y\in[\U_{V}^{\prime}]
, we define the modified kernel

k_{f^{+}}^{T}(x,y)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})k_{f^{+},P}(\gamma x,\delta y),

and for
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
we put

k_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(x,y)=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{F}_{V}}\epsilon_{P}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\\
\delta\in P^{\prime}(F)\backslash\U_{V}^{\prime}(F)\end{subarray}}\widehat{\tau}_{P^{\prime}}(H_{P^{\prime}}(\delta y)-T_{P^{\prime}})k_{f^{+},\alpha,P}(\gamma x,\delta y).

Lemma 8.5
.

For
T
sufficiently positive, the integral

\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}\left\lvert k_{f^{+}}^{T}(x,y)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is absolutely convergent and defines a continuous seminorm on

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
. Put

j^{T}(f^{+}):=\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}k_{f^{+}}^{T}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Then
j^{T}
is the restriction of an exponential-polynomial function of
T

whose purely polynomial term is a constant that equals
J((f^{+})_{\ddagger})
.

Proof.

The absolute convergence follows from Lemma
8.3
and
Proposition
7.5
, the second follows from
Lemma
8.3
and Theorem
7.6
.
∎

8.2.
The coarse geometric expansion

The following theorem gives the geometric expansions of relative trace formulae on
unitary group.

Theorem 8.6
.

We have the following assertions.

(1)

For
T
sufficiently positive, the expression

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}\left\lvert k_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(x,y)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

is finite and defines a continuous seminorm on

\mathcal{S}(\U_{V}^{+}(\mathbb{A}))
.

(2)

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)
and
T
sufficiently positive, put

j^{T}_{\alpha}(f^{+})=\int_{[\U_{V}^{\prime}]\times[\U_{V}^{\prime}]}k_{f^{+},\alpha}^{T}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Then
j^{T}_{\alpha}
coincides with the restriction of an exponential-polynomial function of
T

whose purely polynomial term is a constant
j_{\alpha}(f^{+})
.

(3)

The linear form
f^{+}\mapsto j(f^{+})
is continuous and satisfies
the invariant property that

j(\mathrm{L}^{+}(x)\mathrm{R}^{+}(y)f^{+})=j(f^{+})

for all
x,y\in\U^{\prime}_{V}(\mathbb{A})
.

The proof is the same as Theorem
6.6
and make use of
Proposition
3.27
.

8.3.
Synthesis of the results: the coarse relative trace formula

We now summarize what we have done. For
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
we put

J_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+})=j_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+}^{\ddagger}),\quad J_{\alpha}(f_{+})=j_{\alpha}(f_{+}^{\ddagger}).

Then Theorem
8.6
tells us that if
T
is sufficiently
positive then
J_{\alpha}^{T}(f_{+})
is the restriction of a polynomial
exponential and the purely polynomial part is a constant that equals

J_{\alpha}(f_{+})
. By Lemma
8.4
, the distribution

J_{\alpha}
is bi-
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
-invariant, i.e. for
x,y\in\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})

we have

J_{\alpha}(\mathrm{L}_{+}(x)\mathrm{R}_{+}(y)f_{+})=J_{\alpha}(f_{+}).

We summarize the coarse relative trace formula on the unitary groups as the
following theorem.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
f_{+}\in\mathcal{S}(\U_{V,+}(\mathbb{A}))
be a test function. Then we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathfrak{X}(\U_{V})}J_{\chi}(f_{+})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(F)}J_{\alpha}(f_{+}).

Each summand on both sides are bi-
\U_{V}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
-invariant.

This is simply a combination of Theorem
7.6
,
Lemma
8.5
and
Theorem
8.6
.
